The Piano Trio No. 1 in B major, Op. 8, for piano, violin, and cello, was composed in 1854 when Brahms was twenty, during the months of Robert Schumann's suicide attempt and committal, and then radically revised by Brahms in 1889 at the age of fifty-six; it runs about thirty-five minutes in the revised version, opens with one of Brahms's most glorious soaring melodies (kept from the original), and fuses youthful Romantic ardor with late-style control, closing in a shadowed B minor; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the recording by the Beaux Arts Trio (Philips), chosen for its warmth, balance, and long-admired command of the Brahms trios, playing the standard 1889 revision.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Piano Trio No. 1 Op. 8 Beaux Arts Trio" or "Brahms B major trio Op. 8 1889 version" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 第一钢琴三重奏 作品8 美艺三重奏" 或 "勃拉姆斯 B大调钢琴三重奏 作品8" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* The Florestan Trio (Hyperion, 2000s), a clear, finely balanced modern reading much praised in the Brahms trios.
* The trio of Isaac Stern, Leonard Rose, and Eugene Istomin (Columbia/Sony, mid-century), a classic large-scale Romantic account.
* For the genuinely curious: a recording of the **original 1854 version** (several exist, including one by the Florestan Trio coupling both versions). Hearing the long, discursive original next to the tightened 1889 revision is one of the most instructive comparisons in all of Brahms, a direct lesson in what the mature composer's self-editing did.

On a first listen, hear the standard 1889 version whole. The opening melody (the first minutes) and the shadowed B-minor close are the two things to fix in your ear; the chapter's discussion of the two versions will mean more once you know how the revised trio sounds.

### 4.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Beaux Arts Trio recording of the **1889 revision** ; with another ensemble the clock times will differ. We will follow the first movement closely and sketch the rest, because the opening melody and the overall warmth-to-shadow arc are the keys.

**0:00. First movement: the great opening melody.** The piano begins, and the cello soon enters with the broad, soaring main theme, one of Brahms's supreme melodies, rising and opening out with warm, generous lyricism over a flowing piano part. Let it expand; it is long, unhurried, and openhearted. This is the music of the twenty-year-old, the melody both versions kept. Notice how the violin and cello sing together, and how the piano supports rather than dominates, the chamber-music ideal of equal partnership.

**About 3:00. The theme developed and the second idea.** The opening melody is taken up, varied, and worked; a second, somewhat more restless theme appears. Listen for how Brahms grows the music out of the opening material rather than simply moving to new tunes, the developing-variation habit at chamber scale. The first movement is broad and richly argued.

**About 11:00. Second movement (Scherzo).** A quick, shadowy, scurrying scherzo in B minor, light-footed and slightly uneasy, with a warmer, songful contrasting middle section (the trio) that blooms into broad lyricism before the shadowy music returns. Listen for the contrast between the nervous outer music and the warm central melody.

**About 18:00. Third movement (Adagio).** A deep, hymn-like slow movement, solemn and song-filled. It opens with a grave, chorale-like exchange between piano and strings, then unfolds a long, tender cello melody. This is the trio's still center, music of great inwardness and warmth. Some hear in it an elegiac quality fitting the work's origins in the Schumann tragedy, though Brahms himself left no such program; treat that as a listener's impression, not a documented fact.

**About 26:00. Fourth movement (Finale), the turn to shadow.** The finale is in B minor, not the bright B major of the opening, and it is restless, driven, and dark. After all the warmth of the earlier movements, the work closes in agitation and shadow, ending firmly in the minor. Listen for this: a trio that began in such radiant B major does not return to it at the end but darkens into B minor, an unusual and poignant trajectory. Whether this reflects the older Brahms shadowing his young self's sunlit work, or simply the demands of the music, the effect is of warmth remembered and lost, a large-scale backward glance.

The two things to carry away: the great soaring opening melody, and the shadowed minor-key close. Between them lies a work that is, uniquely, the meeting of two Brahmses across thirty-five years.

### 4.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### One work, two Brahmses: the 1854 and 1889 versions

The central musical fact about Op. 8 is that it exists in two versions, and the story of the revision is one of the most revealing things in all of Brahms. He wrote the trio in 1854, at twenty, in the thick of the Schumann crisis, and published it; it was a long, ardent, discursive, sometimes unwieldy young man's work, full of beautiful ideas not always tightly controlled, and it even quoted other composers' music (a Beethoven song, a Schubert song) in ways that carried private meaning. Then, in 1889, the fifty-six-year-old Brahms, a master at the height of his powers, was given the chance to revise it for a new edition, and he did not merely touch it up; he rewrote it, cutting great stretches, recomposing whole sections, removing the quotations, and tightening the structure ferociously, while keeping the best of the original melodies (above all the great opening theme).

The result is a fascinating hybrid, and a direct lesson in what Brahms's legendary self-criticism actually did. Set the two versions side by side, as a few recordings let you do, and you hear the difference between youthful inspiration and mature mastery in the same musical material: the 1889 version is shorter, denser, more sharply argued, less indulgent, every transition smoothed, every excess cut. Brahms said, with characteristic dry self-deprecation, that he had not given the work a new wig but had merely combed its hair a little; in truth he had remade it. That a composer would return to a thirty-five-year-old success and rebuild it from the inside tells you everything about the self-editor we met in Chapter 1, the man who published only what survived his own scrutiny, and who held even his own youthful self to that standard.

#### Chamber music as equal conversation

This is the first true chamber work we have anchored, so a word about the medium. Chamber music, music for a small group of instruments, one to a part, is conversation among equals: there is no soloist and no accompaniment, only a handful of voices in dialogue. The piano trio (piano, violin, cello) is one of the central chamber genres, and one of its challenges is balance, because the piano can easily overpower the two strings. Listen to how Brahms manages this: the piano shares and trades melodies with the violin and cello rather than dominating, and the three instruments pass ideas among themselves as in a good conversation. This ideal of equal partnership will govern all the chamber music in this volume, the Piano Quintet (Chapter 5), the violin sonatas (Chapter 14), the clarinet works (Chapter 15). Brahms was one of the supreme chamber-music composers, and the genre suited his temperament: intimate, serious, conversational, the opposite of the showy virtuoso display he disliked.

#### The warmth-to-shadow arc

Notice the trio's overall emotional trajectory: it opens in radiant B major and closes in dark B minor. This is the reverse of the minor-to-major, darkness-to-light arc you heard in the Op. 5 sonata and will hear in the First Symphony. Here the journey runs the other way, from light into shadow. It is unusual, and it gives the work its distinctive poignancy. One cannot prove that the older Brahms, revising in 1889, deliberately darkened the close to color his young self's work with the knowledge of all that had followed, the Schumann tragedy, the long unconsummated bond with Clara, a lifetime's losses. But the effect is unmistakable, and it makes Op. 8, the work that spans the widest gap of years in Brahms's catalogue, feel like a meditation on time itself: the sunlit melodies of youth, looked back on from age, and found to end in shadow.

### 4.4 The Stories

#### The three readings of the Clara question

Now to the question everyone asks, and the reason this chapter must be careful. What was the relationship between Brahms and Clara Schumann? There are, broadly, three readings, and this book presents all three and chooses none, in keeping with its conservative posture toward disputed biography.

**The nineteenth-century romantic reading: a tragic love affair.** In this telling, the young Brahms fell desperately in love with Clara; the two were held apart only by Robert's continued existence and then by honor and circumstance after his death; and Brahms, heartbroken, never married because no woman could replace Clara, carrying a lifelong unconsummated passion. This is the version that novels and films love, and it is built on real material, Brahms's passionate letters of 1854-1856 leave no doubt of the depth of his feeling. But it goes far beyond the evidence in asserting a settled, mutual, romantic-erotic love and a single tragic cause for Brahms's lifelong bachelorhood.

**The modern scholarly reading: a complex deep friendship and intellectual partnership.** Jan Swafford and most contemporary biographers describe something richer and less tidy than a thwarted romance. The young Brahms was certainly in the grip of overwhelming feeling for Clara in the crisis years; he himself, in later life, seems to have regarded those feelings with some embarrassment as a young man's passion. But the relationship that actually lasted, for forty years, was a profound friendship and artistic partnership: Clara was Brahms's most trusted musical adviser and one of the foremost performers of his music; he was her confidant, supporter, and lifelong intimate. Whether their bond was ever physically romantic is, in this reading, genuinely unknown and probably unknowable, and to reduce four decades of complex intimacy to "a love affair" is to falsify it. Brahms's failure to marry (he was once engaged, briefly, to another woman, and had other attachments) had many roots; pinning it solely on Clara is a novelist's simplification.

**The primary-source caution: what the documents actually allow.** Styra Avins, who edited the great modern English edition of Brahms's letters, and other source-scholars insist on a hard distinction between what the surviving documents support and what later writers have imagined. And here a crucial fact intrudes: Brahms and Clara, late in their lives, burned a great deal of their correspondence, by mutual agreement, precisely so that posterity could not read it. What survives is partial and was partly curated by the principals themselves. So the honest source-based position is one of pronounced uncertainty: we have ardent early letters from Brahms; we have evidence of a lifelong devoted friendship; and we have a deliberate gap, the burned letters, where the most intimate evidence would have been. Any confident claim about the romantic-erotic dimension is reading into that gap.

This book holds all three readings up and declines to choose. What is certain, and more interesting than the gossip, is the forty-year partnership itself: that the most important woman in Brahms's life was a great artist and his deepest musical confidante, that the relationship survived the unimaginable pressure of its origins, and that it ended only with Clara's death in 1896, an event so shattering to Brahms that the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ (Chapter 16) and arguably his own death within a year were bound up with it.

#### Robert Schumann's death and the long aftermath

Robert Schumann died at Endenich on July 29, 1856. Clara, kept from him through most of his confinement, reached his side only at the very end. Brahms was there too. With Robert's death, the strange, charged household configuration of the crisis years dissolved, and a question hung in the air: what now, for Clara and Brahms, both free? Whatever was said or felt in the months after, no marriage followed, and the two settled, over the following years, into the lifelong friendship described above. Brahms left Düsseldorf, resumed his own career, and began the long road that would lead, eventually, to Vienna. But he never lost Clara, and she never lost him; their letters and visits continued for forty years.

#### Recording history briefly

Op. 8 in its 1889 revision is a staple of the chamber repertoire, recorded by all the major piano trios; the Beaux Arts Trio (our primary recording) set a long-admired standard, and the Florestan Trio and the historic Stern-Rose-Istomin group offer fine alternatives. The single most rewarding listening project here is to find a recording that includes the **original 1854 version** as well, and to compare the two, the surest way to hear what Brahms's self-editing meant in practice. The fuller Brahms chamber-music lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects forward across the volume.

To the _German Requiem_ (Chapter 6). The deaths that haunt these years, Robert Schumann's in 1856 and, later, Brahms's mother's in 1865, are among the documented stimuli behind the Requiem, the great consolation-work Brahms built for the living who mourn. The bond formed in the Schumann tragedy fed, over a decade later, into the century's greatest sacred work.

To the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ (Chapter 16) and the final years (Chapter 19). The relationship begun in 1854 ends in 1896 with Clara's death, which moved Brahms to write the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , his last great vocal work, a meditation on death, composed in her shadow. The arc that opens in this chapter closes in those. Brahms reportedly suffered a collapse on the journey to or around Clara's funeral, and died himself less than a year later.

To the variation and "backward glance" instinct (Chapters 3, 20). The double-Brahms structure of Op. 8, young material reworked by the old master, is itself a kind of developing variation across a lifetime, the composer varying his own past self. It is the most extended "Rückblick" in his output, and a vivid instance of the transforming-return principle Chapter 20 collects.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter lays out three readings of the Brahms-Clara relationship, the nineteenth-century 'tragic love affair,' the modern 'complex deep friendship and partnership,' and the source-scholar's insistence that the burned letters make the romantic question genuinely unknowable, and it deliberately refuses to choose among them. Why might a book choose not to settle such a famous question? Is there value in saying 'we do not know,' or do you find it unsatisfying? What is gained, and what is lost, by refusing the tidy story?"_

* _"Brahms wrote this trio at twenty and rewrote it at fifty-six, keeping the great opening melody but recomposing much of the rest. If you could return to something you made at twenty and remake it with everything you have learned since, would you? What would you keep, and what would you cut? Listen for the places where the old Brahms's control meets the young Brahms's ardor, and consider what it means to revise your younger self."_

* _"The trio opens in radiant B major and closes in dark B minor, a warmth-to-shadow arc that is the reverse of the usual darkness-to-light shape. Whether or not Brahms intended it as a comment, the effect is of sunlit youth remembered from a darker age. As you listen, do you hear the ending as sad, or as something else, acceptance, depth, truth? How does ending in shadow change the meaning of all the warmth that came before?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Beaux Arts Trio recording of the 1889 version (or one of the alternatives in §4.1). This second listen focuses on the work's unique double nature and its emotional arc.

  1. **The great opening melody (0:00).** Fix in your ear the broad, soaring main theme of the first movement, the supreme tune both versions kept. Notice the equal partnership of cello, violin, and piano in singing it.

  2. **The hymn-like slow movement (about 18:00).** Listen to the grave, chorale-like opening of the Adagio and the long tender melody that follows. This inward center is the trio's emotional depth, and some hear an elegiac quality fitting the work's tragic origins.

  3. **The turn to B minor at the close (about 26:00).** Track how the work, having opened in radiant B major, closes in dark, agitated B minor. This warmth-to-shadow arc is the work's most poignant and unusual feature.

  4. **(If you can find both versions) the 1854 against the 1889.** Compare even a few minutes of the original long version with the tightened revision. This is the clearest possible lesson in what Brahms's self-editing did, and it brings the "two Brahmses in one work" idea fully to life.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the source): Robert Schumann's Piano Quintet in E-flat or his own Piano Trios, to hear the music of the man at the center of this chapter's tragedy, the composer who blessed Brahms and then collapsed. Schumann's chamber music is the immediate tradition Brahms's own grows out of, and hearing it deepens the sense of how much Brahms absorbed from the household he entered in 1853.

**Listen** (Clara's voice): a few of Clara Schumann's own compositions (her Piano Trio in G minor, or her songs), to remember that the woman at the heart of this story was herself a serious composer as well as one of the great pianists of the century. It is easy to reduce Clara to "the woman Brahms loved"; her own music restores her as the artist she was.

**Read** : Jan Swafford's _Johannes Brahms: A Biography_ (1997), the chapters on the Schumann crisis and its aftermath, for the most balanced and humane modern account of the relationship, scrupulous about what is known and what is not.

**Read** (the letters): Styra Avins's edition, _Johannes Brahms: Life and Letters_ (1997), which presents Brahms's own correspondence with linking commentary. Reading the actual letters of the crisis years, and learning where the gaps are (the burned correspondence), is the best cure for the confident myth-making that surrounds this story, and the best way to feel the human reality behind it.

---

## Chapter 5: The Vienna Move 1862 and the Pre-Symphony Years

After the Schumann catastrophe, Brahms spent the rest of the 1850s rebuilding a life and a career: working in Hamburg, taking a post directing a small court music establishment at Detmold, leading a women's choir, and slowly maturing as a composer. Then, in 1862, at twenty-nine, he made the move that would define the rest of his life: he went to Vienna, the city of Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven, and Schubert, the European capital of music, and over the next several years he settled there for good. This chapter covers the first Vienna decade, the 1860s into the early 1870s, the years in which Brahms produced his first run of unquestionable masterworks and yet still could not bring himself to write the one thing the world most expected of him: a symphony.

The chapter anchors this decade in two works that show the two sides of Brahms's craft in these years. The first is the Piano Quintet in F minor, Op. 34, his chamber-music breakthrough, a work of symphonic power and one of the peaks of the entire piano-quintet repertoire. The second is the _Variations and Fugue on a Theme by Handel_ , Op. 24, the great solo-piano variation set that shows Brahms turning, as he would again and again, to variation form as the laboratory where he worked out his deepest compositional thinking. Together they introduce the mature Brahms: the master of the large chamber forms, and the master of variation, the two crafts that would feed directly into the symphonies still to come.

### 5.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

There is a story Brahms told, late in life, that has become the emblem of these pre-symphony years. The conductor Hermann Levi recalled Brahms saying, of the prospect of writing a symphony: "I shall never compose a symphony! You have no idea how it feels to hear behind you the tramp of a giant like Beethoven." The image is unforgettable: the young master, charged by Schumann's "Neue Bahnen" with being the heir of Beethoven, hearing the dead giant's footsteps at his back, unable to step into the symphonic arena because the comparison would be merciless.

Whether or not Brahms used those exact words, the feeling they express is well attested across the decade, and it explains the curious shape of his early maturity. Here was a composer plainly capable of the largest forms, who had written a forty-minute symphonic piano sonata at twenty, and yet who spent the 1860s pouring his symphonic ambitions into chamber music, variations, and choral works, everything _except_ the symphony itself. The Piano Quintet, in particular, sounds like a symphony that has been compressed into five instruments; its weight and scale are orchestral. It is as if Brahms were rehearsing for the symphony in every genre but the one that frightened him. The dam would not break until 1876, with the First Symphony (Chapter 8). Until then, the giant's footsteps kept him in the chamber and at the keyboard, and we are the richer for it, because the works of this "avoidance" are some of his greatest.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

**For the Piano Quintet, Op. 34:**

  1. **Symphonic weight from five players.** From the unison opening, listen to how big the sound is, how the five instruments (string quartet plus piano) generate an almost orchestral mass. This is chamber music thinking like a symphony. Notice the F-minor seriousness, the same dark, ambitious key as the Op. 5 sonata.

  2. **The drive and the lyricism in alternation.** The quintet alternates powerful, driving music with passages of great lyrical warmth. Listen for the contrast, especially the songful slow movement against the fierce energy of the scherzo and finale.

**For the Handel Variations, Op. 24:**

  3. **A small theme growing into a world.** The piece begins with a short, plain tune by Handel, then builds twenty-five variations and a closing fugue on it, an entire half-hour world grown from a few bars. Listen to how each variation transforms the theme into something new, fast or slow, gentle or thunderous, while the theme's bones remain underneath. This is developing variation made fully explicit, and it ends with a grand fugue, Brahms saluting his beloved Baroque masters.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on two works: the **Piano Quintet in F minor, Op. 34 (1864-1865)** , for piano and string quartet, a symphonically scaled, four-movement chamber masterwork that reached its final form only after Brahms had first written the material as a string quintet and then as a two-piano sonata; and the **Variations and Fugue on a Theme by Handel, Op. 24 (1861)** , for solo piano, twenty-five variations and a closing fugue on a theme from a Handel keyboard suite, one of the supreme variation sets of the nineteenth century; the **Primary Recordings** are, for the quintet, the recording by the pianist Maurizio Pollini with the Quartetto Italiano (Deutsche Grammophon), and for the Handel Variations, the recording by Julius Katchen (Decca) or Stephen Kovacevich (Philips), chosen for power and clarity.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Piano Quintet Op. 34 Pollini Quartetto Italiano"; "Brahms Handel Variations Op. 24 Katchen" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 钢琴五重奏 作品34 波利尼"; "勃拉姆斯 韩德尔主题变奏曲 作品24" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Piano Quintet: Sviatoslav Richter with the Borodin Quartet (a powerful, famous live account); the Leon Fleisher / Juilliard Quartet recording; the Clifford Curzon / Amadeus Quartet reading (a long-admired classic).
* Handel Variations: Stephen Kovacevich (Philips), Wilhelm Backhaus (historic), and Murray Perahia have all recorded distinguished accounts.

These are two substantial works; you may want to take them on separate listens. If you have time for only one on a first pass, the Piano Quintet is the larger statement, but the Handel Variations is the clearer lesson in variation form, which matters for everything ahead.

### 5.2 The Listening Window

#### The Piano Quintet in F minor, Op. 34

Timestamps refer to the Pollini / Quartetto Italiano recording; with another group they will shift.

**0:00. First movement: the unison opening.** The quintet begins with all the instruments stating the main theme in stark unison, then erupting into driving, powerful music. The key is F minor, dark and serious. Listen to the symphonic weight, the way five instruments fill the space like an orchestra, and the dramatic contrast between the terse opening idea and the surge that follows.

**About 3:00. The lyrical second theme.** A warmer, more songful idea provides contrast. The first movement, in sonata form, sets its fierce first material against this lyricism and develops both. Listen for Brahms growing the music from the opening cell rather than simply moving on, the developing-variation habit at work.

**About 14:00. Second movement (Andante).** A tender, songful slow movement, gentle and consoling after the storm of the first. Listen for the long-breathed melody and the warm, cushioned texture, the lyrical Brahms.

**About 22:00. Third movement (Scherzo).** One of Brahms's most thrilling scherzos: a driving, syncopated, almost demonic movement that builds tremendous energy, with a broad, triumphant chorale-like climax. Listen for the rhythmic excitement, the off-beat accents, and the contrast with the warm trio section.

**About 30:00. Fourth movement (Finale).** The finale opens with a slow, mysterious, brooding introduction before launching into a driving main movement, and it ends in dark, headlong energy. Listen across the whole work for its F-minor seriousness and its symphonic ambition; this is a chamber work that wants to be a symphony.

#### The Handel Variations, Op. 24

**0:00. The theme.** A short, plain, slightly old-fashioned tune by Handel, stated simply. Fix it in your ear, its shape and its bass line, because everything that follows is grown from it.

**Through the variations (roughly 0:30 onward).** Twenty-five variations follow, each transforming the theme. Listen for the variety: some are fast and brilliant, some slow and tender, some gentle, some thunderous, some imitate a music box, a hunting horn, a Hungarian dance. In each, try to keep the theme's outline in your ear underneath the transformation. This is the pleasure of variation form, hearing the same bones wearing endlessly different flesh.

**The closing fugue (the final several minutes).** The set ends not with another variation but with a large, grand fugue built on a subject derived from the theme, a contrapuntal showpiece that salutes the Baroque masters (Bach and Handel) Brahms revered. Listen for the fugue's gathering grandeur and its triumphant close. Ending a variation set with a fugue is a deliberate homage to tradition, and a demonstration that Brahms could handle the most learned form with total mastery.

### 5.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The long road of the Piano Quintet

The Piano Quintet's final form was hard-won, and the story illustrates Brahms's self-critical method. He first wrote the material, around 1862, as a string quintet (with two cellos, like Schubert's; the Schubert volume). Dissatisfied, he rewrote it as a sonata for two pianos (a version that still exists and is occasionally played). Still not satisfied, he combined the two conceptions, strings and piano together, into the Piano Quintet we know, completed in 1864-65. The journey, from all-strings to all-pianos to the fusion of both, tells you that Brahms heard in this material a weight that neither strings alone nor pianos alone could carry; it needed the full color and mass of strings _and_ piano together. The result is one of the great piano quintets, a work whose symphonic ambition you can hear in every bar, and a clear sign of where Brahms was heading: toward the orchestra, by way of every other large form first.

#### Variation form as Brahms's laboratory

The Handel Variations introduces the form that mattered most to Brahms across his whole life, and that this book treats as central (Chapters 7, 11, 18, 20). Theme and variations is an old form: state a theme, then present a series of transformations of it, each keeping some essential feature (the melody, the harmony, the bass line, the structure) while changing others. For most composers it was a pleasant, sometimes decorative form. For Brahms it was the deepest expression of his compositional nature, because his nature was to transform rather than repeat, to grow music from a seed. In variation form that instinct becomes the explicit subject of the piece: the music is _about_ the transformation of a theme. The Handel Variations shows the form at its grandest, twenty-five transformations of Handel's little tune, ranging across every mood and technique, capped by a fugue. When you reach the _Haydn Variations_ (Chapter 7), the Fourth Symphony's passacaglia (Chapter 11), and the _Paganini Variations_ (Chapter 18), you will be hearing the same lifelong preoccupation. The Handel set is where, in this volume, you first hear Brahms's central form laid bare.

#### The fugue, and Brahms's love of the old masters

Ending the Handel Variations with a fugue is significant. The fugue, the strictest contrapuntal form, where a single theme is woven through multiple independent voices in elaborate imitation, was the supreme achievement of the Baroque, of Bach and Handel, and by Brahms's day it was widely regarded as old-fashioned, academic, a thing of the past. Brahms loved it. He studied the old masters obsessively, collected and edited early music, and folded counterpoint and fugue into his own work all his life, the Requiem (Chapter 6) has great fugues, the Fourth Symphony's finale is a passacaglia (a Baroque form), his very last work would be a set of Bach-style chorale preludes (Chapter 19). This deep engagement with the musical past is part of what made the nineteenth century call Brahms a "conservative." But notice what he does with the old forms: he fills them with the most modern, developing-variation thinking. The Wagner camp's backhanded compliment, which you will meet in Chapter 18, was that Brahms showed "what can still be done in the old forms by a man who knows what to do with them." The Handel fugue is an early, brilliant instance: tradition mastered, and made new.

### 5.4 The Stories

#### Brahms comes to Vienna

In 1862 Brahms made his first extended visit to Vienna, and the city took hold of him. Vienna was the place where the German classical tradition lived, where Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven, and Schubert had worked, where their music was in the air and their graves were tended. For a composer who saw himself as the custodian of that tradition, charged by Schumann to carry it forward, Vienna was the natural home, and over the following years Brahms settled there permanently, taking choral- and orchestral-conducting posts (including, later, the directorship of the Vienna Singakademie and then of the prestigious Gesellschaft der Musikfreunde concerts). He had hoped for a major conducting post in his native Hamburg, and was wounded when the city passed him over; Vienna's embrace was partly a consolation for Hamburg's rejection, and Brahms never fully forgave his hometown.

Vienna gave Brahms an audience, a circle, and a tradition to belong to. It also placed him, geographically and symbolically, at the center of the musical politics of the age, the "war of the Romantics" between the Wagner-Liszt "Music of the Future" party and the more classical, absolute-music camp that gathered around Brahms and the critic Eduard Hanslick. That war, which Chapter 21 takes up in full, was fought largely in Vienna, and Brahms's settling there made him, willingly or not, the figurehead of one side.

#### The deaths behind the decade

These were also years of loss that fed directly into Brahms's greatest work of the period. His mother, Christiane, died in early 1865, a profound blow; Brahms had been deeply attached to her, and her death is one of the documented emotional springs of the _German Requiem_ , which he completed in the years immediately following (Chapter 6). The earlier death of Robert Schumann in 1856 is the other commonly cited stimulus. The Requiem, the supreme work of this Vienna decade, grew out of the soil of these bereavements, the consolation-work of a man who had lost the mentor who blessed him and the mother who raised him.

#### Recording history briefly

Both anchor works are central repertoire. The Piano Quintet has a rich discography, the Pollini / Quartetto Italiano, the Richter / Borodin, the Curzon / Amadeus all distinguished; comparing a fierce, driven reading with a warmer, more lyrical one shows how much interpretive room the work allows. The Handel Variations belongs to the great Brahms pianists, Katchen, Kovacevich, Backhaus, Perahia, and is a favorite recital and recording piece precisely because its succession of contrasted variations lets a pianist show every color. The fuller lineages are gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects forward across the volume.

To the symphonies (Chapters 8-11). The symphonic ambition you hear straining inside the Piano Quintet is the ambition that, after years of fear, finally produced the First Symphony in 1876. The quintet is, in a sense, a symphony Brahms could let himself write while the real symphony still terrified him.

To the variation chapters (Chapters 7, 11, 18, 20). The Handel Variations is the first full statement, in this volume, of the variation form that organizes so much of Brahms's art. The _Haydn Variations_ (Chapter 7) carries the form into the orchestra; the Fourth Symphony's passacaglia (Chapter 11) makes it the climax of a symphony; the _Paganini Variations_ (Chapter 18) turns it to virtuoso display; and Chapter 20 collects the whole lineage.

To the _German Requiem_ (Chapter 6), the next chapter and the supreme work of this decade, whose great fugues grow from the same contrapuntal mastery you hear in the Handel fugue, and whose consolation grows from the same losses that shadowed these Vienna years.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter argues that Brahms spent the 1860s pouring his symphonic ambitions into chamber music and variations precisely because the 'tramp of the giant' Beethoven made him afraid to write an actual symphony. Listen to the symphonic weight of the Piano Quintet and ask: can you hear a symphony trying to escape from five instruments? What might it cost a great talent to be so afraid of comparison that he avoids his largest ambition for over a decade?"_

* _"The Handel Variations grows an entire half-hour world from a short, plain Handel tune, twenty-five transformations and a fugue. As you listen, try to keep the original theme in your ear underneath each variation. What is the particular pleasure of variation form, of hearing the same bones wear endlessly different flesh? Why might this form have meant more to Brahms than to almost any other composer?"_

* _"Brahms ended his variation set with a fugue, the strictest, most 'old-fashioned' Baroque form, and filled the old forms with the most forward-looking thinking. The nineteenth century called this 'conservative.' Listen to the closing fugue and ask whether reverence for the past and genuine originality have to be opposites. Can looking backward and looking forward be the same gesture?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

  1. **The Piano Quintet's unison opening (0:00).** Fix in your ear the stark unison statement of the main theme and the eruption that follows. Notice the symphonic weight, five instruments sounding like an orchestra, and the dark F-minor seriousness.

  2. **The Piano Quintet's scherzo (about 22:00).** Listen to the driving, syncopated, almost demonic energy and the broad chorale-like climax. This is one of Brahms's most exciting movements and a clear case of his rhythmic power.

  3. **The Handel theme and a few variations (0:00 onward).** Fix the plain Handel tune in your ear, then follow several variations, hearing how each transforms it while keeping its bones. Pick out two or three of the most strongly characterized (a gentle one, a thunderous one, a music-box one) and hear the range.

  4. **The Handel fugue (the final minutes).** Listen to the closing fugue gather grandeur and drive to its triumphant end. Hear it as Brahms's homage to Bach and Handel, and as proof that he could master the most learned form completely.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the two-piano version): if you are curious about the Piano Quintet's long compositional journey, find the Sonata for Two Pianos, Op. 34b, the intermediate version Brahms made before the quintet. Hearing the same music for two pianos, without the strings, shows you what the final scoring added and why Brahms was right not to stop at either the all-string or the all-piano version.

**Listen** (the variation lineage): Beethoven's _Diabelli Variations_ , the towering variation set of the generation before Brahms, thirty-three transformations of a trivial little waltz. Brahms knew and revered it, and hearing it alongside the Handel Variations shows the great tradition of the monumental variation set that Brahms was consciously joining.

**Read** : Malcolm MacDonald's _Brahms_ (1990), the chapters on the Vienna move and the 1860s, for a clear, music-focused account of how Brahms established himself in his adopted city and worked toward the first masterworks.

**Read** (on variation): any good general account of variation form (the relevant articles in a music dictionary, or the discussion in the Brahms studies of Walter Frisch and Michael Musgrave) will deepen your sense of why this form mattered so much to Brahms. The Handel set is the ideal piece to have in your ear while you read.
