## Chapter 6: Ein deutsches Requiem, Op. 45 (1865-1868)

In the same years that Brahms wrote the world's most famous lullaby, he also built one of the supreme sacred works of the nineteenth century. _Ein deutsches Requiem_ , "A German Requiem," is the great public masterpiece of the Vienna decade, the work that made Brahms internationally famous, and a piece unlike any other Requiem before it. It is not a setting of the Latin Mass for the Dead, with its prayers for the souls of the departed and its terrifying _Dies irae_ visions of judgment. It is something Brahms made himself, a sequence of texts he chose personally from the German Luther Bible, and its subject is not the dead at all but the living who mourn them. Its very first words are "Blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted." It is a Requiem for the bereaved, a work of consolation, and that single reorientation, from fear of judgment to comfort for the grieving, is what makes it one of the most humane large works in all of music.

This is an anchor-heavy chapter, the first of several in the book, and it deserves the space. The German Requiem is about seventy minutes long, in seven movements, and it is the largest single thing we have met so far. We will not walk through all seventy minutes in equal detail; instead we will fix the work's shape, then go close on four movements that show what the Requiem is and does: the great funeral march and its turn to radiance (Movement 2), the serene "How lovely are thy dwelling places" (Movement 4), the consoling soprano solo added last (Movement 5), and the mighty meditation on mortality and its triumphant fugue (Movement 6). If you come away hearing those four, and the consolation that binds them, you will have the Requiem.

### 6.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Bremen Cathedral, Good Friday, April 10, 1868. Brahms, not yet thirty-five, stands before a large chorus and orchestra to conduct the premiere of his Requiem, in a version of six movements (the seventh, the soprano solo, would be added later). The performance is a triumph, the work that finally lifts Brahms from "promising young man" to "major composer" in the eyes of all Germany. But there is a famous, telling moment around this premiere. A well-meaning cathedral official, troubled that this "Requiem" never once mentions the redeeming death of Christ, never names the doctrine that a Christian funeral work was expected to proclaim, raised the concern; and an aria from Handel's _Messiah_ , "I know that my Redeemer liveth," was inserted into the program to supply the missing Christian message.

The anecdote is revealing because the official was right about the facts and wrong about the work. Brahms's Requiem genuinely does avoid the specific Christian doctrine of redemption through Christ. Brahms, who knew the Lutheran Bible intimately but was not conventionally devout, chose his texts to speak to _all_ mourners, of any faith or none, about grief and comfort, about the brevity of life and the peace of the dead and the consolation of those left behind. When someone suggested he add an explicitly Christian movement, Brahms is reported to have said he would gladly have called it "A Human Requiem" rather than "A German Requiem." Whether or not he used those exact words, they capture the work exactly: it is a Requiem for human beings facing loss, and its comfort is offered to everyone. That universality is a large part of why it has never stopped being performed, sung at funerals and memorials of every kind for over a century and a half.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **Consolation, not terror.** Listen, across the whole work, for its emotional register: this is a Requiem that comforts rather than frightens. There is no movement of judgment and damnation. Even the movements that face death most directly (the funeral march of Movement 2, the meditation of Movement 6) resolve toward peace, light, and reassurance. Compare it in your mind to the fire-and-judgment of the traditional Latin Requiem, and hear what Brahms left out and what he put in its place.

  2. **The great fugues.** Brahms's lifelong study of Bach and the old masters comes to full flower here. Twice the work builds to enormous choral fugues, in Movement 3 ("the righteous souls are in the hand of God") and at the climax of Movement 6 ("Worthy art thou"). Listen for these towering contrapuntal structures, where the chorus divides into independent interweaving lines that build to overwhelming grandeur. They are the contrapuntal mastery of Chapter 5's Handel fugue, raised to monumental scale.

  3. **The orchestral color and the choral writing.** Notice the warmth and depth of the sound. Brahms famously opens the whole work without violins, keeping the texture low and dark (violas, cellos, basses), so that the first sound is grave and consoling rather than bright. Listen for that dark warmth at the opening, and for the way the chorus is treated, sometimes hushed and homophonic (all voices moving together), sometimes split into the intricate independence of the fugues.

#### One-sentence background

_Ein deutsches Requiem_ (A German Requiem), Op. 45, is a seventy-minute sacred work for soprano and baritone soloists, chorus, and orchestra, composed mainly between 1865 and 1868 on texts Brahms selected himself from the Luther Bible (not the Latin Mass for the Dead), oriented toward the consolation of the living mourner rather than prayers for the dead or visions of judgment; its seven movements move from "Blessed are they that mourn" to a final benediction on the dead, with two great choral fugues and a symmetrical arch of keys, and it was the work that made Brahms internationally famous after its Bremen Cathedral premiere on Good Friday 1868; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the classic account by Otto Klemperer conducting the Philharmonia Orchestra and Chorus, with Elisabeth Schwarzkopf and Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau as soloists (EMI, 1961), long regarded as the reference recording.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms German Requiem Klemperer Schwarzkopf Fischer-Dieskau" or "Brahms Ein deutsches Requiem Op. 45" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 德意志安魂曲 克伦佩勒 施瓦茨科普夫" 或 "勃拉姆斯 德意志安魂曲 作品45" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin or Vienna forces (Deutsche Grammophon/EMI, various), a warm, large-scale, luminous account.
* John Eliot Gardiner with the Monteverdi Choir and the Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique (period-instrument, Philips, 1990s), leaner, faster, with the textures clarified and the choral lines sharply etched, a very different and revealing approach.
* Simon Rattle with the Berlin Philharmonic (EMI/Warner), a fine modern reading.

This is a seventy-minute work; give yourself an uninterrupted hour for the first listen if you can, and follow a text-and-translation (widely available online) so you know what is being sung. The words are the key to this Requiem; Brahms chose every one of them, and the consolation lives in the meaning.

### 6.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to the Klemperer recording; with another conductor (especially the much faster Gardiner) they will differ considerably. We fix the whole shape briefly, then go close on Movements 2, 4, 5, and 6.

**The whole shape (orientation).** Seven movements, arranged as a symmetrical arch. Movement 1 ("Blessed are they that mourn") and Movement 7 ("Blessed are the dead") are the calm, blessing outer pillars, related in music and mood. Movements 2 and 6 are the great dark-to-light movements, the funeral march and the mortality-meditation, each turning from the contemplation of death to triumph. Movements 3 and 5 are the soloist movements (baritone in 3, soprano in 5). Movement 4 ("How lovely are thy dwelling places") is the serene keystone at the center of the arch. Carry that shape: blessing, march-to-triumph, baritone, serene center, soprano, meditation-to-triumph, blessing.

**Movement 2: "Denn alles Fleisch, es ist wie Gras" (For all flesh is as grass).**

_0:00._ The movement opens with one of the most extraordinary passages Brahms wrote: a slow, heavy, tread-like march, the timpani pulsing, the chorus intoning "For all flesh is as grass" in a dark, grinding minor, the music of a funeral procession, of mortality itself walking. Listen to the weight and the menace, the relentless tread. This is as close as the consoling Requiem comes to the terror of death.

_About 3:00._ A gentler middle section: "So be patient" (the words urge patience until the coming of the Lord, like a farmer waiting for the harvest). The music lightens and consoles before the dark march returns.

_About 6:00. The great turn._ The funeral march comes back, and then, on the words "But the word of the Lord endureth for ever" and "the ransomed of the Lord shall return," the music breaks into radiance: a surging, exultant fast section, the chorus blazing into major, "everlasting joy upon their heads; they shall obtain joy and gladness, and sorrow and sighing shall flee away." Listen for this transformation, the funeral tread giving way to blazing joy. The movement that began with mortality marching ends in everlasting gladness. This dark-to-light turn is the emotional core of the whole Requiem.

**Movement 4: "Wie lieblich sind deine Wohnungen" (How lovely are thy dwelling places).**

_0:00._ After the baritone movement (3), the Requiem reaches its serene center. This is the gentlest, most beloved movement, a flowing, songful setting of "How lovely are thy dwelling places, O Lord of hosts." Listen to the gently rocking melody, the warmth, the sense of arrival and peace. If the Requiem has a single tune people leave humming, it is this. After the weight of Movement 2, this is repose, the green pastures at the heart of the work.

**Movement 5: "Ihr habt nun Traurigkeit" (And ye now therefore have sorrow).**

_0:00._ This was the last movement composed, added after the Bremen premiere, and it introduces the soprano soloist. Over a hushed orchestra and chorus, the soprano sings "And ye now therefore have sorrow, but I will see you again, and your heart shall rejoice." Then comes the line that gives the movement its deepest meaning: "As one whom his mother comforteth, so will I comfort you." Listen to the tenderness of the soprano line, floating high and consoling, with the chorus murmuring beneath. Many hear in this movement, with its image of a mother's comfort, Brahms's tribute to his own mother, whose death in 1865 was one of the work's springs. It is the most personal and the most tender movement, comfort itself given a human voice.

**Movement 6: "Denn wir haben hie keine bleibende Statt" (For here we have no continuing city).**

_0:00._ The longest and most dramatic movement begins quietly, the baritone and chorus contemplating that we have no lasting home on earth.

_About 2:00. "Behold, I show you a mystery."_ The baritone announces the mystery: "we shall not all sleep, but we shall all be changed, in a moment, in the twinkling of an eye, at the last trumpet." The music gathers tension and breaks into a tremendous passage, the trumpet, the raising of the dead, "Death is swallowed up in victory." Listen to the surge of power. "O death, where is thy sting? O grave, where is thy victory?", the words of triumph over death, set to overwhelming music.

_About 6:00. The great fugue._ The movement climaxes in an enormous choral fugue: "Worthy art thou to receive glory and honour and power." Listen to the chorus split into independent interweaving lines, building a colossal contrapuntal structure that drives to a blazing close. This is the grandest of the Requiem's fugues, Brahms's Bach-study turned into monumental affirmation, the second great dark-to-light arch (after Movement 2) and the work's structural and emotional summit.

After Movement 6's triumph, Movement 7 ("Blessed are the dead which die in the Lord") returns to the calm, blessing music of the opening, closing the arch in peace. The Requiem ends as it began, not in triumph but in quiet benediction, on the word "selig," blessed, the same word it started with.

### 6.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### A Requiem of consolation: the texts Brahms chose

The single most important fact about the German Requiem is the one the Bremen official noticed: Brahms made his own text. The traditional Latin Requiem is a fixed liturgical sequence, dominated by prayers for the dead and the fearful _Dies irae_ , the Day of Wrath, with its trembling, its judgment, its flames. Composers from Mozart to Verdi set that text and made tremendous drama of its terror. Brahms refused it. He went instead to the Luther Bible and assembled, himself, a sequence of passages, from the Beatitudes, the Psalms, Isaiah, the Gospels, the Epistles, Revelation, chosen to speak about grief and its comfort, the brevity of life, the peace of the dead, and above all the consolation of those who mourn. The result faces death honestly, the funeral march of Movement 2, the "all flesh is as grass," the "no continuing city", but always turns toward comfort, light, and peace, never toward fear.

This is why the work feels so humane and why it has been embraced far beyond any single faith. Brahms's reported willingness to call it "A Human Requiem" is the key. It is a work about the universal human experience of loss and the universal need for comfort, and it offers that comfort to anyone, the bereaved of any belief. In a century of grand, terrifying, operatic Requiems, Brahms wrote the one that holds the mourner's hand.

#### The symmetrical arch

Listen for the work's architecture, which is a deliberate symmetrical arch. The outer movements (1 and 7) are calm blessings, musically and verbally linked (both built around the word "selig," blessed). Moving inward, Movements 2 and 6 are the two great dark-to-light dramas (the funeral march and the mortality-meditation, each ending in triumph). Movements 3 and 5 are the soloist movements. And Movement 4, the serene "How lovely are thy dwelling places," is the keystone at the very center. This arch shape, with its mirror symmetry around a central pillar, is itself a kind of large-scale developing variation and balancing, the structural thinking of a composer who built everything from carefully weighed relationships. You do not need to track it consciously while listening, but knowing it is there helps you feel why the work has such a satisfying sense of wholeness and return: it ends where it began, having made a complete journey out to the triumph of Movement 6 and back to the peace of the opening.

#### The fugues and the Bach inheritance

The two great fugues (Movement 3's "the souls of the righteous are in the hand of God" and Movement 6's "Worthy art thou") are where Brahms's lifelong devotion to Bach and the Baroque masters becomes monumental public art. A fugue, as Chapter 5 introduced, weaves a single subject through multiple independent voices in elaborate imitation; it is the supreme test of contrapuntal craft and the glory of the Baroque. Brahms, the great student of old music, brings the form into the Romantic concert hall and uses it not as dry display but as overwhelming emotional climax: the fugue on "Worthy art thou" is the moment the Requiem's affirmation reaches its height. Notice, too, a famous detail of the Movement 3 fugue: Brahms sets the entire huge fugue over a sustained pedal point, a single low D held in the bass throughout, so that all the contrapuntal turmoil unfolds over an unshakeable foundation, the firm ground of "the hand of God" under the souls of the righteous. The technical device _is_ the meaning. This fusion of the most learned craft with the deepest expression is the essence of Brahms, and it points directly to the contrapuntal climaxes of the Fourth Symphony (Chapter 11) and the chorale preludes of his very last work (Chapter 19).

#### The orchestra without violins

A last detail of color. Brahms opens the entire Requiem without violins; the first movement keeps the violins silent, building its sound from the darker, warmer middle and low strings (violas, cellos, basses) plus winds and harp. The effect is of a sound with no bright top, grave, warm, consoling, earthbound, exactly right for a movement that begins "Blessed are they that mourn." It is a small instance of Brahms's mastery of orchestral color in the service of meaning: he withholds the brightest instruments to keep the music close to the ground, close to grief, before the work's long climb toward light. Listen for the moment, later in the work, when the violins finally do enter, and feel how much their brightness then means.

### 6.4 The Stories

#### The deaths behind the work, and the long gestation

The German Requiem grew slowly, over more than a decade, and out of real loss. Brahms had begun sketching some of its material in the late 1850s; some of the music of the second movement may go back to the very years of Robert Schumann's illness and death (1854-1856), the catastrophe of Chapter 4. The decisive push came with the death of Brahms's mother, Christiane, in February 1865, a blow that seems to have galvanized the work toward completion. The tender soprano movement (5), with its "as one whom his mother comforteth," added after the premiere, is widely felt to be his memorial to her. So the Requiem stands at the meeting point of the two great losses of Brahms's early life, the mentor who blessed him and the mother who raised him, transformed into a work of comfort offered to all mourners. That a private grief became a universal consolation is the deepest thing about the piece.

#### The premiere and the rise to fame

The six-movement version premiered at Bremen Cathedral on Good Friday, April 10, 1868, with Brahms conducting, and it was a decisive triumph, the performance that established him, at last, as a major composer in the eyes of the German public, fifteen years after Schumann's "Neue Bahnen" had promised it. Brahms then added the soprano movement (5), and the complete seven-movement version was first performed in Leipzig at the Gewandhaus on February 18, 1869. From there the work spread rapidly across Europe and to England and America, becoming one of the most performed large choral works in the repertoire, a position it has never lost. It was, in a real sense, the making of Brahms's international reputation, and the foundation on which the symphonies, when they finally came, would stand.

#### Recording history briefly

The German Requiem has one of the great discographies in choral music. Otto Klemperer's 1961 Philharmonia recording, our primary, with Schwarzkopf and Fischer-Dieskau, is for many listeners the reference, grave, monumental, deeply weighted. Karajan's various accounts offer warmth and luminous beauty. The most illuminating modern alternative is John Eliot Gardiner's period-instrument recording, much faster and leaner, which clarifies the choral counterpoint and strips away Romantic weight to reveal the work's architecture; hearing Klemperer's monumental gravity against Gardiner's lithe clarity is one of the most instructive comparisons in the Brahms discography. The fuller German Requiem lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The German Requiem connects across the volume.

To the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2), composed in the same years. The lullaby and the Requiem are the two faces of Brahms's vocal art in this period, and they share one deep impulse: consolation. The lullaby comforts a child; the Requiem comforts the bereaved. The warmth you felt in the cradle-song is the same warmth that built the century's great work of mourning.

To the Fourth Symphony (Chapter 11) and the chorale preludes (Chapter 19). The great fugues and the pedal-point counterpoint of the Requiem are the same contrapuntal mastery that produces the passacaglia finale of the Fourth Symphony and the Bach-style chorale preludes of Brahms's very last work. The Requiem is where you first hear, on a monumental scale, the old-master counterpoint that runs to the end of his life.

To the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ (Chapter 16). At the end of his life, on the death of Clara Schumann, Brahms returned to Biblical texts about death and wrote the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , "Four Serious Songs," a kind of intimate, private counterpart to the great public Requiem of his maturity. The young man's consolation for all mourners and the old man's reckoning with his own mortality frame his career.

To the Schumann-Clara story (Chapter 4). The Requiem grew partly from the death of Robert Schumann, the mentor at the center of that chapter, and from Brahms's own mother's death. The bonds and losses of Chapter 4 feed directly into this work; the Requiem is, among other things, the music of Brahms's grief grown universal.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Brahms refused the traditional Latin Requiem, with its prayers for the dead and its terrifying visions of judgment, and made his own text from the Bible, oriented entirely toward comforting the living who mourn, its first words are 'Blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted.' He reportedly said he would gladly have called it 'A Human Requiem.' Listen to the work's consoling register and ask: what does it change, to write a Requiem about the grief of the living rather than the fate of the dead? Why might that reorientation be part of why the work has never stopped being sung at funerals of every faith?"_

* _"The second movement opens with a heavy, tread-like funeral march, 'all flesh is as grass,' and then, on the words about everlasting joy, breaks into blazing radiance. Listen to that dark-to-light turn (around 6:00 in the Klemperer recording). How does Brahms make the transformation feel earned rather than easy? Why might a work of consolation need to face the funeral march first, before it can turn to joy?"_

* _"Brahms set the great fugue of the third movement over a single low note held unbroken in the bass, all the contrapuntal turmoil unfolding over one unshakeable foundation, under the words 'the souls of the righteous are in the hand of God.' The technical device and the meaning are the same thing. As you listen across this volume, watch for other places where Brahms's craft and his meaning fuse, where how the music is built is what it is saying."_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Klemperer recording (or one of the alternatives in §6.1), and follow a text-and-translation. This second listen focuses on the four key movements and the consolation that binds them.

  1. **The funeral march and its turn (Movement 2).** Listen to the heavy, tread-like opening march, "all flesh is as grass," and then to the great turn into blazing radiance (around 6:00) on the words about everlasting joy. This dark-to-light arc is the work's emotional core.

  2. **The serene center (Movement 4).** Listen to "How lovely are thy dwelling places," the gentle, songful keystone of the whole arch. After the weight of Movement 2, hear it as repose, the still center.

  3. **The mother's comfort (Movement 5).** Listen to the soprano's tender "As one whom his mother comforteth, so will I comfort you," widely felt to be Brahms's tribute to his own mother. Hear comfort itself given a human voice.

  4. **The great fugue (Movement 6).** Listen to the climactic choral fugue, "Worthy art thou," the chorus splitting into interweaving lines that build to a blazing close. This is the Requiem's summit and the grandest flowering of Brahms's Bach-study.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the contrast): a few minutes of the Verdi Requiem or the Mozart Requiem, both settings of the traditional Latin text, especially the _Dies irae_ , the Day of Wrath. Hearing the terror and judgment of the conventional Requiem makes vivid, by contrast, exactly what Brahms chose _not_ to do, and how radical his turn toward consolation was.

**Listen** (the Bach root): a chorus or two from a Bach cantata or the _Mass in B minor_ (the Bach volume), to hear the source of Brahms's great fugal writing. The German Requiem's fugues are Brahms's homage to and continuation of the Bach choral tradition; hearing Bach first makes the kinship audible.

**Read** : Michael Musgrave's writing on the German Requiem (he has written a short dedicated study as well as the relevant chapters in _The Music of Brahms_ , 1985), for a clear account of the texts, the structure, and the work's place in Brahms's development.

**Read** (the text): print out the full text-and-translation of the Requiem and read Brahms's chosen passages straight through as a sequence, before or after listening. Reading the words Brahms selected, and seeing how they move from "blessed are they that mourn" to "blessed are the dead," reveals the work's design and its consoling purpose more clearly than any analysis, because the design _is_ the choice of words.

---

## Chapter 7: Variations on a Theme by Haydn, Op. 56a (1873) and the Road to the Symphonies

By the early 1870s Brahms had everything a symphony requires except the nerve to write one. He had proved his command of large structures in the Piano Quintet, his mastery of variation and counterpoint in the Handel set and the Requiem fugues, his ability to move a vast public with the German Requiem. The one thing he had never done was write for full orchestra on his own terms, a purely orchestral work standing free of voices, soloists, or chamber partners. The _Variations on a Theme by Haydn_ , Op. 56a, composed in 1873, is the bridge: Brahms's first major work for orchestra alone, his proof to himself and to the world that he could command the instrument, and, not coincidentally, a variation set, the form in which he was most at home. Three years later the First Symphony would finally arrive. The Haydn Variations is the deep breath before that plunge.

This chapter anchors the whole work, about eighteen minutes of music, theme, eight variations, and a grand passacaglia finale. It is the ideal piece for fixing the variation principle firmly in your ear before the symphonies, because here the form is laid out in the clear: a theme you can easily hold in memory, eight sharply characterized transformations of it, and a finale built over a repeating bass line, exactly the kind of structure that will form the climax of the Fourth Symphony (Chapter 11). The Haydn Variations also carries a small mystery in its very title, because the theme it varies was probably not written by Haydn at all.

### 7.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Around 1870, Brahms's friend Carl Ferdinand Pohl, the librarian of the Vienna Gesellschaft der Musikfreunde and a Haydn biographer, showed Brahms a manuscript of wind-band music attributed to Joseph Haydn. One movement contained a stately, dignified little melody marked "Chorale St. Antoni," the St. Anthony Chorale. Brahms copied it out, kept it, and a few years later made it the theme of his orchestral variations, publishing the work as _Variations on a Theme by Haydn_. The title stuck, and for a century the piece was known, simply, as the "Haydn Variations."

Here is the twist. Modern scholarship has come to doubt, strongly, that Haydn wrote the St. Anthony Chorale at all. The wind-band pieces in which it appears are no longer confidently attributed to Haydn; the theme may be by another composer (the name of Haydn's pupil Ignaz Pleyel has been floated), or it may be an older melody, perhaps a pilgrimage hymn, that found its way into the wind-band collection. The honest position today is that the theme's authorship is uncertain and probably not Haydn's. Some modern editions and program notes therefore call the work _Variations on the St. Antoni Chorale_ to avoid the false attribution. But the old title is so deeply established that "Haydn Variations" remains the common name, a small monument to a scholarly correction that arrived too late to change what everyone calls the piece.

This is a good early lesson in the book's conservative posture toward disputed fact. The work is real, the theme is real, the music is glorious; but the label "by Haydn" is doubtful, and an honest guide says so rather than passing on the old certainty. We will keep calling it the Haydn Variations, because that is its name, while remembering that the "Haydn" is in question.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The theme: dignified and easy to remember.** The piece opens with the St. Anthony Chorale stated by the wind instruments, a calm, stately, hymn-like tune with a distinctive shape (its opening phrase has an unusual five-bar length rather than the expected four). Fix this theme in your ear. The whole pleasure of the next eighteen minutes is hearing it transformed, so the more firmly you hold the original, the more you will enjoy the variations.

  2. **The variety of the eight variations.** Each of the eight variations gives the theme a completely different character: one stormy and minor, one a gentle siciliano, one a graceful flowing melody, one a delicate, fleet scherzo, and so on. Listen for how different they are from one another, and try, underneath each, to catch the bones of the original theme still present. Brahms ranges across the whole orchestra and every mood, while the theme's structure holds it all together.

  3. **The passacaglia finale.** The work does not simply end with a last variation; it climaxes with a finale built over a repeating bass line (a ground bass, the basis of the old forms called passacaglia and chaconne). Listen for the way a short bass pattern repeats again and again while the music above it grows and accumulates toward a triumphant restatement of the theme in full glory. This ground-bass finale is the direct ancestor of the Fourth Symphony's finale, and the climax of the whole set.

#### One-sentence background

The _Variations on a Theme by Haydn_ , Op. 56a, is an eighteen-minute work for orchestra (Brahms also made a version for two pianos, Op. 56b) composed in 1873, consisting of the "St. Anthony Chorale" theme, whose attribution to Haydn modern scholarship doubts, followed by eight strongly characterized variations and a grand passacaglia finale built over a repeating bass line; it was Brahms's first major purely orchestral work and a crucial step toward the First Symphony of 1876; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the recording by the conductor Otto Klemperer with the Philharmonia Orchestra, or by Bernard Haitink with the Concertgebouw or London forces, chosen for clarity of orchestral texture and command of the finale's architecture.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Haydn Variations Op. 56a Klemperer" or "Brahms St. Anthony Variations orchestra" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 海顿主题变奏曲 作品56a 克伦佩勒" 或 "勃拉姆斯 圣安东尼众赞歌变奏曲" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Bernard Haitink with the Concertgebouw or the Boston Symphony (Philips), clear, well-balanced, structurally lucid.
* Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), warm and polished.
* For comparison, a recording of the **two-piano version, Op. 56b** (several fine ones exist), which lets you hear the variations stripped to their musical bones, without orchestral color, a good way to hear the structure underneath.

The work is short enough to hear two or three times in one sitting. Do that: the first time to enjoy the colors, the second to track the theme through the variations, the third to follow the ground bass in the finale. Each listen reveals more, which is the nature of variation form.

### 7.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to a roughly eighteen-minute reading (Klemperer); they will shift with tempo. We will name the theme, each variation, and the finale.

**0:00. The theme: the St. Anthony Chorale.** The wind instruments state the theme: a calm, dignified, hymn-like melody. Listen to its character (stately, a little archaic, churchly) and notice its slightly irregular phrase lengths. This is the material everything grows from; hold it.

**About 2:00. Variation 1.** The strings take over with restless, swirling, agitated music, a complete change of mood from the calm theme, full of energy and counter-rhythm. The theme's structure is underneath, but the surface is transformed.

**About 3:00. Variation 2.** A stormier, darker variation, turning to the minor, with sharp, martial gestures. Listen for the dramatic contrast with the first.

**About 4:00. Variation 3.** A gentle, flowing, graceful variation, the theme sung as a smooth, lyrical melody by the winds and strings, consoling after the storm.

**About 6:00. Variation 4.** A slow, songful variation in the minor, with a long, expressive melody (introduced by oboe and horn), tender and a little melancholy. One of the emotional high points.

**About 8:30. Variation 5.** A quick, light, scampering scherzo-like variation, fleet and playful, the orchestra dancing.

**About 9:30. Variation 6.** A bold, vigorous variation with hunting-horn calls and martial energy, bright and outdoor.

**About 11:00. Variation 7.** A graceful, swaying siciliano (a gentle dance in lilting compound meter), the most serene and pastoral of the set, with a beautiful flowing melody.

**About 13:00. Variation 8.** A shadowy, veiled, fast variation, muted and mysterious, hushed and shimmering, a quiet, almost spectral close to the variations proper, setting up the finale by contrast.

**About 14:30. The Finale (passacaglia).** Now the masterstroke. Brahms builds the finale over a five-bar bass line (derived from the theme) that repeats again and again, a ground bass. Listen for the short bass pattern coming around and around, while above it the music grows ever richer and more elaborate, variation piling on variation over the unchanging foundation. The accumulation builds steadily in power until, near the end, the full St. Anthony Chorale theme returns in glory, blazed out by the whole orchestra in triumph. Listen for that return of the theme at full strength, the goal the whole finale has been climbing toward. The work ends in radiant affirmation.

The single most important thing to hear is that finale: the repeating bass under the growing music, and the triumphant return of the theme. That structure, ground bass plus accumulating variations plus a return, is exactly what the Fourth Symphony's finale will do on a tragic scale (Chapter 11). Here it is, in the bright, in eighteen minutes.

### 7.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Variation form heard whole

The Handel Variations (Chapter 5) introduced Brahms's central form for solo piano; the Haydn Variations shows it in the orchestra, and at a scale you can hold whole in your mind. This is the ideal piece for grasping what variation form _is_ as an experience: a theme, simple and memorable, then a series of transformations each keeping the theme's essential structure (its harmonic skeleton, its phrase shape) while changing everything on the surface (tempo, mood, key, color, texture). The pleasure is double: the delight of each new transformation, and the deeper satisfaction of recognizing the constant theme beneath the changing surface, of hearing sameness and difference at once. This is the experience at the heart of Brahms's art, because his whole method, developing variation, is the art of transforming a constant idea. Fix this experience firmly here. Every symphony movement you meet from now on uses the same instinct, even when it is not literally a set of variations: take an idea, transform it, keep its identity through the change.

#### The passacaglia / ground-bass finale

The finale deserves special attention, because it introduces a form that will return as the climax of this whole volume. A passacaglia (or chaconne, the terms overlap) is a Baroque form built over a _ground bass_ : a short bass line that repeats continuously, unchanged, while the music above it varies and grows. It is one of the oldest and most rigorous variation forms, and Brahms, the great student of the Baroque, loved it. In the Haydn finale he builds the climax over a five-bar ground derived from the theme, and the effect is of inexorable accumulation: the repeating bass is a foundation that never moves, while everything above it intensifies, so that the music seems to gather unstoppable momentum toward the return of the full theme. This is the form that will produce the Fourth Symphony's tragic finale (thirty variations over an eight-bar bass) and that connects directly to Bach, whose great organ passacaglia and violin chaconne Brahms revered (the Bach volume). The Haydn finale is your training ground: hear the ground bass here, in the bright and the brief, and you will be ready for its overwhelming use in the Fourth Symphony.

_(Forward pointer, per the series practice of planting threads at first appearance: the ground-bass / passacaglia principle you are hearing in this finale is collected with all its siblings in Chapter 20's survey of Brahms's variation forms, and reaches its tragic summit in the Fourth Symphony finale of Chapter 11.)_

#### Orchestration: the first full canvas

This was Brahms's first major work for orchestra alone, and you can hear him relishing the full palette. Each variation is colored differently: winds state the theme, strings swirl through Variation 1, oboe and horn sing Variation 4, hunting horns ring through Variation 6. Brahms, sometimes accused of "thick" or "muddy" orchestration by partisans of the more brilliant Wagner-Liszt school, here shows a sure and varied hand with orchestral color, deploying the instruments to characterize each variation sharply. The work was, in part, a public demonstration that Brahms could orchestrate, that the man the Wagnerians dismissed as a chamber-music academic could command the modern orchestra. Its success helped clear his path to the symphony. Listen, then, not only to the variations as structure but to the variations as color: this is Brahms learning, in public, to paint with the orchestra.

### 7.4 The Stories

#### The theme that probably is not Haydn's

The attribution question deserves a moment, because it is a model of how this book handles disputed fact. For a century the piece was simply "the Haydn Variations," and the theme was "by Haydn." Then twentieth-century scholarship examined the wind-band music in which the St. Anthony Chorale appears and concluded that it is probably not Haydn's work; the attribution is now widely regarded as doubtful, and the theme's true origin is unknown (it may be by another hand, or an older borrowed melody). None of this changes the music in the slightest, Brahms's variations are exactly as great whatever the theme's pedigree, but it changes what we can honestly _say_. So this book says: Brahms thought the theme was Haydn's and titled his work accordingly; modern scholarship doubts it; we keep the traditional title while flagging the doubt. This is the conservative posture in miniature: name the dispute, don't pretend to a certainty the evidence has withdrawn. You will meet larger versions of the same discipline in the Clara question (Chapter 4) and the Wagner-Brahms wars (Chapter 21).

#### The breath before the symphony

The Haydn Variations sits at a hinge in Brahms's career. Composed in 1873, it was his first major orchestral work, and it was a success, proof that he could write effectively for the orchestra. Just three years later, in 1876, the First Symphony finally appeared, ending the long avoidance that Chapter 5 described. It is hard not to see the Haydn Variations as the necessary intermediate step: a large orchestral work, but in the variation form where Brahms felt most secure, rather than the dreaded symphony with its Beethovenian burden. Having proved he could handle the orchestra here, in his home form, he could at last face the symphony. The variation set was the rehearsal; the symphony was the performance. And tellingly, when the symphony came, its very form bore the variation imprint: the First Symphony grows its material by continuous transformation, and the Fourth would end with a literal passacaglia, the form of the Haydn finale. The road to the symphonies ran straight through this work.

#### Recording history briefly

The Haydn Variations is a repertoire staple and a favorite orchestral showpiece, recorded by virtually every major conductor; Klemperer, Haitink, and Karajan all left distinguished accounts, and the work appears on countless Brahms-orchestral collections. The two-piano version (Op. 56b) is also recorded and is worth seeking out as a way to hear the structure without orchestral color. Because the piece is short and its form is clear, it is an excellent one for comparing conductors: listen to how differently they pace the finale's accumulation and time the return of the theme. The fuller Brahms-orchestral lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects forward across the volume.

To the Fourth Symphony (Chapter 11). The passacaglia finale here, ground bass, accumulating variations, triumphant return, is the direct, bright ancestor of the Fourth Symphony's tragic passacaglia finale. Learn the form here; meet its summit there.

To the symphonies generally (Chapters 8-11). The Haydn Variations was the orchestral proving-ground that cleared Brahms's path to the First Symphony. Its developing-variation method, growing music by transforming a theme, is the method of all four symphonies, even where they are not literally variation sets.

To the variation tradition (Chapter 18) and the synthesis (Chapter 20). The Haydn set takes its place in the great line of Brahms variation works, the Handel (Chapter 5), the Paganini (Chapter 18), the Schumann variations, and Chapter 20 collects them all as the clearest expression of his transforming instinct.

To Bach and the chorale preludes (Chapter 19). The ground-bass form connects Brahms to the Baroque passacaglia and chaconne he revered in Bach, and runs to his very last work, the Bach-style chorale preludes of 1896.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The work is called the 'Haydn Variations,' but modern scholarship doubts that Haydn wrote the theme at all. The book keeps the traditional title while flagging the doubt. Does it matter who wrote the theme, given that Brahms's variations are equally great regardless? What is the difference between the music (unchanged) and what we can honestly say about it (changed by scholarship)? Why might an honest guide insist on naming the doubt even when it changes nothing about the listening?"_

* _"The finale is built over a short bass line that repeats again and again while the music above it grows ever richer, until the full theme returns in triumph. Listen for that repeating bass (around 14:30) and the accumulation it carries. What is it about an unchanging foundation under constantly growing music that creates such a sense of inexorable build? Keep this experience in your ear, the Fourth Symphony's finale will use the same device on a tragic scale."_

* _"The chapter argues that this variation set was the 'deep breath before the plunge,' the work in which the symphony-shy Brahms proved he could command the orchestra, in his safest form, before finally daring the symphony three years later. Why might a great composer need to rehearse his courage this way? Have you ever approached something you feared by first proving yourself in a related but safer task?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Klemperer or Haitink recording (or one of the alternatives in §7.1). Listen at least twice: once for color, once for structure.

  1. **The St. Anthony Chorale theme (0:00).** Fix the calm, dignified, hymn-like theme firmly in your ear, with its slightly irregular phrase lengths. Everything that follows grows from it.

  2. **The variety of the eight variations (2:00 onward).** Track the theme through the eight transformations, the swirling first, the stormy second, the tender fourth, the lilting siciliano seventh, the shadowy eighth. Try to catch the theme's bones underneath each new surface.

  3. **The ground bass in the finale (about 14:30).** Listen for the short bass line repeating again and again under the growing music. This is the passacaglia principle, and the direct ancestor of the Fourth Symphony's finale.

  4. **The return of the theme in triumph (near the end).** Listen for the moment the full St. Anthony Chorale blazes out in glory at the climax of the finale, the goal the whole accumulation has been building toward. This triumphant return is the work's affirmative summit.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the two-piano version): Op. 56b, the version for two pianos, which Brahms made alongside the orchestral one. Stripped of orchestral color, the variations reveal their structure with great clarity; hearing both versions teaches you to separate what is "the music" from what is "the orchestration," a useful distinction for everything ahead.

**Listen** (the ground-bass tradition): Bach's great Passacaglia in C minor for organ, or the Chaconne from his D-minor violin Partita (the Bach volume), the supreme Baroque ground-bass works that Brahms revered. Hearing them shows the tradition the Haydn finale joins, and prepares you for the Fourth Symphony.

**Read** : Donald Francis Tovey's essay on the Haydn Variations in his _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , a classic, amateur-friendly walk through the theme, the variations, and the finale, by the analyst whose warm, lucid hearing guides much of this book's discussion of the orchestral works.

**Read** (on the attribution): the relevant discussion in any good modern Brahms study (Musgrave or MacDonald) of the St. Anthony Chorale's doubtful authorship, for the details of how scholarship came to question the "Haydn" attribution, a small but instructive case of how musical knowledge gets corrected over time.

## Chapter 8: Symphony No. 1 in C minor, Op. 68 (1855/76)

Here, at last, is the symphony. For more than twenty years Brahms had circled it, charged by Schumann to be Beethoven's heir, terrified by the comparison, pouring his symphonic ambition into chamber music and variations and the Requiem while the symphony itself waited. He began sketching ideas for it as early as 1855, in the shadow of the Schumann tragedy; he did not complete it until 1876, when he was forty-three. Few major works in music had so long and so anxious a gestation. And when it finally came, the First Symphony was so grand, so weighty, so unmistakably in the Beethovenian line, that one of the leading conductors of the day called it "Beethoven's Tenth," meaning it as the highest compliment, the symphony Beethoven might have written next.

This is an anchor-heavy chapter. The First Symphony is the work in which Brahms steps into the largest arena in instrumental music and proves he belongs there, and it rewards close attention. We will focus on its two outer movements, the dark, struggling, tightly argued first movement, and the great finale that turns from gloom to a blazing C-major affirmation by way of two unforgettable themes, an alphorn call and a broad chorale-like melody that the whole world hears as a cousin of Beethoven's "Ode to Joy." The inner movements we will sketch. The arc to carry, from the very first chapter, is the darkness-to-light shape, C minor to C major, that you already met in miniature in the Op. 5 sonata: a serious, tragic beginning, a long struggle, a hard-won triumph. It is the most Beethovenian thing Brahms ever did, and the most personal.

### 8.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

When the First Symphony appeared, in 1876, the conductor Hans von Bülow, one of Brahms's great champions, coined a phrase that would attach itself to the work forever: he spoke of "the Tenth," meaning Beethoven wrote nine symphonies, and here, at last, was the tenth, by Brahms. It was the supreme compliment, naming Brahms the legitimate heir of Beethoven. It was also, inevitably, a kind of trap, because it framed the work entirely in terms of the comparison Brahms had spent twenty years dreading.

The Beethoven shadow is written into the music itself, most famously in the finale. Its great broad melody, the "big tune" that arrives as the clouds finally clear, is so similar in outline to the "Ode to Joy" theme from Beethoven's Ninth that everyone noticed it at once, and people pointed it out to Brahms, presumably expecting him to be embarrassed by the resemblance. Brahms, who was prickly about the Beethoven comparison and not given to false modesty, is famously reported to have snapped: "Any fool can see that." The remark cuts two ways. It admits the resemblance is obvious and intentional, not an accident to be hidden; and it dismisses, with gruff impatience, the idea that the resemblance is the _point_. Brahms knew exactly what he was doing: claiming the Beethovenian inheritance openly, on his own terms, the C-minor-to-C-major struggle, the choral-style hymn of triumph, while writing music that is unmistakably his own. The First Symphony is Brahms's settlement with Beethoven's ghost, made in public, in the largest form, after a lifetime of fear. That he could face the comparison so directly, and win, is the drama behind the notes.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The pounding opening.** The symphony begins with one of the most arresting openings in the repertoire: over a relentless, pounding timpani pulse (a sustained drumbeat, a pedal point on a single note), the strings and winds strain upward in a slow, chromatic, anguished climb while the basses push downward, two opposing forces pulling apart. The effect is of enormous, grinding tension, struggle made audible from the first second. Listen for that pounding pulse and the music straining over it. This is the gateway to the whole symphony's drama.

  2. **The C-minor-to-C-major arc.** Listen across the whole symphony for the journey from darkness to light. It begins in turbulent C minor (the same key, and the same tragic-to-triumphant intent, as Beethoven's Fifth, which you met in the Beethoven volume) and ends in radiant, affirmative C major. The finale is where the transformation completes; the whole work is a single long climb from struggle to victory.

  3. **The finale's two great themes.** In the finale, after a dark, mysterious slow introduction, listen for two unforgettable melodies: first a noble horn call (the "alphorn theme," a melody Brahms heard on an alphorn in the Swiss Alps and sent to Clara Schumann with the words "thus blew the shepherd's horn today"), ringing out over hushed strings; and then the great broad "big tune," the Beethoven-cousin melody, that arrives as the main theme of the triumphant fast section. These two themes are the emotional payoff of the entire symphony.

#### One-sentence background

Symphony No. 1 in C minor, Op. 68, is a roughly forty-five-minute work in four movements, sketched from 1855 and completed in 1876 after a famously long and anxious gestation under the shadow of Beethoven, that traces a C-minor-to-C-major arc from struggle to triumph in the Beethovenian manner, with a pounding timpani-pedal opening and a finale crowned by a noble alphorn horn-call and a broad chorale-like "big tune" widely heard as a cousin of Beethoven's "Ode to Joy"; it premiered at Karlsruhe on November 4, 1876, under Otto Dessoff, and prompted Hans von Bülow's "Beethoven's Tenth" remark; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Wilhelm Furtwängler with the Berlin or Vienna Philharmonic (historic), or Bernard Haitink with the Concertgebouw (Philips), chosen for weight, drama, and clarity of the long arc.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Symphony No. 1 Furtwängler" or "Brahms Symphony 1 C minor Op. 68 Haitink" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 第一交响曲 富特文格勒" 或 "勃拉姆斯 c小调第一交响曲 作品68" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon; his several cycles), warm, weighty, and grand.
* Bernard Haitink with the Concertgebouw or London Symphony (Philips/LSO Live), lucid and well-proportioned.
* Wilhelm Furtwängler (historic, various), intensely dramatic and flexible, the great old-tradition reading; the sound is older but the interpretation is overwhelming.
* For the period-instrument approach, John Eliot Gardiner with the Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique, leaner and faster, with the textures clarified.

This is a forty-five-minute symphony; hear it whole on the first listen if you can. The drama only works as a single long arc: the struggle of the opening, the calmer inner movements, and the great release of the finale make sense only in sequence. If you must break it up, at least keep the first movement and the finale together in your mind, because they are the two pillars of the darkness-to-light journey.

### 8.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to a roughly forty-five-minute reading; they will shift with tempo. We go close on the first movement and the finale, and sketch the middle two.

**First movement (Un poco sostenuto — Allegro).**

_0:00. The slow introduction._ The pounding timpani pulse begins, and over it the music strains: the upper instruments climb in a slow, chromatic, aching ascent while the basses pull downward, the two motions stretching apart in grinding tension. This introduction, added late in the work's composition, announces the whole symphony's struggle in its first moments. Listen to the relentless drum and the straining lines.

_About 2:30. The Allegro: the struggle proper._ The main fast section erupts in turbulent C minor, driving, agitated, tightly argued. Listen for how Brahms grows the music from small motivic cells (the rising chromatic shape of the introduction recurs everywhere), developing variation as the engine of a symphonic argument. This is dense, serious, struggling music, the first movement of a symphony that has waited twenty years to be written and means to be worthy of the wait.

_About 12:00._ The movement drives to a tense close, settling at the end onto a quiet C major that hints, briefly, at the light to come, before the struggle resumes its hold. The first movement does not resolve the symphony's tension; it states it.

**Second movement (Andante sostenuto).** A warm, songful slow movement in a glowing major key, deeply lyrical, with a long oboe melody and, near the end, a tender duet for solo violin and horn. After the storm of the first movement, this is repose and beauty. Listen for the long-breathed melodies and the warmth; this is the consoling Brahms.

**Third movement (Un poco allegretto e grazioso).** In place of a driving scherzo, Brahms writes a gentle, graceful, intermezzo-like movement, lilting and songful, with a more energetic middle section. It is a moment of grace and lightness before the weight of the finale, characteristic of Brahms, who often replaced the traditional symphonic scherzo with something more relaxed and lyrical.

**Fourth movement (Adagio — Più andante — Allegro non troppo, ma con brio).**

_0:00. The dark introduction._ The finale opens in gloom and mystery, a slow, troubled introduction full of foreboding, pizzicato strings building tension, the darkness of the whole symphony gathered for one last time before the breakthrough.

_About 3:00. The alphorn call._ Suddenly, over hushed, shimmering strings, a horn rings out a noble, expansive call, the "alphorn theme," the melody Brahms heard in the Swiss mountains. It is a moment of sublime release, light breaking through cloud. A solemn trombone chorale answers. Listen to this passage closely; it is one of the great transitions in the symphonic repertoire, the turn from darkness toward light.

_About 5:00. The great "big tune."_ The fast main section begins, and its principal theme is the broad, noble, hymn-like melody, the "big tune," that everyone hears as a cousin of Beethoven's "Ode to Joy." It surges up in the strings, warm and affirmative, the goal the whole symphony has been climbing toward. Listen to its breadth and its triumph. This is C major arriving in full glory after forty minutes of struggle.

_About 9:00 onward. The drive to triumph._ The finale develops its themes and drives toward a blazing close. Near the very end, the solemn trombone chorale from the introduction returns, now in radiant major, crowning the symphony with a hymn of victory. Listen for that chorale's triumphant return, and for the final blaze of C major. The twenty-year struggle is won.

The arc to hold: the pounding, straining opening of the first movement (struggle stated) and the alphorn-and-big-tune release of the finale (triumph won). Between them lies the whole drama of Brahms's settlement with Beethoven and with his own long fear.

### 8.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Beethoven inheritance, claimed openly

The First Symphony is, among other things, Brahms's public reckoning with Beethoven, and he makes the inheritance explicit rather than hiding it. The C-minor-to-C-major arc is the arc of Beethoven's Fifth: tragedy transformed into triumph through the course of a symphony. The choral-style "big tune" of the finale openly echoes the "Ode to Joy" of Beethoven's Ninth, and Brahms's "any fool can see that" confirms the echo is deliberate. Even the finale's structure, a dark introduction breaking into an affirmative main theme, recalls the Ninth's finale. Brahms is not plagiarizing; he is claiming a lineage, declaring himself the legitimate continuer of the Beethovenian symphony, and doing so in the full knowledge that the world would compare them. The courage of this is easy to miss. To write a C-minor symphony ending in a choral-style C-major hymn, after Beethoven had made that the most famous gesture in all of music, was to invite the most dangerous comparison imaginable, and to bet that one could survive it. Brahms won the bet. "Beethoven's Tenth" was meant, and largely received, as the highest praise.

#### Developing variation as symphonic argument

Beneath the Beethovenian drama, the First Symphony is built by Brahms's own method: developing variation. Listen to how much of the first movement grows from the small motivic shapes of the opening, the rising chromatic figure, the pounding pulse; these cells recur, transformed, throughout, binding the movement into a tight organic whole. This is the principle you met in the lullaby and the variation sets, now operating as the engine of a forty-five-minute symphonic structure. Where Beethoven tends to build by dramatic confrontation of contrasting themes, Brahms builds by continuous derivation, growing the new from the old, so that the whole symphony feels as if it unfolds from a few seeds. This is what Schoenberg meant (Chapter 20) by calling Brahms "progressive": the method of organic motivic growth, more than any harmonic novelty, is what looks forward. The First Symphony is its first full symphonic demonstration.

#### The slow path to the finale's light

Notice how Brahms structures the journey to triumph. Unlike a more impatient composer, he does not rush to the light; he makes us wait, and earn it. The first movement states the struggle without resolving it. The two inner movements offer warmth and grace but not victory. And the finale itself withholds its triumph at first, opening in gloom, building tension, before the alphorn call breaks through and the great tune arrives. This long withholding is essential to the work's power: the C-major triumph of the finale means so much because the symphony has refused it for forty minutes. The breakthrough at the alphorn call (around 3:00 of the finale) is one of the supreme moments in Brahms precisely because of everything that precedes it. This is the architecture of earned affirmation, the darkness-to-light arc taken slowly, seriously, so that the light, when it comes, is overwhelming.

### 8.4 The Stories

#### Twenty years in the shadow

The First Symphony's gestation is one of the longest and most revealing in music. Brahms sketched ideas for a symphony as early as 1855, in the years of the Schumann crisis (some of that early material went instead into the First Piano Concerto, Chapter 12). For two decades the symphony hung over him, unwritten, because to write one was to step directly into Beethoven's arena, and Brahms, charged by Schumann's "Neue Bahnen" to be Beethoven's heir, could not bring himself to risk the comparison until he was certain. "You have no idea how it feels to hear behind you the tramp of a giant like Beethoven," he told Hermann Levi (Chapter 5). He completed the work only in 1876, at forty-three, after the Haydn Variations (Chapter 7) had proved he could command the orchestra. The premiere took place at Karlsruhe on November 4, 1876, under the conductor Otto Dessoff, deliberately in a smaller city rather than in Vienna or Leipzig, so that Brahms could test the work away from the fiercest critics. Its success was decisive, and once the dam broke, the Second Symphony followed within a year (Chapter 9). The twenty-year wait, then a flood: that is the shape of Brahms's symphonic career, and it is the shape of a man finally laying down a fear he had carried since he was twenty.

#### Recording history briefly

The First Symphony is central repertoire with an enormous discography. The great old-tradition readings (Furtwängler, with his overwhelming drama and flexibility) sit alongside the warm, weighty modern accounts (Karajan, Haitink) and the leaner period-instrument approach (Gardiner). Comparing a broad, monumental reading with a swift, clarified one reveals how much the symphony's drama depends on the conductor's pacing of the long arc, especially the timing of the finale's breakthrough. The fuller Brahms-symphony lineage, the Weingartners and Walters and Böhms and Kleibers, is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The First Symphony connects across the volume.

To the Op. 5 Piano Sonata (Chapter 3), which shares the darkness-to-light arc (there F minor to F major, here C minor to C major), the Beethovenian shape the young Brahms already commanded at twenty. The sonata is the seed; the symphony is the full tree.

To the other three symphonies (Chapters 9-11). The First is the heroic, struggling, Beethovenian symphony; the Second is the pastoral, lyric one; the Third is the autumnal, motto-driven one; the Fourth is the tragic, variation-crowned one. Hearing the First first establishes the developing-variation method and the seriousness of Brahms's symphonic ambition that the others build on.

To Beethoven. The whole First Symphony is in dialogue with Beethoven, especially the Fifth (the C-minor-to-C-major arc) and the Ninth (the choral-style finale tune). Having met those works in the Beethoven volume makes the dialogue audible.

To the synthesis (Chapter 20). The developing-variation method that drives the First Symphony's argument is collected, with all its instances, in Chapter 20, where Schoenberg's claim that Brahms's organic motivic growth makes him "progressive" is laid out and weighed.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"When the resemblance between the First Symphony's 'big tune' finale theme and Beethoven's 'Ode to Joy' was pointed out to Brahms, he is reported to have snapped, 'Any fool can see that.' Listen to the finale's great melody (around 5:00) and compare it in your memory to the 'Ode to Joy.' Why might Brahms have made the resemblance deliberate and open rather than hiding it? What does it mean to claim an inheritance in public, knowing everyone will compare you to your great predecessor?"_

* _"Brahms made the symphony withhold its triumph for forty minutes, the struggling first movement, the gentle inner movements, the gloomy finale introduction, before the alphorn call breaks through and the great tune finally arrives in C major. Listen to that breakthrough (around 3:00 of the finale). Why does the triumph mean so much more for having been delayed and refused for so long? What does this teach about the value of earned, rather than easy, resolution?"_

* _"It took Brahms over twenty years to finish his First Symphony, paralyzed by the fear of comparison to Beethoven, whose footsteps he said he heard behind him. Once it succeeded, the next symphony came within a year. What does it tell you that the hardest part was beginning, and that once the fear was faced the music flowed? Have you experienced a long-dreaded task that, once begun, proved far less paralyzing than the dread of it?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a recording from §8.1. Hear the symphony whole, then focus on these.

  1. **The pounding opening (0:00).** Fix in your ear the relentless timpani pulse and the upper and lower instruments straining apart over it. This grinding tension is the whole symphony's struggle stated in its first moments.

  2. **The C-minor-to-C-major arc (across the work).** Track the journey from the turbulent C minor of the opening to the radiant C major of the finale's close. The whole symphony is a single long climb from struggle to triumph.

  3. **The alphorn breakthrough (about 3:00 of the finale).** Listen for the horn ringing out its noble call over hushed strings, light breaking through cloud, answered by a solemn trombone chorale. This is one of the great transitions in the symphonic repertoire.

  4. **The "big tune" and the chorale's triumphant return (about 5:00, and near the end of the finale).** Listen to the broad, Beethoven-cousin melody that crowns the finale, and to the return of the trombone chorale in radiant major at the very end. This is the earned victory the whole work has climbed toward.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the Beethoven dialogue): the first movement and finale of Beethoven's Fifth Symphony (C minor to C major) and the finale of his Ninth (the "Ode to Joy"), both in the Beethoven volume. Hearing them freshly alongside the Brahms First makes the inheritance, and Brahms's transformation of it, fully audible: same arc, same gesture, wholly different voice.

**Listen** (the Second Symphony): if the First's struggle has moved you, go straight to the opening of the Second Symphony (Chapter 9), composed within a year, and hear how completely Brahms changed register, from heroic C-minor struggle to sunlit D-major pastoral. The contrast tells you how much range the symphonist had been holding back.

**Read** : Walter Frisch's _Brahms: The Four Symphonies_ (1996), the chapter on the First, for a clear, music-focused account of the long gestation, the Beethoven relationship, and the developing-variation method at work.

**Read** (the analyst): Donald Francis Tovey's essay on the First Symphony in his _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , the warm, lucid, amateur-friendly walk through the work by the analyst whose hearing guides this book's discussion of the orchestral Brahms.
