## Chapter 9: Symphony No. 2 in D major, Op. 73 (1877)

The First Symphony cost Brahms twenty years of fear. The Second cost him a single happy summer. Having finally broken through the Beethoven barrier in 1876, Brahms wrote his Second Symphony the very next year, in a few months of 1877, while on holiday at Pörtschach, a lakeside village in the Austrian Alps, and the contrast with the First could hardly be greater. Where the First is a dark, struggling, heroic C-minor drama, the Second is sunlit, pastoral, lyrical, a D-major symphony so warm and songful that it has been called Brahms's "Pastoral" (after Beethoven's Sixth) and his "Vienna" symphony. It is the most immediately lovable of the four, the one to fall for first.

And yet, listen closely and you will find that the sunlight has a shadow in it. Brahms himself, with his dry, self-mocking humor, teased his friends about the work's darkness even as he wrote its happiest music. This chapter takes the Second Symphony as a study in Brahms's characteristic doubleness, warmth shadowed by something darker, the same bittersweet quality you first heard in one chord of the Wiegenlied, now spread across a whole symphony. We will focus on the first movement, the great pastoral structure that hides a dark undertow and grows, with wonderful economy, from a tiny three-note cell, and the gracious third movement, one of Brahms's most charming. The first movement is also our central case study in developing variation at the level of pure technique: watch how much music Brahms grows from three notes.

### 9.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Brahms loved to puncture sentiment, especially about himself, and the Second Symphony gave him fine opportunities. While the work was taking shape in the summer of 1877, and even after, he wrote to friends in a vein of mock-gloom that completely contradicted the music's sunny surface. To one he described the new symphony as "so melancholy that you will not be able to bear it"; to his publisher he joked that the score should be printed with a black border, as if for a funeral. To the conductor and to Clara Schumann he sent similarly teasing warnings about how mournful and difficult the piece was.

The joke, of course, was that the Second Symphony is the sunniest, most lyrical, most immediately winning of all four, anything but a black-bordered funeral piece. Brahms was needling the listeners who expected him to be heavy and serious, and perhaps needling himself, the famous melancholic who had just written a symphony full of sunshine. But like many of Brahms's jokes, this one has a true edge. There _is_ darkness in the Second Symphony, a recurring shadow that crosses the pastoral landscape, most audibly in the first movement, where solemn trombones intrude on the sunlit opening with a sudden, grave foreboding. The work is not the unclouded idyll its reputation suggests; it is sunlight that knows about shadow, happiness shadowed by the awareness of loss. The black-border joke, like most of Brahms's humor, points at a real truth wrapped in self-mockery. Listen for the shadow inside the sunshine; it is the most Brahmsian thing about the work.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The pastoral warmth.** From the opening, listen to the symphony's sunlit, songful, outdoor character: warm horn calls, lilting rhythms, long flowing melodies, the feel of a summer landscape. This is the most relaxed and lyrical of the four symphonies, and its beauty is immediate. Let it charm you.

  2. **The three-note cell, and how much grows from it.** The very opening of the first movement contains a tiny three-note figure in the low strings (a turn around a note: down a step and back, D-C#-D). Listen for this small shape, because it is the seed from which Brahms grows an astonishing amount of the movement. Once you have it in your ear, you will start to hear it everywhere, transformed into melodies, accompaniments, transitions. This is developing variation in its purest, most demonstrable form.

  3. **The shadow crossing the sun.** Listen, in the first movement, for the moment the sunlight darkens: solemn, low trombones (held in reserve for this) enter with a grave, almost funereal shadow, a sudden cloud over the pastoral scene. This intrusion of darkness into the idyll is the truth behind Brahms's "black border" joke, and it recurs, subtly, across the work. The Second Symphony is sunlight that contains its own shadow.

#### One-sentence background

Symphony No. 2 in D major, Op. 73, is a roughly forty-minute work in four movements composed in a single happy summer in 1877 at Pörtschach in the Austrian Alps, the year after the long-delayed First, and is the sunniest, most pastoral and lyrical of Brahms's four symphonies, though it hides a recurring dark undertow (Brahms joked it should be printed with a funeral black border); its first movement grows much of its material from a tiny three-note cell, and it premiered in Vienna on December 30, 1877; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Carlos Kleiber with the Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), one of the most celebrated of all Brahms recordings, chosen for its radiance, lift, and clarity (Kleiber recorded only the Second and Fourth).

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Symphony No. 2 Carlos Kleiber Vienna" or "Brahms Symphony 2 D major Op. 73" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 第二交响曲 卡洛斯·克莱伯 维也纳" 或 "勃拉姆斯 D大调第二交响曲 作品73" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), warm and golden.
* Bernard Haitink with the Concertgebouw (Philips), lyrical and well-proportioned.
* Bruno Walter with the Columbia Symphony (Sony, mid-century), genial and songful, a classic.
* John Eliot Gardiner (period instruments), lighter and clearer in texture.

Hear the symphony whole; its four movements form a satisfying arc from pastoral warmth through a deep slow movement and a gracious intermezzo to a joyful, blazing finale. The Kleiber recording in particular is famous for making the whole work feel buoyant and alive, an ideal first encounter.

### 9.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to a roughly forty-minute reading (Kleiber is on the swifter side); they will shift with tempo. We go close on the first and third movements and sketch the second and fourth.

**First movement (Allegro non troppo).**

_0:00. The three-note cell and the horn theme._ The movement opens with the tiny three-note figure in the low strings (down a step and back), and immediately the horns sing a warm, pastoral melody above it. Listen for both at once: the little cell underneath, the broad horn tune on top. Almost everything in the movement will grow from that cell.

_About 1:30. The shadow: the trombones._ The sunlit opening is suddenly crossed by a grave shadow: solemn low trombones (and tuba) enter with a dark, foreboding gravity, a cloud over the pastoral landscape. This is the darkness Brahms's "black border" joke pointed to. Listen for how it intrudes on the warmth, and how it colors the whole movement with an undertow of unease beneath the beauty.

_About 3:00. The lyrical second theme._ A famous, broad, warm melody in the cellos and violas (one of Brahms's most beloved tunes, sometimes likened to a lullaby and indeed related in shape to the Wiegenlied). Listen to its songful warmth. The movement sets this lyricism against the developing energy of the cell-derived material.

_About 6:00 onward. Development._ Listen here for developing variation made audible: Brahms takes the three-note cell and the themes and transforms them continuously, the cell turning into accompaniments, transitions, new melodic shapes, so that the whole long movement seems to grow organically from its opening seeds. This is the technique at its clearest; if you have the cell in your ear, you can hear it constantly reborn.

_About 15:00. The close._ The movement ends gently, the pastoral warmth restored, the horn singing the work toward repose. The shadow has crossed but not conquered.

**Second movement (Adagio non troppo).** A deep, serious, richly scored slow movement in B major, the most solemn music in the symphony, with long, complex melodies and a searching, sometimes troubled character. This is where the work's hidden gravity comes most to the surface. Listen for the depth and the harmonic richness; after the pastoral first movement, this is the symphony's contemplative heart.

**Third movement (Allegretto grazioso).** A delightful, gracious movement in place of a scherzo: it opens with a charming, gently lilting oboe melody over pizzicato cellos (a little serenade), then breaks twice into faster, scampering variations of the same tune before the gentle melody returns. Listen for the wit and grace, and for the way the faster sections are transformations of the opening melody, developing variation turned to playful, charming ends. This is one of Brahms's most immediately delightful movements.

**Fourth movement (Allegro con spirito).** A joyful, energetic finale that begins quietly, almost secretively, then bursts into exuberant, driving music and builds to one of the most jubilant conclusions Brahms ever wrote, the trombones that brought shadow to the first movement now blazing out in triumph, the symphony ending in a blaze of D-major joy. Listen for the quiet, conspiratorial opening and the overwhelming brightness of the close. The shadow is finally banished; the sun wins.

The two things to carry: the three-note cell and the wealth Brahms grows from it (first movement), and the dark trombone shadow crossing the sunlight (first movement, banished in the finale). Together they make the Second Symphony a study in warmth that knows about shadow.

### 9.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Developing variation made demonstrable

The first movement of the Second Symphony is the clearest classroom example in this book of developing variation as a technique, which is why we dwell on it. The movement opens with a three-note cell, a simple turn around a note, and Brahms then grows a vast amount of the movement's material from it: the horn theme is built on it, the transitions derive from it, accompaniment figures echo it, new melodies unfold from its shape. The whole movement seems to develop organically from this seed, the way a plant grows from a single cell, each new phrase derived from what came before by small transformation. This is exactly what Schoenberg meant by "developing variation," and what he claimed made Brahms forward-looking (Chapter 20). The pleasure of the movement, once you hear the technique, is the pleasure of organic unity: everything belongs, everything is related, nothing is arbitrary, and yet the surface is endlessly varied. If you want to understand in your ears what this book means by Brahms's central method, listen to this movement with the three-note cell fixed in your memory, and hear how it is everywhere. It is the most demonstrable instance in the whole volume.

#### The shadow inside the sun: Brahms's doubleness

The Second Symphony's deepest quality is its doubleness, the way its sunny surface contains a genuine shadow. The intruding trombones of the first movement, the searching gravity of the slow movement, the conspiratorial hush before the finale's joy: these are not flaws in an otherwise happy work but the source of its depth. Pure sunshine would be merely pleasant; sunshine that knows about shadow is moving. This is the same quality you heard, in miniature, in the Wiegenlied, where one bittersweet chord kept the lullaby from being merely sweet. It runs through all of Brahms: the major-key warmth shadowed by minor-key feeling, the happiness aware of loss, the consolation that has faced grief (the German Requiem). Brahms's "black border" joke names it exactly. The Second Symphony is the largest, sunniest demonstration of the principle that, in Brahms, light and shadow are always present together, and that the warmth is the deeper for the shadow within it.

#### The lyrical symphonist

The Second also shows Brahms as a supreme melodist on the symphonic scale. The first movement's broad cello-and-viola second theme, the third movement's lilting oboe serenade, the horn melodies throughout, these are tunes as singable and immediate as his Lieder, and the work flows with melodic generosity. This corrects the "heavy academic" caricature decisively: the composer of the densest fugues was also the writer of the world's lullaby and of melodies as warm and natural as any in the Romantic symphony. The Second Symphony is where Brahms's melodic gift fills a symphony with sunlight, and it reminds us that beneath all the developing-variation craft there is, always, a song-composer's instinct for a beautiful, breathing tune. Hold both: the rigor of the three-note-cell construction, and the warmth of the melodies it generates. They are the same Brahms.

### 9.4 The Stories

#### The happy summer at Pörtschach

After the twenty-year ordeal of the First Symphony, the Second came as a gift. Brahms spent the summer of 1877 at Pörtschach am Wörthersee, a beautiful village on a lake in the Austrian region of Carinthia, and he found the place so inspiring that he wrote, in a happy mood, that the landscape was so full of melodies that one had to be careful not to step on them. The Second Symphony poured out of him there in a few months, an astonishing contrast to the agonized decades behind the First. The lakeside, the mountains, the summer light: all of it is in the music's pastoral warmth. Brahms loved spending his summers in such places (Pörtschach, later Mürzzuschlag, Bad Ischl, Thun), composing in the mornings and walking in the afternoons, and much of his greatest music was written on these working holidays. The Second Symphony is the supreme product of that happy summer rhythm, the sound of a man who had finally laid down a great fear and could let the melodies come. It premiered in Vienna on December 30, 1877, under Hans Richter, and was an immediate and lasting success, the most quickly loved of the four.

#### Recording history briefly

The Second Symphony has a glorious discography, and Carlos Kleiber's Vienna Philharmonic recording (our primary) is among the most celebrated of all Brahms recordings, famous for its buoyancy, lift, and radiance; Kleiber, who recorded very little, left only the Second and Fourth symphonies of Brahms, and both are legendary. The warm Karajan and Walter readings and the lucid Haitink offer fine alternatives, and Gardiner's period approach lightens the textures. Because the work's character is sunlight-with-shadow, comparing a buoyant reading (Kleiber) with a weightier one (Karajan) shows how much the balance of warmth and gravity lies in the conductor's hands. The fuller lineage is in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Second Symphony connects across the volume.

To the First Symphony (Chapter 8). The pairing is one of the great contrasts in the repertoire: the dark, struggling, heroic First and the sunlit, lyrical, pastoral Second, written within a year of each other. Hearing them together shows the range Brahms had been holding back during the long symphonic silence.

To the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2). The bittersweet shadow inside the Second's sunshine is the same quality you heard in the lullaby's one dark chord, and the first movement's broad second theme is itself related in shape to the lullaby. The smallest and the symphonic share a single Brahmsian truth: warmth shadowed by knowledge.

To the synthesis (Chapter 20). The first movement's three-note cell is the volume's clearest demonstration of developing variation as a technique, and Chapter 20 returns to it as a central example of the method Schoenberg called "progressive."

To the Third Symphony (Chapter 10). The Third also threads a small motivic cell, the F-A♭-F "motto," through a whole symphony, taking the developing-variation principle of the Second's first movement and making it the organizing idea of an entire work. The Second prepares your ear for the Third.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Brahms joked that his sunny Second Symphony was so melancholy it should be printed with a funeral black border, a joke that contradicts the music's warm surface but points to the real shadow within it, the grave trombones that cross the sunlight. Listen for that dark intrusion in the first movement (around 1:30). Why might a composer want shadow inside an otherwise happy work? Is sunshine that knows about shadow more moving than pure sunshine, and if so, why?"_

* _"The first movement grows an enormous amount of music from a tiny three-note cell heard in the very first bars. Try the experiment: fix that little three-note shape in your ear, then listen for how often it returns, transformed, across the movement. What is the satisfaction of hearing a whole structure grow organically from one seed? Does knowing the technique increase your pleasure, or do you prefer to let it work on you unanalyzed?"_

* _"The Second Symphony poured out of Brahms in a single happy summer, the year after the First had cost him twenty years of fear. What does it tell you that, once the great barrier was broken, the music came so easily and so joyfully? Listen to the buoyancy of the Kleiber recording and consider how much of a work's character might come from the composer's state of mind, and how much from pure craft."_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Kleiber recording (or an alternative from §9.1). Hear the symphony whole, then focus on these.

  1. **The three-note cell and the horn theme (0:00).** Fix the tiny three-note figure in the low strings and the warm horn melody above it. Then listen for how the cell generates material throughout the first movement, developing variation made demonstrable.

  2. **The trombone shadow (about 1:30).** Listen for the grave, low trombones crossing the sunlit opening with sudden foreboding. This dark intrusion is the truth behind the "black border" joke and the source of the work's depth.

  3. **The gracious third movement (the Allegretto grazioso).** Listen to the charming lilting oboe melody over pizzicato cellos, and to the two faster scampering sections that are transformations of it. This is developing variation turned to playful, delightful ends.

  4. **The jubilant finale (the fourth movement).** Listen to the quiet, conspiratorial opening and the overwhelming brightness of the close, the trombones that brought shadow now blazing in triumph. The sun wins.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the pairing): the First Symphony's finale (Chapter 8) back to back with the Second's first movement, to feel the full contrast between the heroic, struggling Brahms and the pastoral, lyrical one, written within a single year. The two symphonies are the dark and light faces of the same newly liberated symphonist.

**Listen** (the pastoral tradition): Beethoven's Sixth Symphony, the "Pastoral" (in the Beethoven volume), the great precedent for the symphony-as-landscape. Brahms's Second is sometimes called his "Pastoral," and hearing Beethoven's first shows the tradition of the sunlit, outdoor symphony that Brahms was joining and transforming.

**Read** : Walter Frisch's _Brahms: The Four Symphonies_ (1996), the chapter on the Second, especially his analysis of the first movement's developing variation from the three-note cell, which makes the technique vivid and precise.

**Read** (the analyst): Tovey's essay on the Second Symphony in his _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , warm and lucid as always, particularly good on the work's lyrical character and its hidden gravity.

## Chapter 10: Symphony No. 3 in F major, Op. 90 (1883)

The Third Symphony is the shortest of the four, the most concentrated, and in some ways the most personal. Composed in a single summer in 1883, when Brahms was fifty, it is built around a tiny three-note motto, F-A♭-F, that Brahms is widely held to have associated with a personal motive of his own: _Frei aber froh_ , "free but happy," his answer to his friend Joseph Joachim's youthful motto _Frei aber einsam_ , "free but lonely." Whether or not the motto-to-motive link is as firm as tradition makes it, the three notes are unmistakably present, opening the symphony and threading through all four movements, and they make the Third the clearest case in this volume of an entire symphony organized around a single motivic cell. Where the Second grew its first movement from three notes, the Third grows a whole symphony from three notes.

The Third is also the most autumnal and the most enigmatic of the four. The conductor Hans Richter, who led the premiere, called it Brahms's "Eroica," likening it to Beethoven's heroic Third, but the comparison is misleading, because Brahms's Third is the least triumphant of his symphonies. It does not end in a blaze of victory like the First and Second; it ends quietly, the storm subsiding into a hushed, golden calm. This chapter focuses on the famous third-movement Poco allegretto, one of the most beloved melodies Brahms ever wrote, and the first movement, where the F-A♭-F motto is launched and the symphony's characteristic major-minor ambiguity is established. The Third is an anchor-heavy chapter because its concentration repays close listening: there is not a wasted bar.

### 10.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

In their idealistic youth, Brahms and his closest friend, the violinist Joseph Joachim, exchanged personal mottos in the Romantic fashion of the time. Joachim, lonely and unmarried, took as his device _Frei aber einsam_ , "Free but lonely," and the two friends even built a little musical motif on its initials, the notes F-A-E, which they and Schumann once wove into a collaborative violin sonata. Years later, the story goes, Brahms answered his friend's melancholy motto with a happier one of his own: _Frei aber froh_ , "Free but happy," F-A-F, the notes of his own device.

Tradition has long connected this _Frei aber froh_ motto to the three-note cell, F-A♭-F, that opens and pervades the Third Symphony. The link is appealing and widely repeated, the symphony built on Brahms's personal declaration of contented freedom, and the notes really are there, threading the whole work. But honesty requires a flag: the firm biographical connection between the F-A-F motto and the symphony's F-A♭-F cell is partly a later interpretive tradition rather than a fully documented statement by Brahms himself, and the symphony's cell uses A-flat (the minor-mode note), not the A-natural of the supposed "happy" motto, which complicates the "free but happy" reading and gives the music its characteristic major-minor ambiguity. So we will say: the three-note cell unmistakably organizes the symphony; the connection to Brahms's personal _Frei aber froh_ motto is a traditional and attractive reading that should be held a little loosely. What matters for listening is the cell itself, F-A♭-F, present from the first bar, threading every movement, the clearest motivic unification in all four symphonies.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The F-A♭-F motto, from the very first bar.** The symphony opens with three strong wind chords, and the top notes spell the motto: F, then A-flat, then F. This three-note shape, with its telltale flattened middle note, is the seed of the entire symphony. Fix it in your ear at once, because Brahms threads it through all four movements, as a bass, as an accompaniment, as the bones of melodies. Listen for those three notes everywhere.

  2. **Major and minor at once.** The flattened A in the motto (A-flat, not A-natural) means the symphony lives in an ambiguity between major and minor, between brightness and shadow, from its first chord. The opening is grand and major, but the A-flat darkens it instantly. Listen for this constant shimmer between light and dark; it is the harmonic signature of the whole work and the reason the Third feels more ambivalent, more autumnal, than the heroic First or the sunny Second.

  3. **The quiet ending.** Unlike the First and Second, the Third does not end in triumph. Listen, at the very end of the finale, for how the storm subsides into a hushed, glowing calm: the motto returns transformed, the music sinks to a peaceful, golden close in the major. This quiet, un-triumphant ending is one of the most beautiful and unusual in the symphonic repertoire, and it is the key to the work's autumnal character.

#### One-sentence background

Symphony No. 3 in F major, Op. 90, is a roughly thirty-five-minute work in four movements, the shortest and most concentrated of Brahms's symphonies, composed in a single summer in 1883 (at Wiesbaden) and organized throughout by a three-note motto, F-A♭-F, traditionally linked to Brahms's personal motive _Frei aber froh_ ("free but happy") though the connection is partly a later reading; it lives in a characteristic major-minor ambiguity, contains the famous third-movement Poco allegretto, and ends not in triumph but in a hushed, golden calm; it premiered in Vienna on December 2, 1883, under Hans Richter, who called it Brahms's "Eroica"; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the recording by Bernard Haitink with the Concertgebouw, or by Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), chosen for warmth, clarity, and command of the work's autumnal close.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Symphony No. 3 Haitink Concertgebouw" or "Brahms Symphony 3 F major Op. 90 Karajan" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 第三交响曲 海廷克" 或 "勃拉姆斯 F大调第三交响曲 作品90" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), warm and richly upholstered.
* Wilhelm Furtwängler (historic), intensely dramatic; the older sound rewards patience.
* Bernard Haitink with the Concertgebouw or the London Symphony (Philips/LSO Live), lucid and finely balanced.
* John Eliot Gardiner (period instruments), clearer textures, lighter weight.

Hear the symphony whole; it is short enough to take in one sitting easily, and its concentration means the four movements form an unusually tight, continuous arc. Pay special attention to how the symphony begins (the three motto chords) and ends (the hushed calm); the journey between them is the work's secret.

### 10.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to a roughly thirty-five-minute reading; they will shift with tempo. We go close on the first and third movements and sketch the second and fourth.

**First movement (Allegro con brio).**

_0:00. The motto chords._ The symphony opens with three grand, emphatic wind-and-brass chords whose top notes spell the motto: F, A-flat, F. The flattened middle note darkens the major brightness instantly. Then, over this motto in the bass, the violins launch the great main theme, a soaring, impassioned melody that sweeps downward and surges. Listen for the motto underneath the theme; the whole movement rides on those three notes.

_About 2:00. The major-minor shimmer._ Listen for how the music constantly shifts between major and minor, bright and shadowed, the A-flat of the motto pulling the brightness toward darkness again and again. This ambiguity is the movement's, and the symphony's, harmonic signature.

_About 3:30. The lyrical second theme._ A gentler, swaying second theme (introduced by the clarinet in a relaxed, almost dance-like meter) provides contrast, warm and graceful. The movement sets the impassioned first material against this lyricism and develops both, the motto woven through everything.

_About 9:00. The close._ The first movement ends not loudly but with the great main theme returning and then subsiding into a quiet, warm close, the motto softly present. Even the first movement, for all its passion, declines a triumphant ending, a foretaste of the whole symphony's preference for repose over victory.

**Second movement (Andante).** A gentle, songful slow movement in C major, opening with a simple, hymn-like wind melody, calm and pastoral, with a more mysterious, harmonically searching middle section. Listen for the serene wind chorale at the opening and the way Brahms shadows it with stranger harmonies later. This is quiet, inward music, the symphony's contemplative rest.

**Third movement (Poco allegretto).**

_0:00. The famous melody._ In place of a scherzo, Brahms writes one of the most beloved melodies he ever composed: a melancholy, yearning tune in C minor, sung first by the cellos, rising and falling in a long, sighing arc over a gently rocking accompaniment. Listen to this melody; it is autumnal, tender, a little heartbroken, and unforgettable. (It became so famous that it has been borrowed for films and popular songs.) The melody is then taken up by other instruments, passed around, deepened.

_About 3:00. The horn's return of the theme._ In one of the movement's most magical strokes, the great melody returns, sung by the solo horn (and then oboe) over the rocking accompaniment, with a warmth and nostalgia that is the emotional peak of the whole symphony. Listen for the horn taking up the tune; it is one of the supreme moments in Brahms. The movement closes in gentle melancholy.

**Fourth movement (Allegro).**

_0:00. The hushed, ominous opening._ The finale begins quietly, mysteriously, with a hushed, restless theme in the low strings, full of suppressed tension and foreboding, the gathering of the symphony's darkest energies.

_About 1:30. The storm._ The music erupts into the most turbulent, dramatic passage in the symphony, a real storm, with a grave chorale-like theme (derived from the second movement) sounding through the turmoil. Listen for the drama and the struggle; the finale is where the work's tensions come to a head.

_About 6:00. The subsidence into calm._ And then, instead of a triumphant climax, the most remarkable thing: the storm subsides. The energy drains away, the music grows quiet, and the symphony sinks toward a hushed, glowing close. The motto, F-A♭-F, returns transformed; the great first-movement theme is recalled, softened; and the whole work settles into a peaceful, golden F major, fading to silence. Listen for this extraordinary ending, the storm spent, the music at peace. No fanfare, no blaze, just resolution and rest. This quiet close is the heart of the Third Symphony's autumnal character and one of the most beautiful endings in the repertoire.

The two things to carry: the F-A♭-F motto threading the whole symphony (heard from the first bar), and the autumnal Poco allegretto with its horn-borne return (third movement). And the singular fact that this symphony, alone of the four, ends not in triumph but in quiet golden calm.

### 10.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### A symphony built on three notes

The Third Symphony is the clearest case in this volume of an entire symphony unified by a single motivic cell. The F-A♭-F motto opens the work and then threads through all four movements: it is in the bass under the first theme, in accompaniment figures, in the bones of melodies, in the transformed return at the very end. This is developing variation extended from a single movement (as in the Second's first movement) to an entire symphony: the whole work grows from, and is bound together by, three notes. The effect is of extraordinary organic unity, a sense that everything in the symphony belongs to one family, derived from one seed, which is part of why the Third feels so concentrated and so personal. If the Second Symphony's first movement is the volume's clearest demonstration of developing variation within a movement, the Third is its clearest demonstration across a whole symphony. Chapter 20 returns to the motto as a central example of the method.

#### Major and minor, brightness and shadow

The motto's flattened middle note, A-flat rather than A-natural, gives the Third Symphony its defining harmonic character: a constant ambiguity between major and minor, brightness and shadow. The work is nominally in F major, but the A-flat keeps pulling it toward F minor, so that the music shimmers between the two, never settling wholly into either. This is why the Third feels more ambivalent, more autumnal, more shadowed than the heroic First (which earns its way decisively from minor to major) or the sunny Second (whose shadow is an intruder on a major-key idyll). In the Third, light and dark are fused at the level of the motto itself, present together in every statement of the three notes. The supposed "free but happy" reading is complicated by exactly this: the symphony is not simply happy; it is happy-and-shadowed, free-and-melancholy, which is more truthful and more moving than unclouded contentment. The major-minor shimmer is the sound of a fifty-year-old who knows that freedom and happiness are real but never simple.

#### The refusal of triumph

The most unusual structural feature of the Third is its ending. The First and Second symphonies end in blazes of major-key triumph, the inherited Beethovenian shape of struggle crowned by victory. The Third refuses it. Its finale gathers the work's darkest energies into a real storm, and then, instead of resolving the storm into a triumphant climax, lets it subside into a hushed, golden calm, the motto returning transformed, the music sinking to peaceful rest. This is a profound choice. To end a symphony quietly, with repose rather than triumph, was unusual and bold, and it gives the Third its distinctive emotional truth: not the conquest of darkness by light, but the acceptance of both, the storm spent and the soul at peace. Hans Richter's "Eroica" comparison, suggesting heroic triumph, misses this entirely; the Third is the least heroic and the most reconciled of the four. The quiet ending is the work's deepest statement, and it points forward to the autumnal, valedictory tone of Brahms's late music, the late piano pieces (Chapter 17), the clarinet works (Chapter 15), where repose and acceptance, rather than struggle and victory, become the prevailing mood.

### 10.4 The Stories

#### The summer of 1883 and the "Eroica" label

Brahms composed the Third Symphony in the summer of 1883 at Wiesbaden, a German spa town, in his characteristic working-holiday rhythm. He was fifty, at the height of his powers, and the symphony came relatively quickly. It premiered in Vienna on December 2, 1883, under Hans Richter, the great Wagnerian conductor who was nonetheless a devoted Brahmsian, and Richter, in proposing a toast, called it Brahms's "Eroica," after Beethoven's heroic Third Symphony. The label stuck, but it has caused confusion ever since, because the Third is not a heroic, triumphant work; it is concentrated, autumnal, and ends in quiet. Richter perhaps meant only that it was Brahms's third symphony and a noble one, or he was reaching for a grand compliment; either way, the "Eroica" tag misleads more than it illuminates. The premiere itself was complicated by the musical politics of the day: Vienna's Wagnerians, resenting Brahms as the figurehead of the opposing camp (Chapter 21), reportedly tried to disrupt it, but the work triumphed with the public. It quickly became one of Brahms's most admired symphonies, prized precisely for the concentration and the haunting quiet close that the "Eroica" label fails to capture.

#### Recording history briefly

The Third Symphony, like its siblings, has a deep discography. The warm, weighty Karajan and the lucid Haitink (our primary candidates) are joined by the intensely dramatic Furtwängler and the period-instrument Gardiner. The work's special interpretive challenge is the ending: a conductor must pace the finale's storm and subsidence so that the quiet close feels like earned repose rather than mere fading-out, and comparing how different conductors manage that subsidence is unusually rewarding. The fuller Brahms-symphony lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Third Symphony connects across the volume.

To the Second Symphony (Chapter 9). The Second grows its first movement from a three-note cell; the Third grows a whole symphony from the F-A♭-F motto, extending the developing-variation principle from a movement to an entire work. The two symphonies together teach the technique at two scales.

To the late music (Chapters 15, 17). The Third's autumnal character and its refusal of triumph, ending in quiet acceptance rather than blazing victory, anticipate the valedictory tone of Brahms's late clarinet works and late piano pieces. The fifty-year-old of the Third is on the road to the reconciled, inward old master.

To the synthesis (Chapter 20). The F-A♭-F motto is a central example of developing variation organizing a whole structure, and Chapter 20 returns to it alongside the Second's three-note cell as the clearest cases of the method Schoenberg called "progressive."

To the Wagner-Brahms wars (Chapter 21). The Third's premiere, disrupted by Vienna's Wagnerians, is a small episode in the larger culture war that Chapter 21 takes up, the war that made Brahms the figurehead of the classicist camp against the Wagner-Liszt party.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Third Symphony is built throughout on a three-note motto, F-A♭-F, traditionally linked to Brahms's personal motive 'free but happy,' though the book flags that the connection is partly a later reading and that the flattened note actually pulls the music toward shadow. Listen for the three motto notes opening the work and threading through it. Does it matter whether the 'free but happy' story is literally true, given that the motto unmistakably organizes the music? What is the difference between hearing the cell and believing the biographical tale?"_

* _"Alone of the four symphonies, the Third ends not in triumph but in a hushed, golden calm, the storm of the finale subsiding into peace rather than resolving into a blazing victory. Listen to that quiet ending (around 6:00 of the finale). Why might Brahms have refused the triumphant close that his first two symphonies, and Beethoven's model, seemed to demand? Is acceptance a deeper ending than victory?"_

* _"The motto's flattened note keeps the symphony shimmering between major and minor, brightness and shadow, so that it is never simply happy but always happy-and-shadowed. The chapter argues this is more truthful than unclouded contentment. As you listen, do you find the major-minor ambiguity more moving than pure brightness would be? Why might a fifty-year-old composer hear freedom and happiness as things that are real but never simple?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a recording from §10.1. Hear the symphony whole, then focus on these.

  1. **The F-A♭-F motto (0:00).** Fix the three motto notes, with the telltale flattened middle, in your ear from the opening chords. Then listen for the motto threading through all four movements, as bass, accompaniment, and melodic bones. This is the clearest motivic unification in the four symphonies.

  2. **The major-minor shimmer (first movement).** Listen for the constant shift between brightness and shadow, the A-flat pulling the major toward minor again and again. This ambiguity is the symphony's harmonic signature and the source of its autumnal feeling.

  3. **The Poco allegretto melody and its horn return (third movement, opening and about 3:00).** Listen to the famous melancholy cello melody, then to its return sung by the solo horn over the rocking accompaniment. This is the emotional peak of the symphony and one of the supreme moments in Brahms.

  4. **The quiet ending (finale, about 6:00).** Listen for the storm subsiding into hushed, golden calm, the motto returning transformed, the symphony sinking to peaceful rest in the major. This refusal of triumph is the work's deepest and most unusual stroke.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the late music ahead): the Op. 118 No. 2 Intermezzo for piano (Chapter 17), to hear the same autumnal, reconciled, quietly shadowed tone that the Third Symphony's ending introduces, now in the intimate world of the late piano pieces. The symphony's quiet close and the late intermezzos share a single mood: acceptance rather than triumph.

**Listen** (the popular afterlife): the Poco allegretto melody has been borrowed many times in films and popular music (most famously in a mid-twentieth-century French film and the song derived from it). Hearing how the tune has traveled into popular culture, like the Wiegenlied, shows how Brahms's melodic gift reaches far beyond the concert hall.

**Read** : Walter Frisch's _Brahms: The Four Symphonies_ (1996), the chapter on the Third, especially on the motto's pervasive role and the unusual quiet ending, for a precise account of how three notes unify a whole symphony.

**Read** (the analyst): Tovey's essay on the Third Symphony in his _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , fine on the work's concentration and on the magical close, by the analyst whose hearing guides this book's orchestral discussions.

## Chapter 11: Symphony No. 4 in E minor, Op. 98 (1884-1885)

You have met this symphony before. In _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , Chapter 18 put the finale of Brahms's Fourth in front of you and asked you to listen closely to one of the strangest and grandest things in the orchestral repertoire: a movement built as a passacaglia, thirty variations over an eight-bar bass line that repeats, unbroken, from the first bar to the last, a Baroque form resurrected for a tragic Romantic climax. That finale was the doorway through which you entered the whole _Close Listening_ series' acquaintance with Brahms. Now, at the end of the symphonies, we return to it, go deeper into it, and place it where it belongs: as the crowning movement of Brahms's last and greatest symphony, the convergence of everything this book has been teaching (developing variation, the variation forms, the Bach inheritance, and the tragic seriousness) into a single overwhelming structure.

This is the most anchor-heavy chapter of the four symphonies, and the Fourth deserves it, because it is, by wide agreement, Brahms's symphonic summit and one of the supreme tragic works of the orchestra. We will give all four movements their due, the falling-thirds melancholy of the first movement, the archaic mystery of the slow movement, the one true scherzo Brahms wrote in a symphony, and above all the passacaglia finale, but the finale is the goal, the place where the whole volume's themes come together. The Fourth is also the work that holds, most completely, the two great readings of Brahms in tension: the "archaicist," who looks backward to Bach and the Baroque, and the "developing-variation modernist," who looks forward to the twentieth century. In the passacaglia finale, the backward and forward gazes are the same gesture, which is the deepest thing this book has to show about Brahms, and the reason the Fourth is its symphonic climax.

### 11.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

When Brahms played the Fourth Symphony through on the piano for a small circle of friends before its premiere, the reception was, by several accounts, cool, even troubled. The work seemed austere, severe, difficult; one influential listener reportedly compared the experience to being beaten by two very witty but very tough people. Brahms's friends worried that the public would find it forbidding. Brahms himself, characteristically, made gloomy jokes about it. The Fourth is the most uncompromising of the symphonies, the one that makes the fewest concessions to easy charm, and its first hearers felt that.

And there is the matter of the finale. For his last symphonic movement, at the climax of his last symphony, Brahms chose a passacaglia, a strict Baroque variation form, with an eight-bar theme he derived from the bass line of a Bach cantata, "Nach dir, Herr, verlanget mich" (BWV 150). To end a Romantic symphony not with a triumphant sonata-form finale but with thirty variations over a repeating bass, in a form two hundred years old, was an extraordinary, almost defiant choice, the supreme statement of Brahms the lover of old forms. To his critics in the Wagner camp it confirmed everything: Brahms the backward-looking academic, dressing his symphony in antique costume. To his admirers, and to posterity, it was something else entirely: proof that the oldest, strictest form could carry the most powerful and modern of utterances, that looking backward to Bach and looking forward to the music of the future could be one and the same act. The Fourth Symphony's finale is the hill on which the whole argument about Brahms, conservative or progressive, is fought, and the answer it gives is "both, at once." That is the drama you are about to hear.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The falling thirds of the opening, and the autumnal tragedy.** The symphony opens with a melody that seems to sigh downward in a chain of falling intervals (descending thirds), tender and melancholy and immediately distinctive. Listen to this opening; it sets the whole work's tone of autumnal, tragic lyricism. The Fourth is in E minor, and it is the most consistently serious and tragic of the four symphonies, but its tragedy is lyrical, song-filled, not grim.

  2. **The one true scherzo.** The third movement is the only genuine, full-blooded scherzo Brahms wrote in a symphony (the First, Second, and Third all have gentler intermezzo-like third movements). Listen for its boisterous, almost rowdy energy, a blazing C-major outburst, with triangle and piccolo, completely unlike the lyrical movements around it. It is a moment of fierce, even brutal joy before the tragic finale.

  3. **The passacaglia finale: the repeating bass.** This is the goal. Listen for the eight-bar theme stated at the very start of the finale by the winds and brass, a stark, rising-then-turning melody, and then listen for how it repeats, again and again, thirty times, while the music above and around it transforms completely, growing from quiet to overwhelming and back. The bass line never stops; everything else changes. By the end you should hear both the unchanging foundation and the immense variety built upon it, the passacaglia principle, which you met in the bright in the Haydn Variations (Chapter 7), now carrying tragedy.

#### One-sentence background

Symphony No. 4 in E minor, Op. 98, is a roughly forty-minute work in four movements composed in the summers of 1884 and 1885 at Mürzzuschlag in the Austrian Alps, Brahms's last symphony and his symphonic summit, the most consistently tragic and uncompromising of the four, opening with a chain of falling thirds and culminating in a finale cast as a passacaglia, thirty variations over an eight-bar ground bass derived from the bass of Bach's Cantata BWV 150, a Baroque form resurrected for a tragic Romantic climax; it premiered at Meiningen on October 25, 1885, conducted by Brahms, with the Vienna premiere under Hans Richter on January 17, 1886; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Carlos Kleiber with the Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), one of the most celebrated of all symphony recordings, chosen for its drive, clarity, and command of the finale's architecture.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Symphony No. 4 Carlos Kleiber Vienna" or "Brahms Symphony 4 E minor Op. 98 passacaglia" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 第四交响曲 卡洛斯·克莱伯 维也纳" 或 "勃拉姆斯 e小调第四交响曲 作品98" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Wilhelm Furtwängler (historic, Berlin Philharmonic), overwhelmingly dramatic and tragic, the great old-tradition reading; the sound is older but the interpretation is monumental.
* Herbert von Karajan with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), weighty and grand.
* Carlos Kleiber with the Bavarian State Orchestra (live, alternative to the Vienna studio account), even fiercer.
* John Eliot Gardiner (period instruments), clarifying the textures, especially the finale's counterpoint.

Hear the symphony whole; the tragic arc only works in sequence, and the finale's full effect depends on the three movements before it. If you met the finale alone in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ , this is the time to hear it in its place, after the falling-thirds first movement, the archaic slow movement, and the blazing scherzo, so that you feel why the passacaglia is the necessary tragic culmination of everything before it.

### 11.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps refer to a roughly forty-minute reading (Kleiber); they will shift with tempo. We give each movement its due, with the deepest attention to the finale.

**First movement (Allegro non troppo).**

_0:00. The falling thirds._ The symphony opens immediately, with no introduction, on the main theme: a tender, melancholy melody in the violins that seems to sigh downward in a chain of falling intervals, each phrase drooping and then lifting. Listen to this descending shape; it is the seed of the movement and the source of its autumnal sadness. (The whole movement, in fact, grows from this falling-thirds idea by developing variation, a tragic counterpart to the Second Symphony's three-note cell.)

_About 2:00. The lyrical and the dramatic._ The movement unfolds with great lyrical warmth shadowed by tragic intensity, the falling-thirds material transformed continuously, a noble second theme in the cellos and horns, surges of passion and grief. Listen for the song-filled tragedy, sorrow that sings rather than rages.

_About 9:00. The coda._ The movement drives to a powerful, tragic close, the falling-thirds theme returning transformed, the music gathering to a stern E-minor end. Listen for how the lyrical sadness of the opening has hardened into tragic force.

**Second movement (Andante moderato).** A slow movement of archaic, mysterious beauty. It opens with a bare horn call in an old church mode (the Phrygian mode, which gives the music its antique, modal color, neither quite major nor minor), and unfolds long, grave, song-filled melodies. Listen for the archaic quality, the sense of looking back to old music, and for the deep lyrical warmth of the great cello-and-string melody at its heart. This is one of Brahms's most beautiful and most backward-gazing slow movements, and its modal color prepares the Bach-derived finale.

**Third movement (Allegro giocoso).** The one true scherzo in Brahms's symphonies, and a shock after the autumnal movements around it: a boisterous, driving, almost violent C-major romp, full of energy and even crude high spirits, with triangle and piccolo adding brilliance. Listen for the fierce, rowdy joy, completely unlike the lyrical tragedy elsewhere. Some hear it as grim, even desperate gaiety, a forced festivity before the tragic finale; others simply as raw energy. Either way, it is a blazing contrast, the necessary burst of light and force before the darkness of the passacaglia.

**Fourth movement (Allegro energico e passionato), the passacaglia.**

_0:00. The eight-bar theme._ The finale opens with the passacaglia theme stated starkly by the winds and brass: an eight-bar phrase that rises stepwise and then turns, grave and implacable. Fix this theme in your ear; it is the ground bass, the foundation that will repeat thirty times, unchanged in essence, through the entire movement. (Brahms derived it from the bass line of a Bach cantata, BWV 150, "Nach dir, Herr, verlanget mich", a connection the analyst Donald Tovey confirmed as deliberate, fitting the slow movement's backward gaze toward old music.)

_The variations (roughly 0:30 onward)._ Now listen for the heart of the form: the eight-bar theme repeats, again and again, while everything above and around it transforms. Each repetition is a variation, the bass constant, the music upon it utterly changed: first stark and forceful, then quieter, then a famous lyrical interlude. Listen especially for:

_About 4:00. The flute solo and the lyrical center._ Around the middle of the movement, the relentless tread relaxes, the tempo eases, and a long, grieving flute solo unfolds over the slowed bass, followed by a solemn, chorale-like passage for the trombones in a hushed major. This is the still center of the finale, the eye of the storm, lyrical and inward, before the tragic drive resumes. Listen for the flute's lament and the trombone chorale; they are among the most beautiful moments in all Brahms.

_About 7:00. The return and the intensification._ The opening tread returns, and the variations resume their accumulation, building in power and tragic intensity, the constant bass driving the music toward catastrophe rather than triumph. Listen for the gathering force, the variations piling up, the relentless eight-bar foundation pushing everything forward.

_About 9:00. The coda._ The movement drives to its overwhelming close, the passacaglia theme blazing out, the music ending not in triumph but in tragic finality, a stern, crushing E-minor conclusion. Unlike the First and Second symphonies, the Fourth ends in tragedy, the only one of the four to do so. Listen for the tragic weight of the ending; this is darkness not transcended but accepted, faced, made monumental.

The whole symphony, and this whole part of the book, culminates here: the constant bass, the thirty transformations, the lyrical center, the tragic close. If you carry away one thing, let it be the sound of the unchanging eight-bar foundation under the endlessly changing music, the passacaglia principle at its tragic summit.

### 11.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The passacaglia: an old form for a tragic climax

The Fourth's finale is the most important single structure in this volume, because it brings together everything the book has taught. A passacaglia, as Chapter 7 introduced through the Haydn Variations finale, is a Baroque form built over a ground bass: a short bass line that repeats continuously, unchanged, while the music above it varies and grows. It is one of the oldest and strictest variation forms, the form of Bach's great organ passacaglia and violin chaconne (the Bach volume). For Brahms to choose it for the finale of his last symphony, at the climax of his symphonic career, was a profound statement. It declared his allegiance to the old masters and the old forms; it demonstrated that the strictest, most "academic" of structures could carry the most powerful and tragic of expressions; and it crowned his lifelong devotion to variation with the grandest variation movement he ever wrote. Thirty variations over an eight-bar bass, ranging from stark force to lyrical lament to tragic catastrophe, all over a foundation that never changes: this is the variation principle, which you first heard varying the two verses of a lullaby, raised to its ultimate power. The finale of the Fourth is where developing variation, the variation forms, and the Bach inheritance converge, and it is the reason this chapter is the climax of the symphonies.

#### The backward and forward gazes as one gesture

The Fourth holds, more completely than any other work, the two great readings of Brahms in tension, and shows them to be the same thing. To one way of hearing, the Fourth is the most backward-looking of the symphonies: the archaic modal slow movement, the Bach-derived passacaglia finale, the whole reverence for old forms, the "archaicist" Brahms, the man the Wagner camp dismissed as a museum-keeper. To another way of hearing, the Fourth is the most forward-looking: the relentless developing variation, the way the first movement grows entirely from a falling-thirds cell, the rigorous motivic construction that Schoenberg (Chapter 20) would claim as the ancestor of twentieth-century music, the "progressive" Brahms. The genius of the Fourth is that these are not two different things but one. The passacaglia finale is simultaneously the most archaic gesture (a two-hundred-year-old Baroque form) and the most modern (continuous variation as the engine of structure). Looking backward to Bach and forward to Schoenberg turns out to be a single act, because the old variation forms _are_ developing variation in its purest, strictest shape. The Fourth Symphony resolves the "conservative or progressive" argument by being completely both, and that resolution is the deepest insight this book offers about Brahms. Chapter 20 lays out the full argument; the Fourth is where you hear its answer.

#### Tragedy that sings

A word about the Fourth's emotional character, which is unique among the symphonies. The First struggles toward triumph; the Second basks in shadowed sunlight; the Third reaches autumnal acceptance; the Fourth alone is tragic to the end, the only one that closes in the minor, in defeat rather than victory. But notice that its tragedy is not grim or harsh; it is lyrical, song-filled, beautiful. The falling-thirds opening is tender even as it is sad; the slow movement is gravely beautiful; even the passacaglia finale has, at its center, the heartbreaking flute lament and the consoling trombone chorale. This is tragedy that sings, sorrow made into great melody, grief that is faced with full lyrical beauty rather than mere bleakness. It is one of the supreme examples in music of the tragic made beautiful, and it shows the autumnal Brahms of the 1880s, who had reached, in the Third Symphony, a kind of acceptance, now facing tragedy itself with open eyes and an unbroken lyric voice. The Fourth does not console as the Requiem does, and it does not triumph as the First does; it accepts tragedy, and makes of it the most beautiful and monumental of his symphonies.

### 11.4 The Stories

#### The summers at Mürzzuschlag and the cool first reception

Brahms composed the Fourth in the summers of 1884 and 1885 at Mürzzuschlag, a town in the Austrian Alps, in his usual working-holiday rhythm. When he previewed the work for his circle, the reception was wary; the symphony struck his friends as austere and forbidding, and they feared the public would not take to it. Brahms made his customary gloomy jokes. But the premiere, which Brahms himself conducted on October 25, 1885, at Meiningen, was a success, helped by the superb Meiningen court orchestra (a small but excellent ensemble under the patronage of Duke George II, which Brahms used as a kind of laboratory). The Vienna premiere followed on January 17, 1886, under Hans Richter. The Fourth's reputation grew steadily until it came to be regarded, as it is today, as Brahms's symphonic masterpiece. There is a poignant coda to its story: at one of the last concerts Brahms ever attended, in March 1897, weeks before his death, the Vienna Philharmonic played the Fourth, and the audience, knowing he was dying, gave him an overwhelming ovation after every movement, a public farewell to the composer through his greatest symphony. Brahms, mortally ill, stood in his box, weeping. The Fourth was, in a sense, the music the city said goodbye to him with.

#### Recording history briefly

The Fourth has one of the great discographies in the symphonic repertoire. Carlos Kleiber's Vienna Philharmonic recording (our primary) is among the most celebrated of all symphony recordings, prized for its drive and clarity; Furtwängler's historic accounts are overwhelming in their tragic weight; Karajan offers grandeur, Gardiner clarifying period textures. The finale is the great interpretive test: a conductor must hold the thirty variations together as a single inexorable structure while shaping the lyrical center and driving to the tragic close, and comparing how different conductors pace the passacaglia is one of the most rewarding listening projects in all of Brahms. The fuller lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later, and have heard before

The Fourth Symphony is the great convergence point of the volume.

To _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 18, where you first met the passacaglia finale. You now hear it in its place, as the tragic culmination of the whole symphony, and you understand the form (the ground bass, the thirty variations) and its lineage (the Haydn Variations, Bach) that _Close Listening: Classical Music_ could only gesture at.

To the Haydn Variations (Chapter 7). The bright passacaglia finale of the Haydn Variations was your training ground; the Fourth's finale is its tragic summit. Same form, opposite emotional pole, ground bass plus accumulating variations plus a return, turned from triumph to tragedy.

To Bach. The passacaglia theme is derived from a Bach cantata bass, and the form itself is Bach's. The Fourth's finale is the fullest flowering of the Bach inheritance that runs through Brahms's whole life, the Requiem fugues (Chapter 6), and on to the Bach-style chorale preludes of his very last work (Chapter 19).

To the synthesis (Chapter 20). The Fourth is the symphonic climax of developing variation and the variation forms, and Chapter 20 returns to it as the supreme case where the backward gaze (archaic forms) and the forward gaze (continuous variation) are revealed to be a single gesture, the heart of the "Brahms the Progressive" argument.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"For the finale of his last symphony, Brahms chose a passacaglia, thirty variations over an eight-bar bass line that repeats unbroken from start to finish, a strict Baroque form two hundred years old, and derived its theme from a Bach cantata. His critics called this backward-looking academicism; the book argues that looking back to Bach and forward to modern music are, in this movement, the same gesture. Listen to the repeating bass under the endlessly changing music. How can the oldest, strictest form also be the most forward-looking? What does it mean that Brahms's conservatism and his radicalism turn out to be one thing?"_

* _"Alone of the four symphonies, the Fourth ends in tragedy, in the minor, in defeat rather than triumph. Yet its tragedy is lyrical and beautiful, sorrow made into great melody rather than grim bleakness. Listen to the falling-thirds opening and the grieving flute solo at the center of the finale. Why might 'tragedy that sings' be more powerful than either triumph or mere despair? What does it mean to face tragedy with an unbroken lyric voice?"_

* _"At one of the last concerts Brahms attended, weeks before his death, the Vienna audience, knowing he was dying, gave him an overwhelming ovation after every movement of the Fourth, and the mortally ill composer stood weeping in his box. Listen to the whole symphony knowing it became, in effect, the music Vienna said goodbye to him with. Does knowing this change how the tragic finale sounds to you? How does a work's place at the end of a life color the way we hear it?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Kleiber recording (or an alternative from §11.1). Hear the symphony whole, then focus on these.

  1. **The falling-thirds opening (0:00).** Fix the descending, sighing melody of the first movement in your ear. This falling-thirds shape is the autumnal seed of the movement and the source of the work's lyrical tragedy.

  2. **The archaic slow movement (second movement).** Listen for the bare horn call in the old Phrygian church mode and the modal, antique color of the whole movement, the backward gaze toward old music that prepares the Bach-derived finale.

  3. **The eight-bar passacaglia theme and the repeating bass (finale, 0:00 onward).** Fix the stark eight-bar theme at the start of the finale, then listen for it repeating thirty times, unchanged, while everything above it transforms. This constant foundation under endlessly varied music is the heart of the form.

  4. **The lyrical center of the finale (about 4:00).** Listen for the grieving flute solo and the solemn trombone chorale at the eye of the storm, where the relentless tread relaxes. These are among the most beautiful moments in Brahms, and they make the tragic finale sing.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the form's bright twin): the finale of the Haydn Variations (Chapter 7), the passacaglia in the major, in triumph. Hearing it again right after the Fourth's tragic passacaglia shows the same form serving opposite emotional ends, the clearest possible lesson in what a ground-bass structure can carry.

**Listen** (the Bach root): Bach's great organ Passacaglia in C minor and the Chaconne from his D-minor violin Partita (the Bach volume), the supreme Baroque ground-bass works Brahms revered. Hearing them shows the tradition the Fourth's finale crowns, and makes the kinship between Brahms and Bach fully audible.

**Read** : Walter Frisch's _Brahms: The Four Symphonies_ (1996), the chapter on the Fourth, especially on the passacaglia finale and on how the first movement grows from the falling-thirds cell, for a precise account of the work's construction.

**Read** (the analyst): Tovey's essay on the Fourth Symphony in his _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , which confirms the Bach-cantata source of the passacaglia theme and walks lucidly through all four movements, the warm, authoritative hearing of the analyst whose voice guides this book's symphonic discussions.
