## Chapter 12: The Two Piano Concertos — Op. 15 (1858) and Op. 83 (1881)

Brahms was a formidable pianist, and the piano was his home instrument, the one through which he first conquered Schumann and first earned his living. So it is natural that two of his greatest works are concertos for the piano, one from each end of his maturity: the First, in D minor, Op. 15, a tragic, symphonic giant born of the Schumann-asylum years and the young man's struggle to find his largest voice; and the Second, in B-flat major, Op. 83, a vast, expansive, four-movement masterpiece of his full maturity, written at the height of his powers after the symphonies. Together they frame his career, and together they show what Brahms did to the concerto: he refused the showy, soloist-versus-orchestra display piece that the Romantic age loved, and made instead the _symphonic concerto_ , in which the soloist is woven into the orchestral argument as a partner, not set against it as a rival.

This chapter anchors on two contrasting movements: the tragic, storm-tossed first movement of the D-minor Concerto, where the young Brahms pours out the grief and grandeur of the Schumann years; and the serene, song-filled Andante of the B-flat Concerto, with its famous cello solo, where the mature Brahms reaches a late-summer warmth. Between them lies the whole development of his concerto art, from struggling young tragedian to serene old master, and a clear lesson in what "symphonic concerto" means: listen for the piano as a member of the orchestra, not its adversary.

### 12.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The First Piano Concerto had a difficult birth and a worse reception. Brahms began the music in the mid-1850s, in the immediate aftermath of Robert Schumann's suicide attempt and committal, and the work carries the weight of those years; he first conceived some of the material as a symphony, then as a sonata for two pianos, before it found its final form as a concerto, the same restless search for the right medium you saw in the Piano Quintet (Chapter 5). When it was finally finished and performed, in 1859, with Brahms himself as soloist, the audience in Leipzig received it with open hostility, hisses, near-silence, a humiliating failure for the twenty-five-year-old. The concerto was too dark, too serious, too symphonic, too little like the brilliant display-concerto the public expected; it gave the soloist grandeur and tragedy instead of dazzle, and the public did not know what to do with it.

Brahms, writing to Joachim afterward, was wounded but defiant, taking the failure as a sign that the work was ahead of its time, and predicting it would eventually be understood. He was right. The D-minor Concerto is now recognized as one of the great Romantic concertos, precisely for the qualities that doomed its premiere: its symphonic weight, its tragic depth, its refusal of empty display. The story is a model of Brahms's whole relationship to his public, the serious artist who would not pander, who trusted the work over the applause, and who was vindicated by time. Twenty-three years later, the Second Concerto would be received with the acclaim the First had been denied, proof of how far both Brahms and his audience had traveled.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

**For the D-minor Concerto, Op. 15 (first movement):**

  1. **The tragic, symphonic opening.** The concerto begins not with the soloist but with a long, stormy orchestral introduction: a thunderous, tragic outburst in D minor, with trilling, lashing figures and grinding tension, music of enormous gravity. Listen to how symphonic this is; the orchestra states a whole world of tragedy before the piano even enters. This is the young Brahms of the Schumann years pouring out grief and grandeur.

  2. **The piano as partner, not showman.** When the piano enters, listen for how it joins the argument rather than showing off. It does not dazzle; it deepens, adding its own grave, lyrical, sometimes consoling voice to the orchestra's tragedy. The piano and orchestra are partners in a symphonic drama, not rivals in a display contest. This is the "symphonic concerto" principle.

**For the B-flat Concerto, Op. 83 (Andante):**

  3. **The cello solo.** The slow movement of the B-flat Concerto opens with one of the most beautiful melodies Brahms wrote, sung not by the piano soloist but by a solo cello from within the orchestra, a long, warm, songful tune. Listen for this cello solo and for the way the piano, when it enters, decorates and supports rather than dominates. It is the symphonic-concerto ideal at its most beautiful: the "soloist" yielding the great melody to an orchestral player.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on two works: the **Piano Concerto No. 1 in D minor, Op. 15 (1854-1858)** , a roughly fifty-minute, tragic, symphonically scaled concerto born of the Schumann-asylum years, which failed at its 1859 premiere but is now recognized as a great Romantic concerto; and the **Piano Concerto No. 2 in B-flat major, Op. 83 (1881)** , a vast four-movement concerto of Brahms's full maturity (with an added scherzo as its second movement and a famous cello solo in its Andante), premiered in Budapest on November 9, 1881, with Brahms as soloist; both embody Brahms's "symphonic concerto," in which the piano is woven into the orchestra rather than set against it; the **Primary Recordings** are, for both concertos, Krystian Zimerman with Leonard Bernstein and the Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), chosen for grandeur, partnership, and beauty of tone.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Piano Concerto No. 1 Zimerman Bernstein"; "Brahms Piano Concerto No. 2 Zimerman Bernstein" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 第一钢琴协奏曲 齐默尔曼 伯恩斯坦"; "勃拉姆斯 第二钢琴协奏曲 作品83" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* D-minor Concerto: Clifford Curzon with George Szell and the London Symphony, Decca 1962, a classic of grandeur and gravity; Emil Gilels with Eugen Jochum (Deutsche Grammophon).
* B-flat Concerto: Emil Gilels with Eugen Jochum and the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon), long a reference; Maurizio Pollini with various conductors; Sviatoslav Richter.

These are two large works; take them on separate listens. The D-minor's first movement (about twenty minutes) and the B-flat's Andante (about twelve minutes) are the anchors; hear those closely, and hear each concerto whole at least once to feel the symphonic scale.

### 12.2 The Listening Window

#### Piano Concerto No. 1 in D minor, Op. 15 (first movement)

Timestamps refer to the Zimerman / Bernstein recording; they will shift with tempo.

**0:00. The tragic orchestral introduction.** The concerto opens with a long, stormy orchestral tutti: a thunderous, tragic theme in D minor, with lashing trills and grinding dissonance, music of huge gravity and grief. Listen to the symphonic weight; the orchestra states the whole tragic world before the soloist appears. This introduction is one of the most powerful in any concerto.

**About 3:30. The piano enters.** The piano comes in not with a flourish but with a quiet, grave, almost prayerful new theme, a moment of inwardness after the orchestral storm. Listen for how the soloist deepens rather than dazzles, entering as a contemplative voice within the tragedy, not a rival to it.

**About 7:00. The lyrical second theme.** A warmer, more consoling melody provides contrast to the tragic first material. The movement, in large sonata form, sets the storm against this lyricism and develops both, piano and orchestra woven together throughout. Listen for the partnership: the piano takes up orchestral ideas, the orchestra answers the piano, the two are a single symphonic argument.

**About 14:00 onward. Development and close.** The movement builds through tragic development to a powerful close, the storm of the opening returning transformed. Listen across the whole movement for its symphonic scale and tragic seriousness, the qualities that baffled its first audience and that we now hear as its greatness.

#### Piano Concerto No. 2 in B-flat major, Op. 83 (Andante)

This is the third movement of the four-movement concerto; timestamps are within the movement.

**0:00. The cello solo.** The Andante opens with a long, warm, songful melody sung by a solo cello from within the orchestra, one of Brahms's most beautiful tunes. Listen to it whole before the piano enters; the great melody belongs, remarkably, to an orchestral player, not the soloist. (Brahms loved this melody so much he later reused its mood in a song.)

**About 2:30. The piano enters, decorating.** When the piano comes in, listen for how it supports and decorates the cello's melody rather than seizing it, weaving filigree around the song, deepening the warmth. This is the symphonic-concerto ideal at its tenderest: the soloist in service of the orchestra's beauty.

**About 5:00. The deeper, stranger middle.** The movement moves to a more searching, harmonically richer central section (with delicate exchanges between piano and clarinets), then returns to the cello melody. Listen for the warmth, the inwardness, the late-summer glow of this music; it is the mature Brahms at his most serene.

**About 10:00. The close.** The cello melody returns to close the movement in peace, the piano supporting to the end. Listen for the sense of repose and warmth, a complete contrast to the D-minor Concerto's tragic storm, the same composer twenty-three years on, having traveled from struggling tragedian to serene master.

### 12.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The symphonic concerto

The central thing both concertos teach is what Brahms did to the concerto form. The Romantic age loved the _virtuoso concerto_ , the soloist as hero, dazzling the orchestra and the audience with display, the orchestra reduced to accompaniment, the piece a vehicle for spectacular technique. Brahms refused this. He made the _symphonic concerto_ , in which the soloist is a partner woven into a symphonic argument, the orchestra a full equal (sometimes more than equal), and the music a serious structure rather than a display vehicle. This is why the D-minor Concerto opens with a vast orchestral tragedy before the piano enters, and why the B-flat Concerto gives its most beautiful melody to a solo cello rather than the pianist. The piano in both works is fearsomely difficult to play, more so than most display concertos, but the difficulty is in the service of the music, not of showing off; the soloist works as hard as anyone in the orchestra and is heard as part of the whole. This symphonic conception is one of Brahms's great contributions to the form, and it is why his concertos feel like symphonies with piano rather than showpieces with orchestra. Listen, in both, for partnership rather than rivalry; that is the key to hearing Brahms's concertos rightly.

#### Two ends of a career

The two concertos frame Brahms's maturity and show how far he traveled. The D-minor is a young man's work, born of crisis: composed in the late 1850s in the shadow of the Schumann tragedy, it is tragic, stormy, struggling, the music of grief and grandeur, and it failed precisely because it was too serious for its first audience. The B-flat is a mature master's work: composed in 1881, after all the symphonies but the Fourth, it is vast, expansive, confident, serene, the music of a man at the height of his powers and at peace with his public, which now received it with acclaim. Hearing the two together is hearing the arc of a life: the same composer, the same instrument, the same symphonic-concerto principle, but the storm of youth become the warmth of maturity, the tragic D minor become the genial B-flat. The B-flat even adds a fourth movement, an extra scherzo as its second movement, expanding the concerto beyond its usual three movements toward the scale of a symphony, a sign of the mature Brahms's expansive confidence. The two concertos are the piano-and-orchestra chapters of the same story the symphonies tell: a serious artist growing from struggle toward serenity.

#### The cello solo and the song-instrumental connection

The cello solo that opens the B-flat Concerto's Andante deserves special note, because it shows the Brahms who is always, at heart, a melodist and song-composer. The melody is so songful that Brahms later drew on its mood for one of his Lieder ("Immer leiser wird mein Schlummer"), and giving it to a solo cello, the most voice-like of instruments, rather than the piano, makes the song-quality explicit: this is a wordless aria, sung from within the orchestra. The moment connects to a thread that runs through Brahms's chamber and orchestral music, the constant presence of song, of singable, breath-shaped melody, even in his most ambitious instrumental structures (you heard it in the symphonies; you will hear it in the violin sonatas of Chapter 14, which quote his actual songs). The composer of two hundred Lieder never stopped being a song-writer, and the cello solo in the B-flat Concerto is one of the most beautiful instances of his song-instinct singing through an orchestral work.

### 12.4 The Stories

#### The D-minor Concerto and the Schumann years

The First Piano Concerto is bound up with the most fraught period of Brahms's youth, the Schumann tragedy of 1854-1856 (Chapter 4). Brahms began the music in those years, and some of it may have been conceived first as a symphony memorializing or responding to the catastrophe, before becoming the two-piano sonata and finally the concerto. The tragic weight of the first movement, the grief and grandeur, is widely felt to carry the emotion of those years, the young man's response to his mentor's collapse and his own overwhelming situation in the Schumann household. Whether or not one reads specific biography into specific bars (the book counsels caution about such readings), the concerto unmistakably belongs to the emotional world of Chapter 4: it is the largest, most tragic statement of the young Brahms, born in the crucible of the Schumann disaster, and its hostile 1859 reception was a hard early lesson in the cost of refusing to pander. The slow movement, gentle and prayerful, Brahms once associated with a "gentle portrait" of Clara, another link to those years, though here too the connection is more suggestive than documented.

#### The B-flat Concerto and the mature master

The Second Concerto, composed in 1881, comes from a wholly different place in Brahms's life: secure, celebrated, at the height of his powers, after the first three symphonies. Brahms premiered it himself in Budapest on November 9, 1881, and, characteristically self-deprecating, described it to friends as a "tiny, tiny piano concerto with a tiny, tiny wisp of a scherzo," when it is in fact one of the largest and grandest concertos ever written, four movements, nearly fifty minutes, of symphonic scope. The joke (like the Second Symphony's "black border") is pure Brahms, puncturing the grandeur of his own achievement. The concerto was a success, and it has remained one of the pinnacles of the repertoire, beloved especially for its expansive first movement (opening with a horn call answered by the piano), its powerful scherzo, the serene cello-led Andante, and its genial, dancing finale. It is the mature Brahms's great gift to the piano, and the serene counterpart to the tragic concerto of his youth.

#### Recording history briefly

Both concertos are central repertoire with rich discographies. The Zimerman / Bernstein recordings (our primary) are prized for their grandeur and partnership; the classic Curzon / Szell D-minor and Gilels / Jochum readings of both concertos are touchstones, and many great pianists (Pollini, Richter, Serkin, Brendel, Kovacevich) have recorded distinguished accounts. Because these are symphonic concertos, the conductor and orchestra matter as much as the soloist, so comparing recordings means comparing partnerships, how the pianist and conductor balance and answer each other, which is itself a lesson in the symphonic-concerto ideal. The fuller concerto lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects across the volume.

To the Violin Concerto and Double Concerto (Chapter 13). The next chapter takes up Brahms's other concertos, written for his friend Joachim, and the symphonic-concerto principle, the soloist woven into the orchestra, governs them too. The Violin Concerto is the symphonic concerto applied to the violin; the Double Concerto carries the principle to its limit with two soloists.

To the symphonies (Chapters 8-11). The concertos are, in effect, symphonies with a solo instrument, and they share the symphonies' developing-variation construction, their seriousness, and (in the case of the D-minor and the Fourth) their tragic power. Hearing the symphonies first prepares you to hear the concertos as symphonic.

To the Lieder (Chapter 16). The cello solo of the B-flat Concerto's Andante, later echoed in a Brahms song, is a vivid instance of the song-instinct that runs through all of Brahms's instrumental music and is the explicit subject of Chapter 16.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Brahms's First Piano Concerto failed disastrously at its 1859 premiere, hissed by an audience that expected a brilliant display piece and got a tragic, symphonic giant instead. Brahms predicted it would be understood in time, and it was. Listen to the tragic orchestral opening and the piano's grave, undazzling entrance. What does it cost an artist to refuse to give the public what it wants? When is trusting the work over the applause wisdom, and when might it be mere stubbornness?"_

* _"Brahms made the 'symphonic concerto,' in which the soloist is woven into the orchestra as a partner rather than set against it as a showman, most strikingly by giving the B-flat Concerto's most beautiful melody to a solo cello rather than to the star pianist. Listen to that cello solo. Why might a composer hand the great tune to an orchestral player instead of the soloist? What does it change about the meaning of a concerto when the soloist serves the music rather than dominating it?"_

* _"The two piano concertos frame Brahms's career, the tragic, storm-tossed D minor of his youth and the serene, expansive B-flat of his maturity, the same composer twenty-three years apart. Listen to the D-minor's tragic first movement and the B-flat's serene Andante back to back. What do you hear of the journey from struggling young tragedian to reconciled old master? Does the serenity of the later work sound like peace earned through the storms of the earlier one?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Zimerman / Bernstein recordings (or alternatives from §12.1).

  1. **The tragic orchestral introduction of the D-minor Concerto (0:00).** Fix in your ear the thunderous, tragic orchestral storm that opens the concerto before the piano enters. This symphonic weight is what baffled its first audience and what we now hear as its greatness.

  2. **The piano's undazzling entrance (D-minor, about 3:30).** Listen for how the piano enters quietly and gravely, deepening the tragedy rather than showing off. This is the symphonic-concerto principle: partner, not showman.

  3. **The cello solo (B-flat, Andante, 0:00).** Listen to the long, warm cello melody that opens the slow movement, the great tune given to an orchestral player, and to the piano's decorating support when it enters. This is the symphonic-concerto ideal at its most beautiful.

  4. **The two concertos' contrast (across both).** Hold the tragic D minor and the serene B-flat in your mind together, and hear the arc of a career, the storm of youth become the warmth of maturity, in the same composer's two concertos for his home instrument.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the virtuoso concerto Brahms refused): a movement of a brilliant Romantic display concerto (Liszt's First Piano Concerto, or Tchaikovsky's First), to hear the soloist-as-hero tradition that Brahms deliberately rejected. The contrast makes vivid what "symphonic concerto" means: hear how differently the soloist relates to the orchestra in a display piece versus in Brahms.

**Listen** (the song echo): Brahms's song "Immer leiser wird mein Schlummer," whose mood Brahms drew on for the B-flat Concerto's cello-led Andante. Hearing the song shows the constant presence of Brahms the melodist inside Brahms the symphonic-concerto composer.

**Read** : Michael Musgrave's _The Music of Brahms_ (1985), the discussion of the concertos, for a clear account of the symphonic-concerto conception and the contrast between the two piano concertos.

**Read** (the biography): Jan Swafford's _Johannes Brahms_ (1997), on the D-minor Concerto's difficult genesis in the Schumann years and its failed premiere, and on the triumphant maturity behind the B-flat, for the human story framing both works.

## Chapter 13: Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 77 (1878) and Double Concerto in A minor, Op. 102 (1887)

Behind both works in this chapter stands one man: Joseph Joachim, the great violinist who, twenty-five years earlier, had heard the unknown Brahms on tour and sent him to the Schumanns (Chapter 3). Joachim was Brahms's oldest and closest musical friend, his adviser, his champion, the dedicatee of the Violin Concerto and the inspiration for the Double Concerto. The two concertos in this chapter are, among other things, the record of that lifelong friendship, its depth, a painful rupture, and a moving reconciliation. The Violin Concerto, Op. 77, written for Joachim in 1878, is one of the supreme concertos for the instrument, the symphonic-concerto principle of Chapter 12 applied to the violin. The Double Concerto for violin and cello, Op. 102, written in 1887, was Brahms's last orchestral work, and it was, in part, his peace offering to Joachim after a quarrel that had cost the two friends years.

This chapter anchors on the first movement of the Violin Concerto, with its symphonic breadth and its famous slow-movement oboe melody, and on the first movement of the Double Concerto, with its unusual problem (two soloists, one orchestra) and its friendship-repair subtext. Both continue the lesson of Chapter 12: the soloist as partner in a symphonic argument, not a showman. And both deepen Threading A, the biographical arc, by following the Joachim friendship from its origins to its near-end and its mending.

### 13.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The Violin Concerto was written _for_ Joachim and _with_ Joachim, and the collaboration was close and not always smooth. Brahms, who played the piano but not the violin, sent Joachim the solo part and asked for his advice on what was playable and what was idiomatic; Joachim sent back suggestions, many of which Brahms accepted, though he stubbornly kept some passages that violinists found awkward. The most famous fruit of the collaboration is the cadenza, the unaccompanied solo passage near the end of the first movement where the soloist traditionally displays virtuosity. Brahms, unusually, did not write one; he left the first-movement cadenza to the performer, and the cadenza Joachim wrote for it became the standard one, played by most violinists ever since (though others, such as the great Fritz Kreisler, wrote their own alternatives).

There is also a famous joke about the concerto, made by the violinist Pablo de Sarasate, who declined to play it. The slow movement, he complained, does not even give the violinist the main tune; instead, the most beautiful melody of the whole movement is given to the oboe, while the soloist must stand on stage and listen. Sarasate reportedly said he would not stand there, violin in hand, while the oboe played the only real melody. The complaint is funny because it is true, and because it perfectly captures Brahms's symphonic-concerto principle (Chapter 12): even in a violin concerto, Brahms will give the great melody to an orchestral player if the music wants it there, and let the soloist serve the whole rather than hog the tune. Sarasate wanted a display vehicle; Brahms wrote a symphony with violin. The oboe melody, far from being a slight to the soloist, is one of the most beautiful things in the concerto, and the soloist's eventual entry, embroidering around it, is the symphonic-concerto ideal in action.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

**For the Violin Concerto, Op. 77 (first movement):**

  1. **The symphonic breadth.** Like the piano concertos, the Violin Concerto opens with a long orchestral introduction before the soloist enters, and the whole movement has the scale and seriousness of a symphony. Listen for the broad, noble main theme and the symphonic weight; this is a symphony with violin, in the D major and the spacious manner that recall, deliberately, Beethoven's Violin Concerto (also in D).

  2. **The violin as singer and partner.** When the soloist enters, listen for how the violin sings and argues with the orchestra rather than merely dazzling, though there is plenty of brilliance and difficulty too. The violin weaves into the orchestral fabric, takes up its themes, soars above it, but always as part of a symphonic whole.

**For the slow movement of the Violin Concerto:**

  3. **The oboe melody.** The slow movement (the second movement) opens with the famous oboe melody, the tune Sarasate envied, a serene, songful melody given to the oboe while the soloist waits. Listen to it, and then to how the violin, when it finally enters, decorates and develops the oboe's song rather than replacing it. This is the symphonic-concerto principle at its most beautiful.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on two works written for or inspired by Brahms's lifelong friend the violinist Joseph Joachim: the **Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 77 (1878)** , one of the supreme violin concertos, symphonic in scale, with a famous slow-movement oboe melody and a standard cadenza written by Joachim, premiered with Joachim as soloist on January 1, 1879; and the **Double Concerto for violin and cello in A minor, Op. 102 (1887)** , Brahms's last orchestral work, written partly as a reconciliation offering to Joachim after a rupture in their friendship over Joachim's 1884 divorce; both apply the symphonic-concerto principle (soloist as partner, not showman); the **Primary Recordings** are, for the Violin Concerto, a classic such as David Oistrakh or Itzhak Perlman with a major orchestra, and for the Double Concerto, a fine partnered account such as David Oistrakh and Mstislav Rostropovich.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Violin Concerto Oistrakh" or "Brahms Violin Concerto Op. 77 Perlman"; "Brahms Double Concerto Oistrakh Rostropovich" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 小提琴协奏曲 奥伊斯特拉赫"; "勃拉姆斯 二重协奏曲 作品102" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Violin Concerto: Jascha Heifetz with Fritz Reiner (RCA), brilliant and swift; Itzhak Perlman with Carlo Maria Giulini (EMI); Christian Ferras with Herbert von Karajan (Deutsche Grammophon); Hilary Hahn (Sony, 2000s) among modern accounts; Henryk Szeryng; Isaac Stern.
* Double Concerto: David Oistrakh and Mstislav Rostropovich with George Szell (EMI), a reference account; Jascha Heifetz and Gregor Piatigorsky (RCA).

The Violin Concerto's first movement (about twenty-two minutes) and slow-movement oboe melody are the anchors; hear the concerto whole at least once for the symphonic scale and the dancing, Hungarian-flavored finale. The Double Concerto rewards hearing whole; it is shorter and less often performed, and its first movement carries the friendship subtext.

### 13.2 The Listening Window

#### Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 77 (first movement and slow-movement opening)

Timestamps refer to a standard recording; they will shift with the soloist.

**0:00. The orchestral introduction.** The concerto opens with a long orchestral tutti presenting the movement's themes: a broad, noble, lyrical main theme in D major, warm and spacious, with darker and more dramatic material following. Listen for the symphonic breadth; the orchestra states a whole world before the soloist enters, exactly as in the piano concertos.

**About 3:00. The violin enters.** The soloist enters dramatically, with bold, sweeping figures, then takes up the lyrical main theme. Listen for how the violin both soars above the orchestra and weaves into it, brilliant and difficult, but always part of the symphonic argument. The writing is fiendishly hard but never merely showy.

**About 8:00. Development and the lyrical heart.** The movement develops its themes with great breadth, the violin singing, arguing, soaring. Listen for the long-breathed lyricism and the symphonic seriousness; this is a concerto that thinks like a symphony.

**About 18:00. The cadenza.** Near the end of the movement comes the cadenza, the unaccompanied solo passage, traditionally the Joachim cadenza, where the soloist displays virtuosity and reflects on the movement's themes. Listen for the violin alone, brilliant and reflective, before the orchestra returns to close the movement. (Different violinists play different cadenzas; the Joachim is the standard, but you may hear Kreisler's or another.)

**Slow movement (second movement), 0:00. The oboe melody.** The slow movement opens with the famous oboe melody, the tune Sarasate envied: a serene, songful, pastoral melody sung by the oboe over gentle accompaniment, while the soloist waits. Listen to its beauty whole. Then, when the violin enters, listen for how it embroiders and develops the oboe's song rather than displacing it, decorating the melody with rapturous figuration. This is the symphonic-concerto ideal: the soloist serving the orchestra's beauty.

#### Double Concerto in A minor, Op. 102 (first movement)

**0:00. The orchestral call and the soloists' entrances.** The Double Concerto opens with a brief orchestral gesture, then, strikingly, the two soloists enter almost at once in extended solo passages, the cello first, with a grave, declamatory recitative, then the violin answering. Listen for the two soloists establishing themselves before the main argument; Brahms gives each its voice, and then sets them in dialogue.

**About 3:00. The main theme and the partnership.** The movement's main theme (in stern A minor) is taken up by the orchestra and the two soloists, and the central interest is the partnership of violin and cello: the two instruments trade phrases, double each other, argue and agree, a duet within a concerto. Listen for how Brahms balances the two soloists against each other and against the orchestra, a difficult problem he solves by treating them as a pair, often playing together or in close dialogue.

**About 8:00 onward. Development and close.** The movement develops with symphonic seriousness, the two soloists woven through the orchestral argument, and drives to a powerful close. Listen for the gravity and the sense of two voices in partnership; the friendship subtext (two soloists who must work together) is hard not to hear, though Brahms left no explicit program.

### 13.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The symphonic concerto, continued

Both works extend the symphonic-concerto principle of Chapter 12. The Violin Concerto, like the piano concertos, opens with a long orchestral introduction, treats the soloist as a partner in a symphonic argument, and famously gives the slow movement's great melody to the oboe rather than the soloist, the clearest possible statement that, for Brahms, the music comes before the soloist's vanity. Sarasate's complaint that he would not stand and listen while the oboe had the tune is the perfect foil: it is exactly the display-concerto attitude Brahms rejected. The Double Concerto carries the principle further by having _two_ soloists, which forces partnership rather than display: the violin and cello must share, must work together, cannot simply dominate. In both works, the soloist (or soloists) is part of the whole, and the difficulty of the solo writing serves the music rather than mere spectacle. If Chapter 12 introduced the symphonic concerto, this chapter confirms it as Brahms's settled conception: the concerto as serious symphonic structure with solo partners, the opposite of the virtuoso showpiece.

#### The Beethoven inheritance, again

The Violin Concerto, like the First Symphony, is in dialogue with Beethoven. It is in D major, the key of Beethoven's Violin Concerto; it has the same spacious, noble, symphonic character; and it occupies the same place in the violin repertoire that Beethoven's does, the great German symphonic violin concerto. Brahms, the self-conscious heir of Beethoven, was again placing himself in the line, writing the violin concerto that would stand beside Beethoven's as the Brahms First Symphony stands beside Beethoven's symphonies. The two concertos, Beethoven's and Brahms's, are often paired in performance and recording for exactly this reason: they are the two pillars of the German symphonic violin concerto, and Brahms's openly claims the inheritance. As with the First Symphony (Chapter 8), this is not imitation but lineage-claiming, Brahms declaring himself the continuer of the great tradition, on his own terms.

#### Two soloists, one problem: the Double Concerto

The Double Concerto poses a problem few composers have solved: how to write a concerto for two soloists without the music becoming either a tug-of-war or a muddle. Brahms's solution is partnership: he treats the violin and cello as a pair, often having them play together, double each other at the octave, or trade phrases in close dialogue, so that they function almost as a single composite soloist with two voices. This is harder than it sounds, and it is part of why the Double Concerto is less often performed than the Violin Concerto: it needs two great soloists who can blend and partner, not compete. But when it works, the result is unique, a symphonic concerto with a duet at its heart, the two string voices (the bright violin, the warm cello) in lifelong conversation. And here the music and the biography meet: the work was Brahms's reconciliation offering to Joachim, and a concerto built on the partnership of two instruments that must work together, after a friendship had broken and needed mending, carries an unmistakable subtext, even though Brahms, characteristically, never spelled it out.

### 13.4 The Stories

#### The Joachim friendship: origins, rupture, repair

Joseph Joachim runs through Brahms's whole life. He heard the young Brahms on tour in 1853 and sent him to the Schumanns, launching his career (Chapter 3). He was Brahms's closest musical friend for decades, his adviser on string writing, the dedicatee and first performer of the Violin Concerto. Then, in 1884, the friendship broke. Joachim, convinced (wrongly, as it turned out) that his wife Amalie was unfaithful, divorced her; Brahms, believing Amalie innocent, took her side and wrote her a sympathetic letter, which was used in the divorce proceedings against Joachim. Joachim was deeply hurt by what he saw as his oldest friend's betrayal, and the two were estranged for several years.

The Double Concerto was Brahms's path back. In 1887 he wrote the work for violin and cello, intending the violin part for Joachim, and the gesture, a major new work built around his estranged friend's instrument, requiring them to collaborate again, was understood as a peace offering. Joachim accepted; the two worked together on the concerto, performed it, and were reconciled, their friendship restored for the remaining decade of Brahms's life. That a quarrel was healed through music, that Brahms said with a concerto what he could not say in words, is one of the most moving episodes in his biography, and it gives the Double Concerto, his last orchestral work, a special poignancy: the final orchestral statement of a man who used music to mend the oldest friendship of his life.

#### The Violin Concerto's reception and afterlife

Unlike the First Piano Concerto, the Violin Concerto was reasonably well received, though some early critics and violinists (like Sarasate) found it unviolinistic or too symphonic, "a concerto against the violin," one quipped, meaning the orchestra and the music seemed to fight the soloist's display. But the work's greatness was soon recognized, and it has long been one of the four or five central concertos in every violinist's repertoire, the German symphonic violin concerto beside Beethoven's. Its difficulty is legendary, but the difficulty serves the music; its slow movement is among the most beloved in the repertoire (oboe melody and all); and its finale, a dancing, fiery Hungarian-flavored rondo, sends the work off in high spirits, Brahms's love of Hungarian-Romani music (recall the Hungarian Dances, Chapter 2) flavoring the close. The concerto is a summit of the form and one of Brahms's most popular works.

#### Recording history briefly

The Violin Concerto has one of the great discographies in the repertoire: Oistrakh, Heifetz, Perlman, Ferras (with Karajan), Szeryng, Stern, and among moderns Hahn and Janine Jansen, each bringing a different balance of brilliance and lyricism, and a different cadenza choice (mostly Joachim's). The Double Concerto, less often recorded, has the reference Oistrakh / Rostropovich / Szell account and the Heifetz / Piatigorsky among its classics; because it needs two partnered soloists, the great recordings are great partnerships. The fuller concerto lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects across the volume.

To the piano concertos (Chapter 12). The symphonic-concerto principle, soloist as partner, the great melody sometimes given to the orchestra, governs the violin and double concertos exactly as it governs the piano concertos. The four concertos together are Brahms's symphonic-concerto achievement.

To the First Symphony and the Beethoven inheritance (Chapter 8). The Violin Concerto, in D major and symphonic in scale, claims the Beethovenian inheritance as openly as the First Symphony does, placing Brahms beside Beethoven as the master of the German symphonic concerto.

To the Schumann-Clara story and Threading A (Chapters 3-4, 19). Joachim, the friend at the center of this chapter, is the man who launched Brahms's career by sending him to the Schumanns, and the Joachim friendship, its rupture and repair, is a major strand of Brahms's life. The Double Concerto's reconciliation subtext is one of the warmest episodes in Threading A.

To the violin sonatas (Chapter 14). The next chapter takes up Brahms's intimate chamber music for violin and piano, the private counterpart to the public Violin Concerto, where the song-instrumental connection (Brahms quoting his own Lieder) comes to the fore.

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find recordings from §13.1.

  1. **The symphonic opening of the Violin Concerto (0:00).** Fix in your ear the long orchestral introduction with its broad, noble D-major theme before the soloist enters, the symphonic scale that recalls Beethoven's Violin Concerto.

  2. **The oboe melody (Violin Concerto, slow movement, 0:00).** Listen to the serene oboe melody that opens the slow movement, the tune Sarasate envied, and to how the violin decorates rather than displaces it when it enters. This is the symphonic-concerto ideal at its most beautiful.

  3. **The two soloists' entrances in the Double Concerto (first movement, 0:00).** Listen for the cello's grave opening recitative and the violin's answer, the two soloists establishing their voices before the main argument, and then their partnership throughout.

  4. **The Hungarian finale of the Violin Concerto (third movement).** Listen for the dancing, fiery, Hungarian-flavored rondo that closes the concerto in high spirits, Brahms's love of Romani-flavored music lighting up the end of a symphonic work.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the Beethoven pairing): Beethoven's Violin Concerto in D, the great precedent that Brahms's openly claims. The two are often recorded as a pair; hearing them together shows the tradition of the German symphonic violin concerto that Brahms was joining, and how he made it his own.

**Listen** (the cadenza comparison): two recordings of the Violin Concerto with different first-movement cadenzas, the standard Joachim and an alternative such as Kreisler's. Hearing how different cadenzas reflect on the movement's themes is a small lesson in what a cadenza is for and how performers shape a concerto.

**Read** : Michael Musgrave's _The Music of Brahms_ (1985) on the concertos, for the symphonic-concerto conception and the special problems of the Double Concerto.

**Read** (the friendship): Styra Avins's _Johannes Brahms: Life and Letters_ (1997) and Swafford's biography on the Joachim friendship, its origins in 1853, the 1884 rupture over the divorce, and the reconciliation through the Double Concerto, the human story behind both works in this chapter.

---

## Chapter 14: The Three Violin Sonatas — Op. 78 (1879), Op. 100 (1886), Op. 108 (1888)

After the public grandeur of the concertos, this chapter turns to the intimate. Brahms wrote three sonatas for violin and piano, the three numbered ones that survive (he destroyed earlier attempts, true to form), and they are among the most beloved of all his chamber works: private, conversational, song-filled, the opposite of the symphonic concerto's grandeur. Where the Violin Concerto is a symphony with violin for the concert hall, the violin sonatas are intimate dialogues for two equal partners in a room. They also bring to the fore a thread that runs through all of Brahms's instrumental music: the connection between his songs and his instrumental works. Brahms quotes his own Lieder in these sonatas, weaving melodies from his songs into the instrumental fabric, so that the sonatas carry, half-hidden, the words and feelings of the songs they draw on.

This chapter anchors on the first movement of the Third Sonata, in D minor, Op. 108, the most dramatic and passionate of the three, and looks more briefly at the First Sonata, in G major, Op. 78, the "Regenlied" or "Rain Song" Sonata, which weaves in melodies from two of Brahms's own songs about rain. Together they show the intimate, song-haunted Brahms of the chamber music, and continue the lesson, begun in Chapter 4's piano trio, that chamber music is conversation among equals, here narrowed to its most intimate form: two players, two voices, in dialogue.

### 14.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

The First Violin Sonata is called the "Regenlied" Sonata, the "Rain Song" Sonata, and the name points to one of Brahms's most characteristic and tender habits: quoting his own songs in his instrumental music. The sonata's finale is built on the melody of two of Brahms's own Lieder, "Regenlied" (Rain Song) and "Nachklang" (Echo), both settings of poems about rain, and both of which share a gentle, falling, raindrop-like melodic figure. Brahms wove that song-melody into the sonata's last movement, so that the finale is, in effect, a wordless meditation on the rain songs, the violin and piano "singing" a melody that, in its original form, carried words about rain, memory, and childhood tears.

The biographical resonance deepens the gesture. Around the time Brahms wrote the sonata, Felix Schumann, Clara's youngest son and Brahms's own godson, was dying of tuberculosis (he died in early 1879, the year of the sonata), and the rain songs' themes of grief and remembrance took on a personal weight; Brahms sent the sonata to Clara, who was profoundly moved by it, recognizing the songs woven into it and the feelings they carried. Whether or not every detail of the biographical story is firm, the central fact is well attested and deeply Brahmsian: he built an instrumental sonata around his own songs, so that the music carries, beneath its wordless surface, the emotional charge of the words. This song-instrumental weaving is one of the most intimate things in Brahms, the composer of two hundred Lieder folding his songs into his chamber music, and it is what makes the violin sonatas feel like private confidences rather than public statements.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

**For the Third Sonata, Op. 108 (first movement):**

  1. **Passion and restlessness.** The Third Sonata, in D minor, is the most dramatic and passionate of the three, and its first movement is restless, surging, intense. Listen for the agitated energy, the long sweeping lines, the sense of urgency, the opposite of the gentle First Sonata. This is the stormy, passionate Brahms in intimate chamber form.

  2. **The two equal partners.** Listen for the conversation between violin and piano. Neither dominates; the two trade melodies, answer each other, build together. The piano is a full partner, not an accompaniment, and the interest is in the dialogue. Notice, in the first movement, a long passage where the piano holds a single repeated note (a pedal point) while the violin sings above and the harmony shifts beneath, a famous moment of suspended tension.

**For the First Sonata, Op. 78 (the "Regenlied"):**

  3. **The gentle rain-song melody.** The First Sonata is gentle, lyrical, and song-haunted. Listen, especially in its finale, for the gentle, falling, raindrop-like melody drawn from Brahms's own rain songs, a tender, slightly melancholy tune that gives the sonata its name and its intimate, remembering quality. Hold this gentle melody against the Third Sonata's passion to feel the range of the three.

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on two of Brahms's three violin-and-piano sonatas: the **Sonata No. 3 in D minor, Op. 108 (1888)** , the most dramatic and passionate, in four movements, with a restless, surging first movement; and the **Sonata No. 1 in G major, Op. 78 (1879)** , the gentle "Regenlied" or "Rain Song" Sonata, which weaves melodies from Brahms's own songs "Regenlied" and "Nachklang" into its finale; both are intimate chamber dialogues for two equal partners, exemplifying Brahms's song-instrumental weaving; the **Primary Recordings** are classic accounts such as the partnership of a great violinist and pianist, for instance Itzhak Perlman with Vladimir Ashkenazy, or Augustin Dumay with Maria João Pires, chosen for warmth and equal partnership.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Violin Sonata No. 3 Op. 108 Perlman Ashkenazy"; "Brahms Violin Sonata No. 1 Regenlied Op. 78" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 第三小提琴奏鸣曲 作品108 帕尔曼"; "勃拉姆斯 第一小提琴奏鸣曲 雨之歌 作品78" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Itzhak Perlman with Vladimir Ashkenazy (EMI), warm and beautifully partnered, a longtime favorite for all three sonatas.
* Augustin Dumay with Maria João Pires (Deutsche Grammophon), lyrical and finely balanced.
* Gidon Kremer with various pianists; Renaud Capuçon; the historic Adolf Busch / Rudolf Serkin accounts.

The three sonatas are short enough to hear together in one sitting (about an hour total), which is the ideal way to feel their range, the gentle G major, the lyrical A major (Op. 100, not anchored here but lovely), and the passionate D minor. If you hear only two, take the gentle "Regenlied" First and the stormy Third, the two anchors, to feel the contrast.

### 14.2 The Listening Window

#### Sonata No. 3 in D minor, Op. 108 (first movement)

Timestamps refer to a standard recording; they will shift with the performers.

**0:00. The restless main theme.** The sonata opens with the violin singing a long, restless, surging melody in D minor over a quietly agitated piano, urgent and passionate from the first bar. Listen for the sweeping line and the underlying tension; this is the most dramatic of the three sonatas, and its first movement is all forward motion and feeling.

**About 2:00. The dialogue and the second theme.** Listen for the conversation between violin and piano, the two trading and developing the material, and for a warmer second theme. The two instruments are equal partners throughout; neither merely accompanies.

**About 4:00. The famous pedal point.** In the development, listen for a remarkable passage where the piano holds a single repeated note low in the bass (a pedal point) for a long stretch while the violin sings above and the harmony shifts and darkens beneath, a moment of suspended, brooding tension that is one of the most admired strokes in the sonata. The unchanging bass under the shifting harmony is a small cousin of the passacaglia principle (Chapter 11), tension built on a constant foundation.

**About 6:00 onward. The drive to the close.** The movement develops its restless material and drives to a passionate D-minor close. Listen across the whole movement for its surging intensity, the stormy, passionate Brahms in the most intimate of settings, two players in a room generating the energy of a symphony.

#### Sonata No. 1 in G major, Op. 78 (the "Regenlied," finale)

**0:00 (of the finale). The rain-song melody.** The finale opens with the gentle, falling, raindrop-like melody drawn from Brahms's songs "Regenlied" and "Nachklang," a tender, slightly melancholy tune over a softly pattering piano. Listen to it; the falling figure is the "rain," and the melody carries, beneath its wordless surface, the songs' themes of rain, memory, and gentle grief. (The sonata's earlier movements have prepared this melody; Brahms also recalls the slow movement's material here, binding the work together.)

**Through the finale.** Listen for how the rain-song melody is developed and varied, passed between violin and piano, sometimes troubled, sometimes consoled, and for the recall of earlier material that gives the finale, and the whole sonata, a sense of summing-up and remembrance. The movement, and the sonata, end gently, in a quiet, consoled G major, the rain having fallen and passed. Listen for the tender, remembering quality; this is the song-haunted, intimate Brahms at his most personal.

### 14.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Chamber music as intimate conversation

The violin sonatas are the most intimate chamber works in this volume, two players, two voices, in dialogue, and they refine the lesson begun with the piano trio (Chapter 4): chamber music is conversation among equals. Here the conversation is narrowed to its most intimate form, a duet, and the equality of the two partners is total: the piano is never a mere accompanist but a full voice, trading melodies with the violin, answering it, sometimes leading. This equal partnership is the essence of the duo sonata, and Brahms, one of the supreme chamber composers, makes the two instruments genuine partners in feeling and argument. Listen, in all three sonatas, for the give and take, the way an idea passes from violin to piano and back, the way the two instruments build something together that neither could make alone. The intimacy of the form suited Brahms's reserved, private temperament: where the concertos make public statements, the sonatas share confidences, and the listener is admitted, as it were, into a private room where two friends are talking in music.

#### The song-instrumental weaving

The "Regenlied" Sonata introduces, most explicitly, a thread that runs through all of Brahms's instrumental music: the weaving-in of his own songs. Brahms was a song-composer to his core (two hundred Lieder, Chapter 16), and his melodic instinct was a song-writer's instinct; his instrumental themes are often as singable as his songs, and sometimes they _are_ his songs, quoted or alluded to. The First Sonata builds its finale on the rain-song melody; the B-flat Piano Concerto's cello solo (Chapter 12) echoes a song; the violin sonatas and other chamber works are full of such song-connections. The effect is to bring the emotional charge of the words into the wordless music: when you know that the "Regenlied" finale is built on songs about rain, memory, and childhood tears, the instrumental melody carries that feeling, half-hidden, beneath its surface. This song-instrumental interpenetration, deliberate and deeply characteristic, is one of the keys to Brahms's intimate music, and it connects him to the great Lied tradition of Schubert, whose own instrumental works constantly drew on his songs. In Brahms, the symphonist and the song-writer are the same man, and the violin sonatas are where you hear them most intimately fused.

#### Three sonatas, three characters

A word about the three sonatas as a set, since hearing them together is so rewarding. The First, Op. 78, is gentle, lyrical, pastoral, and song-haunted, the "Rain Song" Sonata, music of tenderness and remembrance. The Second, Op. 100 (not anchored here but worth hearing), is sunny, warm, and songful, sometimes called the "Thun" Sonata after the Swiss lake where Brahms wrote it, the most relaxed and lyrical of the three. The Third, Op. 108, is dramatic, passionate, and restless, the stormy one, in four movements rather than three, with the breadth and intensity of a symphony in miniature. Together they span Brahms's emotional range in the intimate duo form: tenderness, warmth, passion. And all three share the qualities that make Brahms's chamber music so beloved, the equal partnership, the song-filled melody, the rich inner texture, the autumnal warmth of his maturity (all three come from his fifties, the same period as the late symphonies). They are the perfect intimate counterpart to the public grandeur of the concertos, and a reminder that the composer of the monumental symphonies was also a master of the most private musical conversation.

### 14.4 The Stories

#### The "Regenlied" and Clara

The First Sonata's connection to Clara Schumann deepens its intimacy. Brahms wove his rain songs into the sonata, and when he sent it to Clara, she was deeply moved, recognizing the songs and the feelings they carried; she wrote to Brahms of how the music touched her. The rain songs' themes, gentle grief, memory, the tears of childhood, resonated with the sorrows of the Schumann circle in those years (Clara's son Felix, Brahms's godson, was dying). The sonata is thus bound up with the lifelong Brahms-Clara relationship (Chapter 4): a work that says, in the indirect language of music and quoted song, what the two old friends felt about loss and remembrance. This is Brahms's reserve in action: he could not easily speak his feelings, but he could weave them into a sonata, and Clara, who knew his songs and his heart, could read them there. The "Regenlied" Sonata is one of the most personal documents of that forty-year friendship, a confidence shared in music.

#### The intimate Brahms

The violin sonatas, like the late piano pieces (Chapter 17) and the clarinet works (Chapter 15), show the side of Brahms furthest from the "heavy academic" caricature: the intimate, tender, song-filled chamber composer, writing music of warmth and feeling for small forces and private settings. Brahms loved this intimate music-making; he played chamber music with friends all his life, and much of his greatest work is for small ensembles. The sonatas were written for and with great players (the Third is dedicated to the conductor and pianist Hans von Bülow), and they belong to the world of the private musical evening, two friends playing together, rather than the concert hall. Hearing them corrects any sense that Brahms was only a composer of monuments; he was also, and perhaps most deeply, a composer of intimacies, and the violin sonatas are among the warmest.

#### Recording history briefly

The three violin sonatas are central chamber repertoire, usually recorded as a set by a violinist-pianist partnership. The Perlman / Ashkenazy and Dumay / Pires accounts (our primary candidates) are warm and beautifully partnered; the historic Busch / Serkin and many modern duos (Kremer, Capuçon, and others) offer fine alternatives. Because the sonatas are duos of equal partners, the great recordings are great partnerships, two musicians who listen and respond to each other, which is itself the subject of the music. The fuller chamber lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects across the volume.

To the Lieder (Chapter 16). The song-instrumental weaving of the "Regenlied" Sonata points directly to Chapter 16, which takes up Brahms's two hundred songs. The sonatas quote the songs; Chapter 16 lets you hear the songs themselves and understand the song-writer at the heart of all Brahms's music.

To the late piano pieces (Chapter 17) and the clarinet works (Chapter 15). The intimate, song-filled, autumnal chamber Brahms of the violin sonatas is the same Brahms who wrote the late piano intermezzos and the clarinet works, all from his maturity, all intimate, all warm and inward. The violin sonatas belong with them as the private, tender counterpart to the public symphonies and concertos.

To the Schumann-Clara story (Chapter 4). The "Regenlied" Sonata, woven from Brahms's songs and sent to a moved Clara, is one of the most personal documents of the forty-year Brahms-Clara friendship, a confidence shared in the indirect language of music.

### Conversation Seeds

Listen to that rain-song melody. What does it mean to weave words into wordless music this way? Why might a reserved man like Brahms find it easier to say something through a quoted song than to say it directly?"_

What might Brahms's preference for intimate, conversational chamber music tell you about his temperament, compared with composers who preferred the grand public gesture?"_

Listen to the tender 'Regenlied' melody and the stormy Third's first movement back to back. What does it tell you that the same composer, in the same years, could write both the most intimate tenderness and the most surging passion? How do the intimate sonatas change your picture of the composer of the monumental symphonies?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find recordings from §14.1.

  1. **The restless opening of the Third Sonata (Op. 108, first movement, 0:00).** Fix in your ear the surging, passionate violin melody over the agitated piano, the most dramatic of the three sonatas in its most intense movement.

  2. **The pedal point in the Third Sonata (about 4:00).** Listen for the long passage where the piano holds a single repeated low note while the violin sings above and the harmony shifts beneath, a brooding, suspended tension, a small cousin of the passacaglia's constant-foundation principle.

  3. **The rain-song melody (Op. 78, finale, 0:00).** Listen to the gentle, falling, raindrop-like melody drawn from Brahms's own songs, and feel how it carries the songs' themes of memory and gentle grief beneath its wordless surface.

  4. **The equal partnership (across both sonatas).** Listen throughout for the conversation between violin and piano, the way ideas pass between the two equal voices. This intimate dialogue is the essence of the duo sonata and the warm, private heart of Brahms's chamber music.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the songs themselves): Brahms's "Regenlied," Op. 59 No. 3, and "Nachklang," Op. 59 No. 4, the rain songs woven into the First Sonata. Hearing the songs with their words, then the sonata that quotes them, makes the song-instrumental weaving vivid and shows how much feeling the wordless sonata carries from the words.

**Listen** (the Second Sonata): the Sonata No. 2 in A major, Op. 100, the sunny "Thun" Sonata not anchored in this chapter, to complete your sense of the three as a set. It, too, draws on Brahms's songs, and its warm lyricism rounds out the emotional range between the gentle First and the passionate Third.

**Read** : Michael Musgrave's _The Music of Brahms_ (1985) on the chamber music, for a clear account of the violin sonatas and the song-instrumental connections.

**Read** (the Lieder context): look ahead to Chapter 16 on the songs, and to the Schubert volume on the song-instrumental interpenetration in Schubert, the great tradition of weaving song into instrumental music that Brahms inherited and continued. Brahms revered Schubert and edited his manuscripts; the kinship is deep.
