## Chapter 15: The Late Clarinet Works — Op. 114, Op. 115 (1891), Op. 120 (1894)

In 1890, Brahms decided to stop. He was fifty-seven, he had written his four symphonies, his concertos, his great chamber music, and he announced, in effect, that he was retiring, that he had done enough and would compose no more. He even put his affairs in order as if for the end of a creative life. And then, in 1891, he heard a clarinetist play, and the retirement collapsed. The playing of Richard Mühlfeld, principal clarinet of the excellent Meiningen court orchestra, so moved and inspired Brahms that he came out of retirement to write a series of works featuring the clarinet, music of such autumnal beauty that it forms, in effect, a second late flowering, an Indian summer of his chamber output. The clarinet works are Brahms's farewell to chamber music, written when he thought he had already said farewell, and they are among the most beloved things he ever made.

This is an anchor-heavy chapter, and it centers on the greatest of the clarinet works: the Clarinet Quintet in B minor, Op. 115, for clarinet and string quartet, widely regarded as the peak of Brahms's chamber music and one of the supreme chamber works in the whole repertoire. We will focus on its first two movements, the autumnal, sighing first movement and the sublime slow movement with its Hungarian-Gypsy-style central episode, and look more briefly at the Clarinet Sonatas, Op. 120, the last sonatas Brahms wrote. The chapter completes the portrait of the late, intimate, valedictory Brahms that the violin sonatas (Chapter 14) began, and it shows the developing-variation principle operating in the cyclical thematic returns that bind the quintet into a single autumnal meditation.

### 15.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

In March 1891, Brahms visited Meiningen, the small German court whose duke maintained an unusually fine orchestra that Brahms loved to work with (it had premiered his Fourth Symphony, Chapter 11). There he heard the orchestra's principal clarinetist, Richard Mühlfeld, and was captivated. Mühlfeld's playing, warm, expressive, singing, vocal in its beauty, opened Brahms's ears to the clarinet as he had never quite heard it, and it reversed his decision to retire. Within months Brahms had written two major works for Mühlfeld, the Clarinet Trio Op. 114 and the Clarinet Quintet Op. 115; three years later he added the two Clarinet Sonatas Op. 120. Brahms grew so fond of Mühlfeld and his playing that he gave the clarinetist affectionate nicknames, calling him his "dear nightingale" and, in mock-grand style, "Fräulein Klarinette" (Miss Clarinet), a playful tribute to the singing, almost feminine beauty of his tone.

The story is one of the most charming in Brahms's late life, and it has a deeper meaning. A great artist who believed he was finished, who had laid down his pen, was called back to creation by a single beautiful sound, the voice of one player's clarinet. The works that resulted are suffused with the autumnal feeling of a man composing past his own announced ending, music of farewell and reconciliation, warm and inward and touched with the melancholy of late evening. That they exist at all is owed to Mühlfeld; that they are so beautiful is owed to the way the clarinet's singing voice drew out of the old Brahms a last, tender lyricism. The Clarinet Quintet, in particular, is the masterpiece of this Indian summer, and one of the works for which Brahms is most loved.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The autumnal, sighing beauty.** From the opening, listen to the quintet's prevailing mood: autumnal, tender, melancholy, warm, the sound of late evening or late life. The clarinet sings over and through the string quartet with a vocal, almost human warmth, and the whole work has a valedictory, reconciled quality, neither tragic nor triumphant but accepting, like the quiet ending of the Third Symphony (Chapter 10) extended into a whole work.

  2. **The clarinet as a singing voice.** Listen for how Brahms uses the clarinet, not as a virtuoso showpiece but as a singing voice, blending with and rising out of the strings, its warm, woody, vocal tone carrying the work's most tender melodies. Mühlfeld's singing playing inspired this, and the clarinet writing throughout treats the instrument as a kind of wordless singer, the song-instinct (Chapters 14, 16) given to a wind instrument.

  3. **The Hungarian episode in the slow movement.** The slow movement (the second movement) is sublime, hushed and songful, but at its center it breaks into a passionate, free, improvisatory episode in the Hungarian-Gypsy style Brahms loved (recall the Hungarian Dances, Chapter 2), the clarinet wailing and curling in elaborate, rhapsodic figuration over tremolo strings. Listen for this startling central outburst, a moment of wild, exotic passion at the heart of the serene slow movement, before the calm returns.

#### One-sentence background

The Clarinet Quintet in B minor, Op. 115 (1891), for clarinet and string quartet, is a roughly thirty-eight-minute, four-movement work, the peak of Brahms's chamber music, written for the clarinetist Richard Mühlfeld after his playing drew Brahms out of an announced retirement; it is autumnal and valedictory in mood, treats the clarinet as a singing voice, contains a sublime slow movement with a wild Hungarian-Gypsy central episode, and is bound together by cyclical thematic returns (the first-movement material returns at the end of the finale); the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is a classic such as the clarinetist Karl Leister with the Amadeus Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon), or Karl Leister with members of the Berlin Philharmonic, chosen for warmth, blend, and command of the autumnal mood; the Clarinet Sonatas Op. 120 (1894) are also briefly considered.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Clarinet Quintet Op. 115 Leister Amadeus"; "Brahms Clarinet Sonatas Op. 120" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 单簧管五重奏 作品115 莱斯特"; "勃拉姆斯 单簧管奏鸣曲 作品120" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Clarinet Quintet: Karl Leister with the Amadeus Quartet or with Berlin Philharmonic colleagues (Deutsche Grammophon); Reginald Kell (historic); Martin Fröst with the Vertavo Quartet (modern); Sabine Meyer with various quartets.
* Clarinet Sonatas Op. 120: Karl Leister, Martin Fröst, Sabine Meyer, and many others, often paired with the quintet; the sonatas also exist in a viola version Brahms made.

Hear the Clarinet Quintet whole; it is one of those works whose autumnal mood accumulates across its four movements, and the cyclical return of the opening material at the very end only makes its full effect if you have heard the whole. The first movement and the Hungarian-centered slow movement are the anchors; the finale's return to the opening is the structural key.

### 15.2 The Listening Window

#### Clarinet Quintet in B minor, Op. 115

Timestamps refer to a roughly thirty-eight-minute reading; they will shift with the performers. We go close on the first two movements and sketch the last two and the crucial return.

**First movement (Allegro).**

_0:00. The opening string figure and the clarinet's entrance._ The movement opens with a gentle, swaying figure in the violins, ambiguous between major and minor, autumnal and questioning. Then the clarinet enters, rising out of the strings with a warm, sighing, vocal melody. Listen for the blend of clarinet and strings and for the prevailing tone of tender melancholy. Fix the opening string figure in your ear; it returns, transformed, at the very end of the whole quintet, binding the work into a cycle.

_About 2:00. The lyrical themes._ The movement unfolds long, songful, autumnal melodies, the clarinet singing through and over the quartet, the mood warm and reflective with an undertow of sadness. Listen for the developing variation, the way the themes grow and transform from the opening material, and for the major-minor shimmer that recalls the Third Symphony's ambiguity.

_About 8:00. The close._ The movement ends gently, the autumnal mood sustained, the clarinet and strings settling into reflective calm. There is no struggle, no triumph, only the prevailing tenderness and melancholy of late evening.

**Second movement (Adagio).**

_0:00. The sublime opening._ The slow movement opens with one of the most beautiful passages Brahms ever wrote: the clarinet sings a long, hushed, songful melody over muted strings, music of profound serenity and tenderness, the still heart of the quintet. Listen to the clarinet's vocal beauty and the hushed, glowing string accompaniment; this is sublime, inward, deeply peaceful music.

_About 4:00. The Hungarian-Gypsy episode._ And then, at the center of this serenity, a startling change: the music breaks into a passionate, free, improvisatory episode in the Hungarian-Gypsy style, the clarinet wailing and curling in elaborate, rhapsodic, almost wild figuration over agitated tremolo strings, like a Gypsy fiddler's improvisation translated to the clarinet. Listen for this central outburst of exotic passion, a moment of wildness and fire at the heart of the calm, before the serene opening music returns. The contrast, sublime peace framing wild passion, is one of the great strokes in Brahms's chamber music, and it shows his lifelong love of Hungarian-Romani music (Chapter 2) surfacing in his most exalted late work.

_About 8:00. The return to calm._ The serene opening melody returns, the clarinet singing the movement to a hushed, peaceful close. The wildness has been contained, framed, by the prevailing serenity; the slow movement ends in deep calm.

**Third movement (Andantino — Presto non assai).** A gentle, intermezzo-like movement (in Brahms's late manner, no driving scherzo) that opens with a relaxed, songful Andantino and then moves into a faster, light, scherzo-like Presto that is a transformation of the opening material, developing variation turned to graceful ends, before closing gently. Listen for the gracious lightness and the thematic transformation.

**Fourth movement (Con moto), and the cyclical return.** The finale is a set of variations on a theme, autumnal and songful, the clarinet and strings transforming the theme through varied moods. And then comes the masterstroke: near the very end, Brahms brings back the swaying opening figure of the first movement, the music the quintet began with, so that the whole work closes by returning to where it started, the cycle completed, the end folding back into the beginning. Listen for this return of the opening material at the close; it gives the quintet a profound sense of wholeness and of looking back, a large-scale "Rückblick" (recall the Op. 5 sonata, Chapter 3) that makes the entire work a single, unified, backward-gazing meditation. The quintet ends in the autumnal B minor it began with, the journey having come full circle.

The two things to carry: the autumnal, sighing beauty and the cyclical return that binds the work (first movement and finale), and the sublime slow movement with its wild Hungarian heart. Together they make the Clarinet Quintet the supreme statement of the late, reconciled, song-filled Brahms.

### 15.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The autumnal late style

The Clarinet Quintet is the central document of Brahms's late style, the manner of his last decade, and it is worth naming that style's qualities, because you will meet them again in the late piano pieces (Chapter 17) and the final works (Chapter 19). The late Brahms is autumnal: the prevailing mood is reflective, tender, melancholy, reconciled, the music of late evening and late life, neither struggling toward triumph (the First Symphony) nor facing tragedy head-on (the Fourth) but accepting, looking back, at peace with sorrow. The textures are warm and inward, often hushed; the melodies are song-like and breathing; the harmony shimmers between major and minor; and the forms turn from public drama toward private meditation. This late style is one of the glories of Brahms, the sound of a great artist who has come through struggle and tragedy to a kind of serene acceptance, and the Clarinet Quintet is its masterpiece. The quiet ending of the Third Symphony pointed the way; the clarinet works and the late piano pieces are the full arrival. Listen for the autumnal tone as the defining quality of all the late music, and hear in it not weakness or fading but a hard-won, reconciled depth.

#### Cyclical return as developing variation

The Clarinet Quintet's structural masterstroke, the return of the first-movement opening material at the end of the finale, is a form of the developing-variation, transforming-return principle that organizes so much of Brahms (Chapters 3, 11, 20). By bringing back the music the work began with, transformed by everything that has happened in between, Brahms makes the whole quintet a single unified arc that folds back on itself, a large-scale backward glance. This cyclical thinking, the binding of a multi-movement work by recalling and transforming its opening at the close, is the chamber-music equivalent of the motto-threading you heard in the Third Symphony: a way of unifying a large structure by the return of transformed material. It gives the quintet its profound sense of wholeness and of memory, the feeling that the end remembers the beginning, that the whole work is a meditation looking back on itself. This is developing variation as a principle of large-scale unity and as an expression of the late style's backward-gazing, reconciled spirit. Chapter 20 collects this cyclical-return technique among the instances of Brahms's transforming method.

#### The Hungarian episode and Brahms's two worlds

The wild Hungarian-Gypsy episode at the center of the slow movement deserves a closer look, because it shows the doubleness in Brahms that this book keeps returning to. The slow movement is, for the most part, the height of refined, serene, "high-art" Brahms, sublime and inward. And at its center it breaks into the free, passionate, "low" or popular world of Hungarian-Romani café music, the world of the young Brahms's tours with Reményi (Chapter 3) and of the Hungarian Dances (Chapter 2), here translated to the clarinet's wailing improvisation. The juxtaposition, refined serenity framing wild popular passion, is pure Brahms: the learned master and the lover of folk and Gypsy music were always the same man, and his greatest works hold both. The Wiegenlied showed the folk-song side; the Requiem fugues showed the learned side; the Clarinet Quintet's slow movement holds both worlds in a single movement, the sublime and the wild, the high and the popular, fused. It is one of the most vivid demonstrations in the volume that the "heavy academic" caricature misses half of Brahms, the half that loved the fiddler's freedom and the folk dance, and that his depth comes partly from holding the refined and the passionate, the learned and the popular, together.

### 15.4 The Stories

#### Mühlfeld and the end of retirement

The Clarinet Quintet exists because of Richard Mühlfeld. Brahms, having announced his retirement in 1890, heard Mühlfeld play at Meiningen in 1891 and was so moved that he resumed composing, writing the Clarinet Trio and Quintet that year and the Clarinet Sonatas in 1894. He adored Mühlfeld's singing tone, calling him his "nightingale" and "Fräulein Klarinette," and he wrote the clarinet parts to exploit exactly the vocal, expressive beauty that had captivated him. The collaboration recalls the Joachim partnership (Chapter 13): as Joachim's playing shaped the Violin Concerto, Mühlfeld's playing shaped the clarinet works, a great performer drawing great music out of a composer. That the works are Brahms's farewell to chamber music, written past his own announced ending, gives the story its poignancy: an old master called back to creation by a beautiful sound, producing, in his Indian summer, some of the most beloved music of his life. Mühlfeld premiered the works and toured them with Brahms, and they remained close until Brahms's death. The clarinet repertoire was permanently enriched; before Brahms, there was little great chamber music for the instrument, and the Quintet and Sonatas became, at once, cornerstones of it.

#### Recording history briefly

The Clarinet Quintet is central chamber repertoire with a deep discography. Karl Leister's accounts (with the Amadeus Quartet and with Berlin Philharmonic colleagues) are classics of warmth and blend; the historic Reginald Kell, and modern players like Martin Fröst and Sabine Meyer, offer fine alternatives, each finding a different balance of serenity and passion, especially in the Hungarian slow-movement episode. The Clarinet Sonatas Op. 120, often recorded alongside the quintet (and existing in a viola version Brahms made), are likewise staples. Because the music is about blend, the clarinet singing with and through the strings, the great recordings are those where the clarinet and quartet truly merge into one autumnal voice. The fuller chamber and clarinet lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

This chapter connects across the volume.

To the late piano pieces (Chapter 17). The autumnal late style of the Clarinet Quintet, reflective, tender, inward, reconciled, is the same style as the late piano intermezzos, written in the same last years. The clarinet works and the late piano pieces together are the heart of Brahms's late flowering.

To the Third Symphony (Chapter 10). The quiet, un-triumphant ending of the Third Symphony pointed toward the autumnal acceptance that the late clarinet and piano works fully embody. The Third's close and the Quintet's mood are the same reconciled spirit at different scales.

To the Hungarian Dances and the folk Brahms (Chapter 2). The wild Hungarian-Gypsy episode in the slow movement is the popular, folk-rooted Brahms surfacing in his most exalted late work, the same love of Romani-flavored music that produced the Hungarian Dances, now at the heart of a sublime chamber masterpiece.

To the final years (Chapter 19) and the synthesis (Chapter 20). The clarinet works belong to Brahms's last creative period, and the cyclical-return technique of the Quintet is a key instance of his transforming method that Chapter 20 collects. Chapter 19 places the clarinet works in the story of Brahms's final years.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Brahms had announced his retirement and laid down his pen when the playing of a single clarinetist, Richard Mühlfeld, drew him back into creation, producing some of the most beautiful music of his life. Listen to the autumnal beauty of the Clarinet Quintet, written past his own announced ending. What does it tell you that a great artist who believed he was finished could be called back by a beautiful sound? Is there something especially moving about work made after one thought one had nothing left to say?"_

* _"At the center of the quintet's sublime, serene slow movement, the music suddenly breaks into a wild, passionate Hungarian-Gypsy improvisation before the calm returns. Listen for that startling central outburst. Why might Brahms have placed wild popular passion at the very heart of his most refined and exalted music? What does this fusion of the 'high' and the 'low,' the learned and the folk, tell you about a composer too often caricatured as merely heavy and academic?"_

* _"The quintet ends by bringing back the music it began with, transformed by everything that has happened, so that the whole work folds back on itself in a large-scale backward glance. Listen for that return of the opening at the very end. Why might an aging composer write a work that ends by remembering its own beginning? How does the cyclical return change the meaning of the whole, making it a meditation that looks back on itself?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a recording from §15.1. Hear the quintet whole, then focus on these.

  1. **The opening string figure and the clarinet's entrance (first movement, 0:00).** Fix in your ear the swaying, autumnal opening figure and the clarinet rising out of the strings with its sighing melody. Remember the opening figure; it returns at the very end of the whole work.

  2. **The sublime slow movement and its Hungarian heart (second movement, opening and about 4:00).** Listen to the hushed, songful clarinet melody over muted strings, then to the wild Hungarian-Gypsy improvisation at the center, then to the return of the calm. This framing of wild passion by sublime serenity is one of the great strokes in Brahms's chamber music.

  3. **The cyclical return (finale, near the end).** Listen for the moment Brahms brings back the first-movement opening figure at the close of the finale, folding the whole work back to its beginning. This cyclical return is the quintet's structural masterstroke and the source of its sense of wholeness and memory.

  4. **The clarinet as a singing voice (throughout).** Listen for how the clarinet is used not as a virtuoso showpiece but as a vocal, singing voice, blending with and rising out of the strings, the song-instinct given to a wind instrument, inspired by Mühlfeld's playing.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the other clarinet works): the Clarinet Trio Op. 114 (clarinet, cello, piano) and the two Clarinet Sonatas Op. 120, the rest of the Mühlfeld-inspired output. Hearing them alongside the quintet completes the picture of Brahms's late clarinet flowering and shows the range, from the trio's intimacy to the sonatas' duo dialogue.

**Listen** (the late piano pieces ahead): the Op. 118 No. 2 Intermezzo (Chapter 17), to hear the same autumnal late style in the solo-piano world. The clarinet quintet and the late intermezzos share a single reconciled, inward, song-filled spirit; hearing them together defines the late Brahms.

**Read** : Michael Musgrave's _The Music of Brahms_ (1985) and Karl Geiringer's _Brahms: His Life and Work_ on the late clarinet works and the Mühlfeld collaboration, for the story and the music.

**Read** (the late style): any good account of Brahms's late period (in Swafford or MacDonald) on the autumnal, reconciled character of the final works, the retirement reversed by Mühlfeld, and the Indian-summer flowering of the clarinet music, the human and creative context of this Indian summer.

## Chapter 16: The Lieder, the ~200-Song Catalogue, and the Choral Works

We began this book with a song, the Wiegenlied, and we have heard, again and again, how the song-instinct runs through all of Brahms's music: the breathing melodies of the symphonies, the cello-song at the heart of the B-flat Concerto, the rain songs woven into the violin sonata, the clarinet treated as a singing voice. Now we turn directly to Brahms the song-composer, who wrote roughly two hundred Lieder (German art-songs) and some sixty choral works across his life, and who, like Schubert before him, placed the song at the center of his art. This chapter takes up that vast vocal catalogue, and it anchors on the work that closes it: the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , "Four Serious Songs," Op. 121, composed in 1896 in the shadow of Clara Schumann's death, Brahms's last great work and one of the most profound meditations on death in all of song.

We will focus on the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , with its Biblical texts and its unflinching gaze at mortality and love, and look more briefly at the _Alto Rhapsody_ , Op. 53, a haunting work for alto, men's chorus, and orchestra, and at a few of the characteristic Lieder. The chapter completes the portrait of the late Brahms (Chapters 14-15) and closes the song-thread that opened the book, returning us, at the end of Brahms's life, to the genre we started with at its beginning. The Wiegenlied was a blessing sung over a sleeping child; the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is a reckoning with death sung by a dying man. Between them lies a whole life in song.

### 16.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

In the spring of 1896, Clara Schumann, the woman who had been at the center of Brahms's emotional life for over forty years (Chapter 4), suffered a stroke and lay dying. Brahms, sixty-two and himself increasingly ill, was distraught. In May, before her death, he composed the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , four songs on Biblical texts about death, the vanity of earthly things, and finally love. He set words from Ecclesiastes ("for that which befalleth the sons of men befalleth beasts... all go unto one place"), from Ecclesiasticus, and finally from St. Paul's great hymn to love in 1 Corinthians ("though I speak with the tongues of men and of angels, and have not charity... and now abideth faith, hope, charity, these three; but the greatest of these is charity"). The four songs move from the bleakest contemplation of death's universality to a final affirmation of love as the thing that endures.

Clara Schumann died on May 20, 1896. Brahms, traveling to her funeral, missed a train connection and endured an exhausting journey; some accounts trace the rapid decline of his own health to this period. He himself would die less than a year later, on April 3, 1897. The _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , written in those final months in her shadow, are thus a double farewell: to Clara, and, as it turned out, to his own life and art. They are his last completed major work (a set of organ chorale preludes, Chapter 19, followed, but the songs were his last great public statement), and they have the weight of a final reckoning. That the lifelong reticent Brahms, who could rarely speak his deepest feelings, poured into these four songs his grief at Clara's death and his own facing of mortality, choosing texts about death and love, gives them an almost unbearable poignancy. They are the dark counterpart to the German Requiem (Chapter 6): where the Requiem consoled all mourners on a public, monumental scale, the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is the private, intimate, personal reckoning of one dying man with death and love.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The unflinching gaze at death.** Listen, across the four songs, for their honesty about mortality. The first two songs face death's universality and bleakness without flinching ("all go unto one place; all are of the dust, and all turn to dust again"); the third turns toward death as a release for the suffering; and the fourth turns, at last, to love. The cycle does not offer easy comfort; it looks death full in the face, and finds its consolation only in the final affirmation of love. This unflinching honesty is the work's greatness.

  2. **The turn to love in the fourth song.** Listen especially for the final song, the setting of St. Paul's hymn to love ("though I speak with the tongues of men and of angels..."). After three songs facing death, the music turns, in the fourth, toward warmth and affirmation, the baritone singing of faith, hope, and love, "but the greatest of these is love (charity)." Listen for how the music opens out into tenderness here; this turn to love as the thing that outlasts death is the cycle's resolution and its deepest statement.

  3. **The depth and gravity of the writing.** Listen for the seriousness of the musical setting: the dark, weighty piano writing, the grave, declamatory vocal line (these are songs for a low voice, baritone or bass), the absence of any prettiness or charm. This is Brahms at his most stripped-down and profound, the lyrical gift of the Wiegenlied now turned to the gravest of subjects. (The songs are usually sung by a baritone or bass; an orchestral version also exists.)

#### One-sentence background

This chapter anchors on the **Vier ernste Gesänge ("Four Serious Songs"), Op. 121 (1896)** , a cycle of four songs for low voice and piano on Biblical texts about death and love, composed in the shadow of Clara Schumann's final illness and death and standing as Brahms's last great work and his personal reckoning with mortality (the private counterpart to the German Requiem); it also considers the **Alto Rhapsody, Op. 53 (1869)** , for alto, men's chorus, and orchestra, on a text by Goethe, and a few characteristic Lieder from Brahms's ~200-song catalogue; the **Primary Recording** for the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is a classic such as Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with a fine pianist (for instance Daniel Barenboim or Jörg Demus, Deutsche Grammophon), chosen for gravity, clarity of text, and depth.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Vier ernste Gesänge Fischer-Dieskau"; "Brahms Four Serious Songs Op. 121"; "Brahms Alto Rhapsody Op. 53" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 四首严肃的歌 费舍尔-迪斯考"; "勃拉姆斯 女低音狂想曲 作品53" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Vier ernste Gesänge: Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau (multiple recordings); Hans Hotter (historic, of great gravity); Thomas Quasthoff; Bryn Terfel; among low voices, each finds a different weight.
* Alto Rhapsody: Kathleen Ferrier (historic, deeply moving); Janet Baker; Christa Ludwig; Marian Anderson.
* For the Lieder generally: recital collections by Fischer-Dieskau, Jessye Norman, Anne Sofie von Otter, Christa Ludwig, and others.

Follow a text-and-translation for the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ ; as with the German Requiem, the words are the key, and Brahms chose every one. The cycle is short (about eighteen minutes); hear it whole, and pay attention to the journey from the bleak first songs to the love-affirming fourth.

### 16.2 The Listening Window

#### Vier ernste Gesänge, Op. 121

Timestamps refer to a standard recording; they will shift with the singer.

**Song 1: "Denn es gehet dem Menschen" (For that which befalleth the sons of men).** _0:00._ The first song sets the bleakest text, from Ecclesiastes: that the same fate befalls men and beasts, that all go to one place, all are dust and return to dust. Listen for the grave, heavy, marching music, the dark piano, the declamatory low voice, and the relentless, almost despairing repetition of "all is vanity." This is death's universality faced without comfort. The music has a tread-like inexorability, akin to the funeral march of the Requiem's second movement.

**Song 2: "Ich wandte mich" (So I returned).** _0:00._ The second song, also from Ecclesiastes, contemplates the tears of the oppressed and concludes that the dead, and the unborn, are better off than the living who suffer. Listen for the dark, troubled music and the bitter consolation of its text, that not to live is better than to suffer. This is the cycle's bleakest point, the gaze into the abyss.

**Song 3: "O Tod, wie bitter bist du" (O death, how bitter you are).** _0:00._ The third song turns, from Ecclesiasticus: "O death, how bitter you are" to one who lives in comfort, but how welcome you are to the needy, the aged, the suffering. Listen for the two halves: the bitter opening (death as terror to the comfortable) and the gentler, almost consoling turn (death as release to the suffering). The music softens in the second half; death begins to be seen not only as terror but as rest. This is the hinge of the cycle, turning from despair toward acceptance.

**Song 4: "Wenn ich mit Menschen- und mit Engelszungen redete" (Though I speak with the tongues of men and of angels).** _0:00._ The final song sets St. Paul's great hymn to love from 1 Corinthians. Listen for the change in the music: after three songs of death, the fourth opens out into warmth, movement, and affirmation. The baritone sings of love (charity) as greater than tongues, prophecy, knowledge, or faith; "and now abideth faith, hope, love, these three; but the greatest of these is love." Listen for how the music rises to tenderness and warmth on the affirmation of love, the cycle's resolution: after facing death's universality and bitterness, the answer is love, the thing that endures when all else passes. The song, and the cycle, end not in despair but in this affirmation, music of hard-won warmth after the darkness.

The journey to carry: from the bleak, marching contemplation of death's universality (Songs 1-2), through the turn toward death as release (Song 3), to the final affirmation of love as the thing that outlasts death (Song 4). It is Brahms's last and most personal reckoning, and one of the profoundest things in all of song.

#### Alto Rhapsody, Op. 53 (briefly)

_0:00._ The _Alto Rhapsody_ sets a fragment of a Goethe poem about a misanthrope wandering alone and bitter in a winter landscape. It opens with dark, troubled orchestral music and the alto's grave, lonely song of the wanderer's despair. _Later,_ the men's chorus enters, and the music turns toward consolation: a prayer that the wanderer's heart be opened and comforted. Listen for the turn from the alto's lonely desolation to the warm, consoling entry of the chorus, a small arc from isolation to solace that prefigures, on a smaller scale, the consolation-after-darkness of the Requiem and the love-after-death of the _Vier ernste Gesänge_. It is one of Brahms's most beautiful and haunting choral-orchestral works.

### 16.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Brahms the song-composer

This chapter places at the center what has been present throughout the book: Brahms was, at his core, a song-composer. He wrote roughly two hundred Lieder across his life, in the great German tradition of Schubert and Schumann, and the song was as central to him as to them. His Lieder range from folk-song settings (he loved and arranged German folk songs all his life) to the grandest art-songs; from the world-famous Wiegenlied to the profound _Vier ernste Gesänge_ ; from love songs to nature songs to the gravest meditations. And the song-instinct, the gift for a singable, breathing, emotionally direct melody, is not confined to the Lieder; it is the foundation of all his music, the source of the symphonies' great tunes, the chamber works' melodies, the clarinet's vocal lines. To understand Brahms, you must understand that beneath the developing-variation craft and the contrapuntal mastery there is always a song-writer, a melodist whose first instinct is the human voice singing. The Lieder are where that instinct is most directly expressed, and the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is where it reaches its profoundest depth. The book began with a song and ends, in this chapter, with songs, because the song is where Brahms's art begins and ends.

#### Facing death: the Vier ernste Gesänge and the German Requiem

The _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is best understood beside the German Requiem (Chapter 6), as its dark, private counterpart. Both face death; both set Biblical texts Brahms chose himself; both are central to his art. But where the Requiem is public, monumental, and consoling, written for chorus and orchestra to comfort all mourners, the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is private, intimate, and unflinching, written for one voice and piano, facing death's bleakness head-on before finding, only at the very end, the consolation of love. The Requiem is the work of a man in his thirties consoling others; the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is the work of a man in his sixties, dying, facing his own death and the death of the person he loved most. The Requiem's first words are "Blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted"; the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ begins with "all is vanity" and reaches comfort only by climbing through the abyss to "the greatest of these is love." Together they frame Brahms's lifelong engagement with mortality: the young man's public consolation and the old man's private reckoning. Hearing them together is to hear the whole arc of a great artist's confrontation with death, from comforting others to facing his own end.

#### The turn to love

The deepest stroke in the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is the turn to love in the fourth song. After three songs facing death, in its universality, its bitterness, its release, the cycle does not end in death but in love: St. Paul's affirmation that love is greater than all else and endures when all else passes. This turn is the cycle's whole meaning, and it is profoundly moving in its biographical context: Brahms, having faced the death of Clara, the woman he loved for forty years, and his own approaching death, ends his last great work not with the abyss but with love, the thing that, the text says, abides when faith and hope and knowledge are gone. Whether or not we read it as Brahms's personal credo, the gesture is unmistakable: the great reticent man, who could rarely speak his love, ends his life's work with the greatest of all hymns to love, sung over the grave of the woman he loved. It is the most personal statement in all of Brahms, made, characteristically, not in his own words but through chosen scripture and through music. The Wiegenlied began the book with a blessing of love over a cradle; the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ ends Brahms's life with an affirmation of love over a grave. The circle of the song, and of the love, is complete.

### 16.4 The Stories

#### Clara's death and Brahms's last year

The _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is inseparable from the death of Clara Schumann. Brahms composed it in May 1896, while Clara, after a stroke, lay dying; she died on May 20. The forty-year relationship that began in the catastrophe of 1854 (Chapter 4) ended here, and Brahms was devastated. He himself was already ill (the liver cancer that would kill him, Chapter 19), and the loss of Clara, by all accounts, accelerated his decline; he died less than a year later. That his last great work was composed in her shadow, on texts about death and love, makes it his memorial to her and his own preparation for death at once. The reticent Brahms, who had burned his letters to Clara so that posterity could not read his heart (Chapter 4), left, in the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , an indirect but unmistakable record of what her death meant to him and of his own facing of the end. It is the most personal document of his last year, and one of the most moving in all of music: a dying man's farewell to the woman he loved and to life, made in the only language he could fully trust, the language of song.

#### The Lieder and the choral works in brief

Beyond the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , Brahms's vocal catalogue is vast and worth exploring. Among the Lieder, the famous ones include the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2); "Wie Melodien zieht es mir" (a tender song about how melody and feeling elude words); "Von ewiger Liebe" (a dramatic song of eternal love); "Die Mainacht" (a nocturne of longing); "Sapphische Ode"; "Feldeinsamkeit" (a famous song of lying in a summer field, gazing at the sky). The folk-song settings, including the _Deutsche Volkslieder_ , show his lifelong love of folk music. Among the choral works, beyond the German Requiem and the _Alto Rhapsody_ , are the _Schicksalslied_ ("Song of Destiny," a profound setting of Hölderlin), the _Nänie_ (a lament on the death of beauty, after Schiller), and the _Gesang der Parzen_ ("Song of the Fates"). These works, less famous than the symphonies and concertos, are among Brahms's most beautiful, and they show the central place of the voice, solo and choral, in his art. The reader who has come this far is encouraged to explore them; they are the song-composer's heart of Brahms, beyond the famous lullaby and the final songs.

#### Recording history briefly

The _Vier ernste Gesänge_ belongs to the great low voices: Fischer-Dieskau (our primary candidate, in several recordings), Hans Hotter, Thomas Quasthoff, Bryn Terfel, each bringing a different gravity to the cycle's reckoning with death. The _Alto Rhapsody_ has classic accounts by Kathleen Ferrier (deeply moving), Janet Baker, and Christa Ludwig. The Lieder generally are documented in great recital recordings across the twentieth century. Because these are songs, where text and voice are everything, the great recordings are those where the singer makes the words live; comparing two singers' accounts of the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is a lesson in how interpretation shapes meaning in song. The fuller Lieder lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later, and have heard before

This chapter connects across the volume.

To the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2). The book's first anchor and this chapter's anchor frame Brahms's life in song: the lullaby's blessing of love over a cradle and the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ 's affirmation of love over a grave. The song-instinct that opened the book closes it here.

To the German Requiem (Chapter 6). The _Vier ernste Gesänge_ is the private, intimate counterpart to the public, monumental Requiem, both facing death with Brahms-chosen Biblical texts. Together they frame his lifelong confrontation with mortality, from consoling others to facing his own end.

To the Schumann-Clara story (Chapter 4) and the final years (Chapter 19). The _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , composed in the shadow of Clara's death, closes the forty-year relationship that Chapter 4 opened, and belongs to the story of Brahms's final year that Chapter 19 tells. The arc that began in the catastrophe of 1854 ends in these songs.

To the violin sonatas (Chapter 14) and the song-instrumental thread. The songs Brahms wove into the violin sonatas, and the song-instinct that runs through all his instrumental music, find their direct source here, in the Lieder. The song-composer is the foundation of the whole edifice.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Brahms composed the Vier ernste Gesänge in May 1896 while Clara Schumann, whom he had loved for forty years, lay dying, and he himself was mortally ill; the songs move from the bleakest contemplation of death to a final affirmation of love. Listen to the cycle's journey, especially the turn to love in the fourth song. Why might the reticent Brahms, who burned his love letters, have chosen to make his most personal statement through chosen scripture and music rather than his own words? What does it mean to end one's life's work with a hymn to love sung over a grave?"_

* _"The Vier ernste Gesänge faces death honestly, without easy comfort, through three songs, and finds consolation only at the very end, in love as the thing that endures when all else passes. Compare this to the German Requiem, which begins with 'blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted.' Why might the young Brahms consoling others and the old, dying Brahms facing his own death have written such different works about mortality? Which kind of consolation moves you more, the public comfort or the private reckoning?"_

* _"The book began with a song, the Wiegenlied, a blessing of love over a cradle, and ends, in this chapter, with the Vier ernste Gesänge, an affirmation of love over a grave, both built on Brahms's gift for the singing human voice. The chapter argues that beneath all of Brahms's craft there is always a song-composer, a melodist whose first instinct is the voice. As you look back over the whole volume, where did you hear the song-instinct most surprisingly, in a symphony, a concerto, a chamber work? How does hearing Brahms as fundamentally a song-composer change your sense of his music?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a recording of the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ from §16.1, with a text-and-translation.

  1. **The bleak opening songs (Songs 1-2).** Listen to the grave, marching music of the first two songs and their unflinching texts about death's universality ("all go unto one place; all are of the dust"). This is death faced without comfort, the cycle's descent into the abyss.

  2. **The hinge (Song 3).** Listen to "O death, how bitter you are," and to how the music softens in its second half as death is seen not only as terror but as release for the suffering. This is the turn from despair toward acceptance.

  3. **The turn to love (Song 4).** Listen to the final setting of St. Paul's hymn to love, and to how the music opens into warmth and affirmation: "the greatest of these is love." This turn from death to love is the cycle's resolution and Brahms's most personal statement.

  4. **The Alto Rhapsody's turn to consolation.** Listen for the moment the men's chorus enters in the _Alto Rhapsody_ , turning the alto's lonely desolation toward warmth and solace, a small arc from isolation to comfort that echoes the larger consolations of Brahms's vocal art.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the Lieder): a recital selection of Brahms's songs, "Wie Melodien zieht es mir," "Von ewiger Liebe," "Die Mainacht," "Feldeinsamkeit", to hear the range of the song-composer beyond the famous lullaby and the final cycle. These songs are the heart of Brahms's vocal art and reward exploration.

**Listen** (the choral works): the _Schicksalslied_ (Song of Destiny) or the _Nänie_ , two of Brahms's most beautiful choral-orchestral works, to hear the choral Brahms beyond the German Requiem and the _Alto Rhapsody_.

**Read** : Michael Musgrave's _The Music of Brahms_ (1985) on the Lieder and choral works, and any good study of the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ in its biographical context, for the songs and their meaning.

**Read** (the biography): Swafford's and MacDonald's accounts of Brahms's last year, Clara's death, and the composition of the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , for the human story behind Brahms's final great work, the reckoning with death and love that closed his life in song.

## Chapter 17: The Late Piano Pieces, Opp. 116-119 (1892-1893)

In the same Indian-summer years that produced the clarinet works (Chapter 15), Brahms returned to the instrument of his youth, the piano, and wrote his last and most inward keyboard music: four sets of short pieces, Opp. 116, 117, 118, and 119, composed in 1892-1893, twenty of them in all. These are not the grand symphonic sonatas of his youth (he had written his last piano sonata at twenty, Chapter 3); they are miniatures, intermezzos and capriccios and ballades and rhapsodies, each a self-contained character piece, and together they form one of the supreme bodies of late piano music in the repertoire, the keyboard counterpart to the autumnal clarinet works. Brahms called the three pieces of Op. 117 "lullabies of my sorrows," and the phrase fits the whole group: these are intimate, reflective, often melancholy pieces, songs without words, the late style at its most personal and inward.

This is an anchor-heavy chapter, because the late piano pieces reward the closest listening, and it centers on the most beloved of them all: the Intermezzo in A major, Op. 118 No. 2, one of the most cherished pieces Brahms ever wrote, a tender, song-like meditation that has become, for many, the emblem of the late Brahms. We will also look at the Op. 117 No. 1 in E-flat (the first of the "lullabies of my sorrows") and the Op. 119 No. 1 in B minor (a piece of extraordinary harmonic richness). The chapter completes the late-style portrait (Chapters 14-15) and shows the developing-variation principle and the inner-voice richness, the central Brahms listening skills, operating in the most intimate possible form. These pieces are where the Wiegenlied, the lullaby that opened the book, grows old.

### 17.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

When Brahms sent the three Intermezzos of Op. 117 to his friends, he described them, in a phrase that has stuck to them ever since, as "lullabies of my sorrows", _Wiegenlieder meiner Schmerzen_. Over the first of them, the Intermezzo in E-flat, he placed an epigraph, lines from an old Scottish lullaby (in German translation) that he found in a collection: "Sleep softly, my child, sleep softly and well; it grieves me so to see you weep." The piece is, literally, a lullaby, a rocking, gentle, song-like meditation, but a lullaby sung over sorrow, a cradle-song for grief rather than for a child.

The phrase "lullabies of my sorrows" opens the whole late-piano world. These pieces are intimate, private, reflective, often melancholy; they are not public statements but inward confidences, the musings of an aging man alone at the piano in the evening of his life. The image of the lullaby is exactly right, and it connects directly to where this book began: the Wiegenlied of 1868 (Chapter 2) was a lullaby sung over a sleeping child, warm and tender with one bittersweet shadow; the "lullabies of my sorrows" of 1892 are lullabies sung over the composer's own griefs, the same gentle, rocking, song-like manner, but darkened and deepened by age and loss. To hear the late piano pieces is to hear the Wiegenlied grown old, the lullaby turned inward, the cradle-song become a meditation on sorrow. They are among the most personal and most beloved music Brahms wrote, and the perfect close to the portrait of the late, inward, reconciled master.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The inward, song-like intimacy.** Listen, across these pieces, for their intimacy and inwardness: these are quiet, private, reflective miniatures, songs without words, meant for solitary listening rather than the concert platform. The Op. 118 No. 2 Intermezzo, in particular, is a tender, flowing, song-like melody of great beauty. Let the intimacy work on you; this is the most personal music in the volume.

  2. **The melody hidden in the inner voices.** Listen for the characteristic late-Brahms texture: the main melody is often not on top but woven into the middle of the texture, sung by an inner voice with notes above and below it, so that you must listen _into_ the music to find the tune. This inner-voice richness, the central Brahms listening skill introduced with the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2), is at its most refined here. Listen for melodies emerging from the middle of the sound.

  3. **The harmonic richness and the autumnal shadow.** Listen for the harmonic depth and the prevailing autumnal, melancholy color. The late pieces are harmonically rich and subtle, full of the major-minor shimmer and the warm, shadowed feeling of the late style. The Op. 119 No. 1 in B minor, in particular, opens with a famous cascade of rich, falling chords of extraordinary harmonic beauty. Listen for the depth of color and the tender melancholy.

#### One-sentence background

The late piano pieces, Opp. 116-119 (1892-1893), are four sets of short character pieces for solo piano (intermezzos, capriccios, ballades, a rhapsody, twenty pieces in all), Brahms's last keyboard music and the keyboard counterpart to the late clarinet works, intimate and inward in the autumnal late style, including the three Op. 117 Intermezzos Brahms called "lullabies of my sorrows"; this chapter anchors on the **Intermezzo in A major, Op. 118 No. 2** , the most beloved of all, and considers the **Op. 117 No. 1 in E-flat** and the **Op. 119 No. 1 in B minor** ; the **Primary Recordings** are classic accounts such as Radu Lupu (Decca), Wilhelm Kempff, or Stephen Kovacevich (Philips), chosen for inwardness, beauty of tone, and clarity of the inner voices.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Brahms Intermezzo Op. 118 No. 2 Lupu"; "Brahms late piano pieces Op. 116 117 118 119" ZH: "勃拉姆斯 间奏曲 作品118之2 卢普"; "勃拉姆斯 晚期钢琴小品 作品116-119" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Radu Lupu (Decca), prized for poetry and tone in the late pieces.
* Wilhelm Kempff (Deutsche Grammophon), classic, songful, inward.
* Stephen Kovacevich (Philips), clear and deeply felt.
* Hélène Grimaud (Deutsche Grammophon), who has recorded the Op. 118 set; Radu Lupu and Glenn Gould (idiosyncratic but illuminating on the inner voices) among others.

These are miniatures; you can hear several in a sitting, but they reward slow, attentive, repeated listening, especially the Op. 118 No. 2, which deepens with familiarity. Start with that piece, then explore the others. The intimacy of this music asks for quiet and attention rather than background listening.

### 17.2 The Listening Window

#### Intermezzo in A major, Op. 118 No. 2

Timestamps refer to a standard recording (the piece runs about five to six minutes); they will shift with the pianist.

**0:00. The opening melody.** The intermezzo opens with one of Brahms's most beloved melodies: a tender, flowing, song-like tune in A major, rising and falling in gentle arcs, deeply warm and a little melancholy. Listen to the melody's breathing, song-like quality, and to the rich, cushioned accompaniment beneath and around it. This is the late Brahms at his most lyrical and intimate.

**About 1:30. The development and the inner voices.** As the piece unfolds, listen for how the melody is varied and developed, and for how the texture thickens, the tune sometimes moving into inner voices, counter-melodies emerging, the harmony deepening. Listen _into_ the texture for the melodic lines woven through the middle. This is developing variation and inner-voice richness in the most intimate form.

**About 2:30. The contrasting middle section.** A central section in a related minor key, more shadowed and searching, with a chorale-like, hymn-like passage of great tenderness. Listen for the deepening of feeling and the harmonic richness, the autumnal shadow at the heart of the piece.

**About 4:00. The return.** The opening melody returns, varied and even more tender, and the piece closes in warm, peaceful A major, the song-like tune settling into repose. Listen for the sense of a song sung and completed, intimate and consoling. The whole piece is a perfect miniature: a song without words, tender and shadowed, the late Brahms in five minutes.

#### Op. 117 No. 1 in E-flat (the first "lullaby of my sorrows")

**0:00.** The intermezzo opens with a gentle, rocking, lullaby-like melody (the one Brahms set under the old Scottish cradle-song epigraph), the tune sung in an inner voice, cradled by notes above and below, a literal lullaby texture. Listen for the rocking gentleness and for the melody hidden in the middle of the sound, the Wiegenlied texture (Chapter 2) made even more inward. _The middle section_ darkens, the lullaby shadowed by sorrow, before the gentle rocking returns. Listen for the prevailing tenderness shadowed by grief; this is the "lullaby of my sorrows" in its purest form.

#### Op. 119 No. 1 in B minor

**0:00.** The intermezzo opens with a famous, extraordinary passage: a slow cascade of rich, overlapping, falling chords, each note allowed to ring into the next, creating a shimmer of harmonic beauty of remarkable depth and strangeness, music that seems to hang suspended, falling endlessly. Brahms described it as melancholy to the point of bliss. Listen to this cascade of falling chords and its harmonic richness; it is one of the most beautiful and harmonically adventurous openings in all of Brahms, the late harmonic imagination at its most refined. The piece unfolds in tender melancholy from this opening.

### 17.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The character piece and the late inwardness

The late piano pieces are _character pieces_ : short, self-contained piano works, each capturing a single mood or character, in the tradition of Schubert's Impromptus, Schumann's piano miniatures, and Chopin's nocturnes and preludes. Brahms, who had written his grand symphonic sonatas in his youth (Chapter 3), turned in old age to this intimate miniature form, and made of it some of his most personal music. The pieces carry names, intermezzo (the most common, meaning simply a lyrical interlude-like piece), capriccio (more energetic and capricious), ballade (narrative, dramatic), rhapsody (free, grand), but the prevailing type is the intermezzo, the quiet, inward, song-like meditation. This turn from the public sonata to the private intermezzo mirrors the whole trajectory of the late style: from public drama (the symphonies) to private meditation (the clarinet works, the late piano pieces). These are not pieces for the grand concert platform but for the intimate room, the solitary evening, the inward hour. They are Brahms's most personal keyboard utterances, and the form, the brief, concentrated character piece, lets him distill a single mood to its essence, a song without words, a lullaby of his sorrows.

#### The melody in the inner voices: the central skill, refined

The late piano pieces are the ideal place to practice, in its most refined form, the central Brahms listening skill introduced way back with the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2): listening to the inside of the texture, not just the top. Brahms's late piano writing is famous for hiding the melody in the inner voices: the tune is often not the highest note but a line woven through the middle of the texture, with notes above and below it, so that the listener must listen _into_ the sound to find the melody. The Op. 117 No. 1 lullaby is the clearest case, the cradle-song melody sung in an inner voice, cradled above and below, but the technique runs through all the late pieces. This inner-voice richness is the essence of Brahms's keyboard texture and one of the things that makes his late piano music so deep and so rewarding: there is always more inside the sound than the surface reveals, and repeated listening keeps uncovering new melodic lines woven into the middle. Listen _into_ these pieces, and they open like flowers. This is the culmination of the inner-voice listening that the whole book has been teaching, the skill at its most refined and most rewarding.

#### The Wiegenlied grown old

The deepest way to hear the late piano pieces is as the Wiegenlied grown old. The book began (Chapter 2) with the Wiegenlied, a lullaby sung over a sleeping child: a gentle, rocking, song-like melody, warm and tender, with one bittersweet harmonic shadow. The late piano pieces, and especially the "lullabies of my sorrows" of Op. 117, are the same thing, transformed by twenty-five years of life and loss: gentle, rocking, song-like melodies, warm and tender, but now shadowed throughout by age, sorrow, and the nearness of death. The lullaby that once consoled a child now consoles the aging composer himself; the one bittersweet chord of the Wiegenlied has spread to color whole pieces. This is developing variation at the scale of a life: the same essential idea, the tender, shadowed, song-like lullaby, transformed across twenty-five years into something deeper and darker. To hear the Op. 117 No. 1 after the Wiegenlied is to hear the whole arc of Brahms's inner life, from the warm lullaby of his maturity to the melancholy lullabies of his age, and to understand that the late style is not a new thing but the old tenderness, deepened by everything that has happened. The book's first anchor and this chapter's anchors are the same music, a generation apart.

### 17.4 The Stories

#### The late piano flowering

The late piano pieces belong to the same Indian-summer years as the clarinet works (Chapter 15), the early 1890s, when Brahms, having thought himself finished, produced a last flowering of intimate masterpieces. He composed the four sets, Opp. 116-119, in 1892-1893, and they were among the last things he wrote (only the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , Chapter 16, and the organ chorale preludes, Chapter 19, came after). Clara Schumann, the supreme pianist and Brahms's lifelong confidante, was among the first to play and treasure them; Brahms sent her the pieces, and she found in them, as in the violin sonata's rain songs (Chapter 14), the indirect speech of his heart. These pieces are the private diary of the late Brahms: not the public statements of the symphonies but the inward musings of an aging man at the piano, distilling his sorrows and his tenderness into brief, perfect miniatures. They have become, especially the Op. 118 No. 2 Intermezzo, among his most beloved music, precisely for their intimacy and their depth, the late master speaking, quietly, of the things closest to the heart.

#### Recording history briefly

The late piano pieces are central repertoire for the reflective pianist, and the great recordings, Lupu, Kempff, Kovacevich, and many others, are prized for inwardness, tone, and clarity of the inner voices. Because the music is so intimate and so dependent on voicing (bringing out the hidden inner melodies), comparing two pianists' accounts of the Op. 118 No. 2, how they shape the song, how they voice the inner lines, how they pace the silences, is a fine lesson in interpretation. These are pieces where less is more, where restraint and intimacy matter more than display, and the great accounts are those that trust the music's quiet depth. The fuller Brahms-piano lineage is gathered in Chapter 22.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later, and have heard before

This chapter connects across the volume.

To the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2). The late piano pieces, especially the "lullabies of my sorrows," are the Wiegenlied grown old: the same gentle, rocking, song-like, bittersweet lullaby manner, deepened by twenty-five years of life and loss. Hearing the Op. 117 No. 1 after the Wiegenlied reveals the whole arc of Brahms's inner life.

To the clarinet works (Chapter 15). The late piano pieces and the clarinet works are the twin peaks of Brahms's late flowering, both from the early 1890s, both intimate, inward, and autumnal. Together they define the late style.

To the synthesis (Chapter 20). The inner-voice richness and the developing variation of the late pieces are the central Brahms techniques in their most refined form, and Chapter 20 returns to them as key instances of his method.

To the final years (Chapter 19). The late piano pieces belong to Brahms's last creative period, and Chapter 19 places them in the story of his final years, alongside the clarinet works, the _Vier ernste Gesänge_ , and the closing chorale preludes.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Brahms called the three Intermezzos of Op. 117 'lullabies of my sorrows,' and placed over the first an old cradle-song epigraph, turning the lullaby from a song for a child into a song for his own griefs. The book argues that the late piano pieces are 'the Wiegenlied grown old.' Listen to the Op. 117 No. 1 after recalling the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2). What has changed, and what has stayed the same, in the twenty-five years between them? What does it mean to sing a lullaby to one's own sorrows?"_

* _"Brahms's late piano writing famously hides the melody in the inner voices, so that the tune is often not the highest note but a line woven through the middle of the texture, asking the listener to listen _into_ the sound. Try this with the Op. 118 No. 2 Intermezzo: listen past the top of the texture for melodies emerging from the middle. What is the effect of a melody half-hidden rather than on the surface? Why might an aging, inward composer write music that rewards listening into rather than just listening to?"_

* _"The late piano pieces are intimate miniatures, songs without words, meant for the solitary evening rather than the grand concert platform, the opposite of the public symphonies. Listen to the Op. 118 No. 2 and consider why a composer who could command the largest forms turned, at the end, to the smallest and most private. Is there something a five-minute intimate miniature can say that a forty-five-minute symphony cannot? What does the turn from the public to the private tell you about the late Brahms?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find a recording from §17.1.

  1. **The opening melody of the Op. 118 No. 2 Intermezzo (0:00).** Fix in your ear the tender, flowing, song-like A-major melody and the rich, cushioned accompaniment around it. This is the late Brahms at his most lyrical and beloved.

  2. **The melody in the inner voices (Op. 117 No. 1).** Listen for the lullaby melody sung in an inner voice, cradled by notes above and below, the literal lullaby texture, the Wiegenlied made even more inward. Practice listening _into_ the sound for the hidden tune.

  3. **The cascade of falling chords (Op. 119 No. 1, 0:00).** Listen to the famous opening of the B-minor Intermezzo, the slow cascade of rich, overlapping, falling chords, the late harmonic imagination at its most refined and beautiful.

  4. **The autumnal shadow (across all the pieces).** Listen for the prevailing late-style color, the major-minor shimmer, the tender melancholy, the warm shadow, that unites these miniatures and connects them to the bittersweet chord of the Wiegenlied, now grown to color whole pieces.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (the Wiegenlied again): return to the Wiegenlied (Chapter 2) right after the Op. 117 No. 1, and hear the lullaby of 1868 and the "lullaby of my sorrows" of 1892 as the same music a generation apart. This pairing is the clearest way to feel the late style as the old tenderness deepened by life.

**Listen** (the character-piece tradition): a Chopin nocturne or a Schubert impromptu, the great precedents for the intimate piano character piece. Hearing them shows the tradition Brahms's late pieces join and transform, and how distinctively Brahmsian his inner-voice textures are within it.

**Read** : Michael Musgrave's _The Music of Brahms_ (1985) and Karl Geiringer's _Brahms: His Life and Work_ on the late piano pieces, for the music and the "lullabies of my sorrows" context.

**Read** (the late style): any good account of Brahms's late period (Swafford, MacDonald) on the intimate piano flowering of the early 1890s, alongside the clarinet works, for the human and creative context of these miniatures, the inward evening of a great composer's life.
